# 3D Gaussian Splatting: A Comprehensive Survey of Representations, Optimization, Rendering, and Applications

## 1 Introduction and Foundational Concepts

### 1.1 Evolution of 3D Scene Representations

The evolution of 3D scene representation has been driven by a persistent pursuit of two often-competing goals: photorealistic fidelity and real-time computational efficiency. The earliest attempts at representing 3D scenes for novel view synthesis relied on point-based rendering, where scenes were depicted as collections of discrete, unconnected 3D points. While these point clouds could be rendered quickly by projecting points onto an image plane and applying splatting—a technique where each point is expanded into a small footprint, such as a circle or ellipse, to fill gaps—they suffered from significant limitations. Point-based methods struggled to accurately capture complex surface properties like specular highlights, transparency, and fine-grained texture, and they lacked a compact, continuous representation of geometry. The visual output was often sparse and noisy, with noticeable holes and aliasing artifacts, particularly when viewing scenes at close range or from novel angles. This sparsity and lack of a coherent surface model made high-fidelity image synthesis highly challenging, and despite decades of research, traditional point-based rendering remained a niche technique, largely supplanted by mesh-based graphics pipelines for most applications.

A paradigm shift occurred with the introduction of Neural Radiance Fields (NeRF). This approach represented a fundamental departure from prior methods by using implicit, coordinate-based neural networks to encode a scene. Instead of storing discrete primitives, NeRF represented a scene as a continuous volumetric function where a multi-layer perceptron (MLP) maps a 5D input—a spatial coordinate and a viewing direction—to an emitted color and volume density. Rendering an image requires ray marching through the volume, sampling points along each camera ray, and accumulating the color and density along these samples to produce the final pixel value. This volumetric rendering approach proved revolutionary: it enabled the synthesis of novel views with an unprecedented level of photorealism, accurately capturing complex occlusions, reflections, and view-dependent effects. This success established NeRF-based implicit representations as the de facto standard for high-quality 3D scene reconstruction [1; 2]. However, the power of this implicit representation came at a steep computational price. The neural network encoding meant that every scene had to be optimized from scratch, leading to slow training times. More critically, rendering required evaluating the MLP hundreds of times per pixel along a ray, making the process extremely slow and preventing real-time or interactive applications. The implicit and continuous nature of the representation also made it difficult to edit or manipulate the reconstructed scene. Unlike an explicit point cloud or mesh, there were no discrete primitives to grab, move, or deform, hindering its use in content creation, VR/AR, and robotics where interactivity is paramount [3].

The field was therefore at an impasse: implicit representations like NeRF offered unmatched quality but were computationally intractable for real-time use, while explicit representations like point clouds were fast but lacked the expressiveness and fidelity needed for photorealistic rendering [4]. The introduction of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) was a watershed moment that elegantly resolved this tension and sparked a rapid transformation of the landscape of 3D scene representations [5; 6]. 3DGS pioneered a novel, explicit representation that leverages millions of learnable 3D Gaussian primitives. Each primitive is defined by a full 3D covariance matrix (encoding its anisotropic scale and rotation), a position, an opacity value, and a set of spherical harmonic coefficients for view-dependent color. This explicit parameterization offers a key advantage over the implicit neural network of NeRF: the scene is directly defined by its primitives, making it inherently editable and suitable for manipulation [1].

The magic of 3DGS lies not just in its explicit representation but in its highly efficient, differentiable rasterization pipeline, which was a game-changer for real-time performance. This rendering mechanism projects these 3D Gaussians onto the 2D image plane, sorts them by depth, and efficiently composites them using alpha-blending, a process that is both extremely fast on modern GPUs and fully differentiable [2]. This hybrid approach combines the strengths of volumetric rendering’s image formation model with the computational efficiency of primitive-based rasterization, achieving a synergistic balance that was previously thought impossible. This departure from the ray-marching paradigm of NeRF toward a rasterization-based, point-centric approach allowed 3DGS to deliver real-time, high-quality novel view synthesis, while also facilitating faster optimization times and the explicit editability that was missing from implicit models. It effectively combined the high visual fidelity associated with NeRF with the interactive speeds and control of point-based graphics [1; 7]. The introduction of 3DGS thus marked a pivotal shift from the deep, expensive implicit representations back towards the explicit, point-based philosophy, but now armed with the powerful, differentiable tools and optimization strategies developed during the era of neural rendering [5]. This convergence created a new, powerful, and versatile framework that has since spurred a vast and rapidly expanding body of research dedicated to enhancing, applying, and extending Gaussian-based representations [8].

The foundational departure of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) from preceding neural rendering techniques lies in its explicit, parametric representation of a scene as a collection of 3D Gaussian primitives. This paradigm shift from continuous implicit functions to a discrete set of optimizable primitives is the bedrock of its real-time rendering capability and inherent editability. A rigorous understanding of this representation requires a detailed examination of the mathematical formulation of these primitives, their parameterization, and the mechanisms through which they encode both geometry and appearance.

At its core, a single 3D Gaussian primitive is defined as a probability distribution in 3D space, mathematically expressed as a Gaussian function. The fundamental parameters for each primitive include a positional mean, which essentially acts as the center of the primitive in 3D space, and a covariance matrix, which defines the spatial extent, orientation, and shape of the primitive. The covariance matrix is the key to the representation's flexibility; it is not restricted to be isotropic, allowing the Gaussians to stretch and rotate to conform to the underlying geometry of the scene. This anisotropic nature is a crucial advantage, as it allows for the efficient representation of surfaces with a minimal number of primitives. Anisotropic ellipsoids can be flattened to represent near-planar surfaces or elongated to cover thin, edge-like structures with far greater fidelity than isotropic spheres, which would require significant over-proliferation to achieve similar coverage.

The covariance matrix is typically decomposed into a rotation matrix and a scaling matrix to ensure it remains positive semi-definite during optimization. This decomposition effectively parameterizes the primitives with a quaternion for rotation and a 3D vector for scale, making the optimization process more stable and interpretable. The shape of the resulting Gaussian ellipsoid has direct implications for geometry reconstruction. As identified in several analyses, an unbounded optimization can lead to Gaussians converging into highly anisotropic, "needle-like" shapes with an effective rank of one, which causes artifacts in the reconstructed geometry and normals [9]. This observation has motivated the introduction of regularization techniques that constrain the shape of the Gaussians to be more planar or spherical, improving surface alignment and reducing artifacts [9]. Similarly, geometric losses based on eigenvalue-derived 3D shape features, such as "planarity" or "eigenentropy" of Gaussian neighborhoods, have been proposed to guide the optimization toward more geometrically accurate and artifact-reduced reconstructions [10; 11].

Accompanying the geometry parameters are two critical attributes for appearance and visibility. Each Gaussian possesses an opacity value, which controls its contribution to the final rendered image. This value is used in alpha-blending to composite the colors from different primitives along a view ray. The color of each Gaussian is not a single static value but is modeled as a function of viewing direction to capture view-dependent effects like specular highlights. This is achieved through the use of spherical harmonic (SH) coefficients, a compact basis for representing functions on a sphere. While low-order SH is efficient, it has limitations. It struggles to represent high-frequency, anisotropic components of the appearance, which is particularly problematic for scenes with complex lighting or specular surfaces [12; 13]. Furthermore, the SH representation is fundamentally asymmetric in its relationship to the geometric rotation of the Gaussian, which complicates the creation of equivariant processing architectures [14]. This has led to numerous extensions that replace or augment SH with alternative appearance models, such as Spherical Gaussians for better specularity and efficiency [12; 15] or general feature vectors that are decoded by a neural network [16].

The standard 3DGS representation assumes that a Gaussian has a uniform color and opacity distribution within its ellipsoidal bound, scaled by the Gaussian's falloff. This limits the expressivity of a single primitive, as its color is effectively a solid color modulated by the Gaussian's radial decay [17]. To overcome this limitation, a significant body of work has focused on increasing the expressive power of individual primitives by decoupling their visual appearance from their geometric extent. One approach is to augment each Gaussian with texture maps, allowing for spatially varying color and opacity across the primitive's surface [17; 18]. This "textured Gaussian" concept can significantly improve the representation of fine details and complex textures while maintaining a compact set of primitives, but it simultaneously introduces significant memory and parameterization challenges [19; 20].

A significant and conceptually important variation of the Gaussian primitive is the 2D Gaussian surfel. By setting the z-scale of a 3D Gaussian to zero, the primitive is flattened into a 2D ellipse, which aligns it with the concept of a surface element or "surfel" [21]. This distinction is crucial for surface-oriented modeling, as 3D Gaussian volumes are inherently volumetric and have an opaque, blob-like nature, making accurate surface extraction and alignment difficult [22]. In contrast, 2D surfels have a clear normal direction (aligned with the local z-axis) and are naturally better suited for tasks requiring geometric precision, such as surface reconstruction and mesh extraction [21; 23]. Gaussian surfels also serve as a base representation for hybrid models that integrate them with 3D volumetric Gaussians to represent both coarse-scale surfaces and fine-scale volumetric details [24]. The explicit and structured nature of the Gaussian primitives is the ultimate source of the representation's strength. Unlike the latent, high-dimensional parameters of a NeRF's neural network, the properties of a Gaussian—its position, shape, orientation, opacity, and color—are directly interpretable. This makes the representation easily editable, as an artist or an algorithm can directly manipulate the object. This explicit parameterization is also what enables the use of uncertainty estimation in a more physically grounded way, by propagating the covariance of the parameters through the rendering Jacobian [25]. Ultimately, the mathematical formulation of these primitives is not just a rendering detail but the core enabling factor for the versatility, efficiency, and scalability that define the 3DGS paradigm across its many applications.

### 1.2 Mathematical Foundations of 3D Gaussian Primitives

This paradigm shift from continuous implicit functions to a discrete set of optimizable primitives is the bedrock of its real-time rendering capability and inherent editability. The explicit and structured nature of the Gaussian primitives is the ultimate source of the representation's strength. Unlike the latent, high-dimensional parameters of a NeRF's neural network, the properties of a Gaussian—its position, shape, orientation, opacity, and color—are directly interpretable. This makes the representation easily editable, as an artist or an algorithm can directly manipulate the object. This explicit parameterization is also what enables the use of uncertainty estimation in a more physically grounded way, by propagating the covariance of the parameters through the rendering Jacobian [25]. Importantly, it is this precise interplay between the geometric attributes (mean, covariance, and shape) and the appearance attributes (opacity and view-dependent color) that forms a direct bridge to the rendering process. The decision to represent a scene as a collection of anisotropic, optimizable 3D Gaussian primitives is not an arbitrary choice; rather, it is deliberately designed to facilitate a subsequent rasterization-based pipeline that projects these ellipsoids onto the image plane and composites them efficiently. The forward mapping from the continuous volumetric parameterization to discrete, overlapping 2D footprints is precisely what enables the high-speed tile-based rendering and alpha-blending that will be examined in the following subsection, while the explicit nature of the primitives ensures that every stage of this rendering process remains differentiable and amenable to direct gradient-based optimization. Ultimately, the mathematical formulation of these primitives is not just a rendering detail but the core enabling factor for the versatility, efficiency, and scalability that define the 3DGS paradigm across its many applications.

### 1.3 Differentiable Rasterization and Alpha-Blending Pipeline

Building upon the explicit and parameterizable primitive representation detailed previously, the rendering pipeline of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) constitutes the crucial operational bridge between this mathematical scene description and the final output image. This pipeline represents a fundamental departure from the ray-marching paradigm of neural radiance fields, replacing per-pixel volumetric integration with a highly efficient, GPU-friendly rasterization process. At its core, the pipeline transforms the continuous volumetric representation of Gaussian primitives into discrete 2D image contributions through a sequence of projection, sorting, and compositing operations. Each 3D Gaussian is first projected onto the 2D image plane, where it becomes a 2D splat whose shape and extent are determined by the viewing transformation and the Gaussian's covariance matrix. The projected splats are then sorted by their depth relative to the camera, and finally, the sorted splats are composited front-to-back using alpha-blending to produce the final pixel color. This process, while conceptually simple, involves several critical design choices that balance computational efficiency with physical accuracy.

The tile-based rasterization strategy is a cornerstone of the 3DGS rendering pipeline's efficiency. Rather than processing each pixel independently, the image is subdivided into a grid of non-overlapping tiles, and for each tile, the set of Gaussians whose projected footprints overlap the tile is identified. This hierarchical culling significantly reduces the number of Gaussians that need to be considered per tile, as only a small fraction of the total scene primitives typically contribute to any given region of the image. The per-tile Gaussian lists are then sorted by depth, and the splats are evaluated in a front-to-back order for each pixel within the tile. This approach not only reduces the computational cost of sorting and blending but also improves memory locality and parallelism, as each tile can be processed independently by separate threads or thread groups. Subsequent optimizations have further refined this pipeline, such as hierarchical tiled rendering that decouples coarse partitioning from fine rasterization to balance the costs of binning and primitive evaluation [26].

The differentiability of the rasterization process is a key enabler of end-to-end optimization. Every operation in the pipeline—from the projection of 3D Gaussians to 2D splats, through the depth-based sorting, to the alpha-blending compositing—is designed to support gradient flow. This allows the Gaussian parameters (position, covariance, opacity, and spherical harmonic coefficients) to be updated via backpropagation based on photometric loss functions comparing rendered images to ground truth. The explicit nature of the representation means that gradients are propagated directly to individual primitives, enabling precise and localized adjustments. However, the requirement for differentiability also imposes constraints on the implementation. For example, the sorting operation must be handled carefully, as it is inherently non-smooth. Recent works have explored differentiable hardware rasterization for 3DGS, achieving faster backward passes by leveraging programmable blending and hybrid gradient reduction strategies to overcome the memory and performance limitations of tile-based software rasterization [27].

The 3DGS rendering pipeline makes several approximations relative to the full volumetric rendering equation. The most significant of these is the treatment of the Gaussian's 3D volumetric density as a 2D splat with a single depth value per pixel. This simplification, while enabling efficient rasterization, leads to artifacts such as popping when the viewpoint changes, as the depth ordering of Gaussians can become inconsistent across frames. To address this issue, hierarchical rasterization approaches have been proposed that systematically resort and cull splats based on per-pixel depth computation, effectively eliminating popping artifacts while maintaining real-time performance [28]. Additionally, the alpha-blending process in standard 3DGS computes alpha and transmittance as scalar quantities per pixel, which can lead to erosion and dilation artifacts at unseen sampling rates. Replacing this with spatially varying alpha and transmittance distributions, as done in Gaussian Blending, enables more accurate composition of background splats at various sampling rates [29].

Despite these approximations, the practical effectiveness of 3DGS is largely attributed to the sheer number of optimized Gaussians. A key insight is that the large, over-parameterized set of primitives can compensate for the errors introduced by the approximate rendering model. In-depth analyses have shown that while more accurate volumetric rendering can indeed improve quality when the number of primitives is low, the power of efficient optimization with thousands of Gaussians allows 3DGS to outperform principled volumetric rendering despite its approximations [7]. This finding suggests that the optimization process effectively adapts the Gaussian distribution to the idiosyncrasies of the rasterization model, embedding the approximation errors into the learned representation.

In parallel with rasterization-based approaches, alternative rendering paradigms have been explored. Ray tracing for 3D Gaussians has been proposed as a means to overcome the limitations of rasterization, such as rigid pinhole camera assumptions and inaccurate handling of reflections and refractions. Early ray tracing approaches sort all intersecting Gaussians along each ray, which is computationally expensive. However, stochastic ray tracing methods that randomly accept and shade a single intersection per ray have been shown to achieve unbiased results with low variance, providing a compelling alternative for both standard and relightable 3DGS scenes [30]. Fast ray tracers like GRay have demonstrated that dense initialization, which slows down rasterization, can actually accelerate ray tracing, leading to performance competitive with splatting-based renderers [31].

The sorting operation in the rasterization pipeline is a significant bottleneck, particularly on resource-constrained platforms. To address this, order-independent transparency (OIT) methods have been developed. One approach uses a dual-hierarchy framework with cell proxies to manage local Gaussians, combined with a physically-inspired weighted sum rendering technique that eliminates popping and transparency artifacts without costly sequential alpha-blending [32]. Similarly, the weighted sum rendering approach approximates alpha blending with weighted sums, removing the need for sorting entirely and simplifying implementation while achieving competitive image quality [33]. Moment-based methods offer another route, characterizing the density distribution along each camera ray with statistical moments to reconstruct a continuous transmittance function, thereby bridging the gap between rasterization and physical accuracy without the need for ray tracing or per-pixel sorting [34].

Furthermore, efforts to improve the physical accuracy of the splatting process itself have gained traction. Volumetrically consistent rasterization directly integrates 3D Gaussians to compute transmittance analytically, producing more physically-accurate alpha values than the standard 3DGS formulation. This approach more closely follows the volume rendering equation while retaining the speed benefits of rasterization, achieving higher accuracy for opaque surfaces with fewer points [35]. The approximations in the standard pipeline have also been analyzed from a theoretical standpoint, leading to improvements such as non-exponential transmittance models that reduce overdraw and accelerate rendering in complex scenes [36]. These developments collectively illustrate an ongoing trajectory toward reconciling the efficiency of rasterization with the fidelity of physically-based rendering, thereby expanding the applicability of 3DGS across diverse domains. This rendering efficiency, in turn, is the foundational enabler of the real-time capabilities, rapid optimization convergence, and practical editability that define the 3DGS paradigm and its downstream applications, as discussed in the following subsection.

### 1.4 Comparative Advantages and Core Properties of 3DGS

The emergence of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) represents a paradigm shift in 3D scene representation, not merely as an incremental improvement but as a fundamental reimagining of the trade-offs that have historically constrained neural rendering. While the preceding discussion detailed the internal mechanics of its rendering pipeline—the projection, sorting, and compositing operations that define its operational efficiency—this subsection shifts focus outward to examine how the distinctive properties of 3DGS—real-time rendering, rapid optimization, inherent editability, compatibility with established graphics pipelines, and a balanced pursuit of visual fidelity and geometric accuracy—collectively position it as a transformative technology bridging neural rendering and traditional computer graphics [5; 2]. Understanding these core advantages is essential, as they directly determine the breadth of downstream applications explored in the following subsection.

**Real-Time Rendering via GPU-Friendly Rasterization.** The most celebrated advantage of 3DGS is its capability for real-time, high-fidelity rendering, a feat largely unattainable by its primary predecessor, the Neural Radiance Field (NeRF), which relies on computationally intensive ray marching. Unlike NeRF's implicit volumetric rendering, 3DGS leverages an explicit, point-based representation that is projected onto the 2D image plane and composited via alpha-blending, a process that maps exceptionally well onto highly optimized GPU rasterization pipelines [37]. This fundamental architectural shift replaces slow, per-pixel ray casting with fast, parallelizable splatting operations. The practical impact is profound: scenes composed of millions of Gaussian primitives can be rendered at interactive frame rates on commodity hardware. This relentless focus on performance confirms that real-time capability is not just a feature but the foundational property that makes 3DGS a practical tool for interactive applications, complementing the tile-based culling and sorting strategies detailed previously. The efficiency of this pipeline has spurred significant research dedicated to further acceleration, underscoring the importance of the real-time capability. For instance, hardware-software co-design approaches enhance existing GPU triangle rasterizers to support 3DGS operations, achieving substantial speedups and energy reductions for edge deployment [38]. Similarly, architectural innovations have leveraged Tensor Cores for the rasterization stage, demonstrating a 1.65× end-to-end performance improvement [39]. System-level optimizations, such as inter-frame caching and tile scheduling, have targeted redundancy in large-scale scenes, yielding up to a 10× speedup over the original algorithm [40]. Even with the added complexity of dynamic scenes, the field is working towards maintaining this real-time performance, with platforms like Visionary aiming to provide web-native, real-time rendering of dynamic Gaussian content [41].

**Rapid Optimization and Convergence.** Just as the rendering pipeline is optimized for real-time performance, the explicit nature of the 3DGS representation confers a second major advantage: significantly faster training times compared to implicit models. NeRF and its variants often require hours or even days of optimization to converge for a single scene. In contrast, 3DGS can achieve high-quality reconstructions in a matter of minutes, a direct consequence of the differentiable rasterization pipeline described earlier that allows for efficient gradient flow to individual primitives. This speedup is attributed to the direct, differentiable rasterization pipeline that allows for efficient gradient flow, bypassing the need for costly volume rendering integrals. The optimization process itself has become a fertile area of research, with efforts focused on refining the densification and pruning strategies to improve both speed and quality [42]. The importance of efficient optimization is highlighted by work like Mini-Splatting2, which introduces a structure-aware distribution and region-prioritized optimization to achieve up to 3× faster convergence while using fewer Gaussians [43]. Furthermore, the initial point cloud quality is critical; methods like GaussianPro have borrowed concepts from multi-view stereo to progressively propagate Gaussians, improving optimization in texture-less regions where SfM initialization is poor [44]. This rapid optimization cycle is not merely a convenience; it enables new workflows such as interactive reconstruction and iterative scene refinement, which are essential for applications in robotics and content creation. The development of specialized hardware further underscores this focus, with prototypes like Vorion being designed to accelerate not only rendering but also the training iterations themselves [45].

**Inherent Editability and Manipulability.** The explicit and discrete nature of 3DGS, where each Gaussian is an independently parameterized primitive as described in the pipeline section, provides an unparalleled level of editability, a stark contrast to the monolithic and opaque nature of implicit neural representations. Each Gaussian primitive is an independent, parameterized entity with explicit properties—position, covariance, opacity, and color—which can be individually manipulated, added, or removed. This property empowers a wide range of downstream tasks, including object-level editing, semantic manipulation, and physical simulation [1; 8]. For instance, the representation's structure facilitates semantic understanding and editing, allowing for text-guided or segment-based manipulations of the scene [8]. This editability is also crucial for scene completion and generation, where training-free plugins can generate and register new Gaussian primitives to fill in missing information from sparse inputs [46]. The ability to treat Gaussians as discrete building blocks makes 3DGS a powerful medium for creative and interactive applications, from virtual content creation to advanced scene understanding and reasoning.

**Compatibility with Mesh-Based Graphics Pipelines.** While 3DGS is an explicit primitive, its compatibility with traditional mesh-based rendering pipelines has been a subject of active research and development. The standard graphics pipeline used in game engines and VR headset ecosystems is deeply entrenched in triangle meshes. To bridge this gap between the splatting-based approach detailed previously and conventional graphics hardware, several approaches have been explored. One strategy involves hybrid representations that integrate 3D Gaussians with textured meshes, using meshes for texture-rich flat areas and Gaussians for intricate geometries, thereby improving rendering efficiency and compatibility [47]. Another ambitious direction is the development of differentiable renderers that directly optimize opaque triangle primitives as a replacement for Gaussians, aiming to produce outputs that are immediately usable in standard graphics engines without post-processing [48]. By demonstrating that mesh-like primitives can be optimized effectively, these works pave the way for 3DGS-inspired techniques to penetrate the vast ecosystem of existing mesh-based software and hardware, which is a critical factor for industrial adoption [37].

**Balancing Visual Fidelity and Geometric Accuracy.** 3DGS offers a unique equilibrium between photorealistic appearance and the fidelity of the underlying 3D geometry, a balance that is directly influenced by the approximate nature of its rendering model discussed earlier. While early reconstructions were put to question regarding geometric accuracy, a large body of subsequent work has focused on enhancing this aspect without sacrificing the rendering speed enabled by efficient splatting. Techniques range from incorporating normal and depth priors to regularize the Gaussians towards surface alignment [49], to hybrid representations that integrate signed distance fields (SDFs) for more globally consistent geometry. However, this balance is not trivial; for instance, while 2D Gaussian representations enforce multi-view consistency and better surface alignment, they can compromise texture details [50]. This tension is well-recognized, and hybrid methods that combine the strengths of different representations are becoming increasingly common [51]. The 3DGS community is actively working to ensure that "photorealism" and "correct geometry" are not seen as opposing goals but as complementary ones, a trend that is critical for applications like robotic navigation and digital twins that demand both a visually compelling and geometrically reliable representation [4].

In summary, the convergence of real-time rendering, rapid training, explicit editability, and growing compatibility with standard graphics pipelines makes 3DGS a uniquely versatile tool. The internal pipeline efficiencies that enable real-time performance, coupled with the explicit primitive structure that permits rapid optimization and direct manipulation, collectively ensure that 3DGS is not merely a rendering technique but a comprehensive 3D data structure. This balanced approach positions it to serve as the backbone for a new generation of applications. From enabling photorealistic digital twins for autonomous driving [52] and supporting memory-efficient real-time SLAM systems [53] to powering immersive VR experiences and generative world models [4; 41], the core properties of 3DGS establish it as a foundational technology. As research continues to address its limitations in areas such as robustness and scalability, its role as a bridge between the worlds of neural rendering and practical computer graphics is set to become even more central [1; 54].

## 2 Core Representation and Rendering Techniques

### 2.1 Alternative Primitive Designs and Kernel Extensions

The foundational 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) framework relies on symmetric, isotropic Gaussian primitives that encode a single view-dependent color and a global opacity. While this representation achieves impressive results, its inherent limitations—particularly its low-pass nature and the coupling of geometry with appearance—often necessitate a large number of primitives to faithfully capture sharp boundaries, high-frequency textures, and complex surface details. To overcome these constraints, a rich body of research has explored alternative primitive designs and kernel extensions. These innovations generally aim to enhance the expressivity of individual primitives, thereby improving representation compactness, rendering sharpness, and fidelity at challenging regions such as object silhouettes and high-frequency structures. This subsection surveys these advances, which form the representational foundation upon which subsequent techniques for scale-aware regularization, frequency-domain supervision, and multi-view consistency are built.

One prominent direction replaces the standard 3D Gaussian volume with **2D Gaussian surfels**. By setting the z-scale of a 3D Gaussian to zero, these primitives become flat, planar ellipses that align well with object surfaces. This design has been shown to improve both reconstruction quality and surface alignment, as it provides clearer geometric guidance during optimization. The use of 2D Gaussian surfels within the 3DGS framework has enabled high-fidelity geometric reconstruction alongside novel view synthesis [21]. Furthermore, this planar representation serves as a foundation for integrating per-primitive textures, effectively decoupling geometry and appearance, which proves beneficial for scenes with simple geometry but complex textures [55]. This early separation of geometry and appearance is a recurrent theme that resonates with the multi-view consistency and transparency challenges addressed later in this survey.

Another key advancement involves **asymmetric and skewed kernels** to better capture discontinuities. The standard Gaussian’s symmetric shape is ill-suited for representing one-sided surfaces or sharp edges. To address this, researchers have introduced the Azzalini Skew-Normal distribution as a more general primitive. By incorporating a learnable skewness parameter, these skew-normal kernels can continuously interpolate between symmetric Gaussians and half-Gaussian-like shapes, enabling flexible modeling of both sharp boundaries and interior regions while maintaining analytical tractability for rasterization [56]. Similarly, the 3D Skew Gaussian Splatting (3DSGS) framework extends the standard primitive to a skew-normal counterpart, enhancing structural fidelity and compactness, particularly in regions with intricate details [57]. Beyond these, the concept of deformable kernels has been explored to overcome the radial symmetry constraint. For instance, Deformable Radial Kernels (DRK) use learnable radial bases with adjustable angles and scales, allowing for precise control over edge sharpness and boundary curvature and leading to higher representation efficiency [58]. The Deformable Beta Splatting (DBS) method replaces Gaussian kernels with deformable Beta kernels that offer bounded support and adaptive frequency control, capturing fine geometric details with greater fidelity and improved memory efficiency [59]. These kernel-level modifications directly address the boundary sharpness issues that underpin the aliasing artifacts and high-frequency detail losses discussed in the subsequent subsection.

To enhance the fidelity of each primitive, another strand of research focuses on **textured and anisotropic kernels**. The core idea is to augment each Gaussian with a spatially varying color or opacity model. Innovatively, this allows a single primitive to represent complex visual phenomena that would otherwise require a cluster of standard Gaussians. The SVGS method implements this by using spatially varying functions, such as bilinear interpolation, and demonstrates that even a single 2D Gaussian surfel can effectively capture varying colors across its extent [60]. Similarly, GStex textures each 2D Gaussian primitive, making appearance representation agnostic to geometry complexity and enabling improved view synthesis when the number of primitives is reduced [55]. The "Textured Gaussians" approach generalizes the Gaussian appearance representation by augmenting each Gaussian with alpha, RGB, or RGBA texture maps, and finds that alpha-only and RGB texture maps significantly boost expressivity [17]. Further advancing this direction, adaptive anisotropic textured Gaussians (A²TG) dynamically adjust texture resolution and aspect ratio based on gradient guidance, leading to more efficient memory allocation while maintaining high rendering fidelity [20]. Content-aware texturing methods further refine this by adapting texture resolution during optimization, ensuring a principled trade-off between quality and the number of primitives [61]. By equipping primitives with internal spatial variation, these methods implicitly provide a form of scale-adaptive representation, complementing the explicit multi-scale supervision strategies described in the following subsection.

A significant limitation of standard 3DGS is its low-pass nature, which struggles to represent high-frequency details. To address this, a class of methods substitutes the Gaussian kernel with **Fourier-encoded or Gabor-based primitives**. The 3D Gabor kernel, a combination of a Gaussian term and wave functions, is introduced to directly represent spatial high-frequency signals, such as fine stripes, which would normally require a large number of skinny Gaussian kernels [62]. Extending this, 3D Gabor Splatting (3DGabSplat) leverages a filter bank of multiple directional 3D Gabor kernels at different frequencies, developing an efficient CUDA-based rasterizer to handle the additional frequency components and a frequency-adaptive mechanism for joint optimization [63]. Neural Gabor Splatting takes a hybrid approach, augmenting each Gaussian primitive with a lightweight multi-layer perceptron (MLP) to model a wide range of color variations within a single primitive, coupled with a frequency-aware densification strategy to control primitive numbers [64]. Fourier Splatting represents another form of scalable primitives. This method parameterizes planar surfels with Fourier descriptors, naming the new primitive "Fourier-encoded", which allows a single model to be rendered at varying levels of detail by simply truncating different numbers of Fourier coefficients at runtime [65]. The "Brane" primitive, introduced in Resonant Brane Splatting, augments the standard Gaussian envelope with internal Gaussian-Hermite modes, assigning distinct color coefficients to each mode. This enables a single Brane to represent spatially varying colors and high-frequency local contrast, improving the speed-quality trade-off in super-resolution tasks [66]. The frequency-aware design of these primitives naturally complements the frequency-domain supervision losses explored in the next subsection, where explicit control over spectral content is used to regularize optimization.

The pursuit of higher expressivity also extends to **kernel extensions with higher-order or non-Gaussian forms**. Drawing inspiration from quantum physics, the Gaussian-Hermite kernel has been proposed as a new primitive, offering improved performance in both geometry reconstruction and novel view synthesis due to its enhanced ability to model sharp edges and anisotropies [67]. Similarly, 3D Linear Splatting (3DLS) replaces Gaussian kernels with linear kernels that possess sharp, hard boundaries, resolving the blurring artifacts caused by the soft boundaries of Gaussian ellipsoids and achieving sharper results in high-frequency regions [68]. These kernel-level innovations directly target the sampling and reconstruction fidelity issues that are central to the anti-aliasing and detail-preservation challenges.

Many of these alternative primitive designs also incorporate enhancements to the rasterization or rendering pipeline to ensure they are compatible with real-time performance and remain differentiable. For example, the depth-peeling strategy is used with Gaussian-enhanced surfels (GES) to establish accurate per-pixel ordering, eliminating popping artifacts and enabling correct transmittance modulation in a sort-free rendering pipeline [69]. Another design, dual-opacity primitives, decouples a Gaussian’s visual existence from its instance occupancy, which proves crucial for object-aware segmentation tasks [70]. The CT-based factorized representation of Gaussian-Enhanced Surfels (GES) supports semi-transparent rendering and high-performance, sort-free rendering, successfully avoiding popping artifacts [24]. A factorization into geometric occupancy and optical opacity, as in RT-Splatting, allows a single set of primitives to handle both high-frequency specular reflections and clear transmission on semi-transparent surfaces, mitigating the common pitfalls of blurry reflections or overly occluded transmissions [71]. These pipeline-level improvements directly address the transparency ordering and multi-view consistency issues that are further elaborated in the subsequent subsection, where advanced transparency modeling is discussed in the context of aliasing and detail preservation.

Overall, these diverse kernel designs substantially expand the representational capacity of Gaussian primitives. By moving beyond simple symmetric Gaussian functions, these methods address the critical issues of boundary sharpness, high-frequency detail modeling, and appearance-geometry decoupling, leading to more compact scene representations and improved fidelity at object boundaries and in high-frequency regions. The continuous evolution of these primitive designs represents a fundamental avenue for advancing the efficiency and quality of radiance field rendering, and it provides the enriched representational toolkit upon which the anti-aliasing, frequency-domain supervision, and multi-view consistency techniques of the subsequent subsection are built.

### 2.2 Anti-Aliasing, Multi-View Consistency, and High-Frequency Detail Modeling

The pursuit of high-fidelity novel view synthesis with 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) is fundamentally challenged by two intertwined issues: aliasing artifacts arising from the discrete sampling of continuous scenes and the accurate reconstruction of high-frequency details, which are often sacrificed in favor of smooth, low-frequency approximations. Furthermore, ensuring that the reconstructed scene remains consistent across multiple viewpoints—a property that is not inherently guaranteed by per-pixel photometric losses—is critical to avoid flickering and ghosting artifacts. The primitive designs discussed in the previous subsection—such as Gabor-based kernels, skewed distributions, and textured surfels—expand the representational capacity of individual Gaussians, yet they do not by themselves resolve the scale-dependent and frequency-dependent pitfalls of optimization. To fully leverage these enhanced primitives, the optimization process itself must be regularized to respect scale, frequency, and multi-view coherence. This subsection surveys techniques designed to address these challenges, focusing on scale-aware regularization, multi-scale supervision, frequency-domain losses, and advanced transparency modeling.

**Anti-Aliasing and Scale-Aware Regularization.** Aliasing in 3DGS manifests as flickering textures, jagged edges, and popping artifacts, particularly when rendering at scales different from those observed during training. The root cause is the mismatch between the size of the Gaussian primitives and the pixel footprint at a given viewpoint. A direct inspiration comes from classical graphics, where mipmaps pre-filter textures to provide scale-appropriate representations. Analogously, Mipmap-GS [72] introduces a scale-consistency guidance loss and creates pseudo ground-truth images for target scales, effectively injecting scale information into the Gaussians. This allows the primitives to self-adjust their properties (color, shape, size, and position) for arbitrary zoom levels, leading to substantial PSNR improvements for both zoom-in and zoom-out scenarios compared to the baseline 3DGS model. This concept is further extended by works that use pyramidal multi-scale supervision to enforce coherence across different viewing distances. For large-scale urban environments, PrismGS [73] proposes a physically-grounded regularization framework that combines pyramidal multi-scale supervision with an explicit size regularizer. The multi-scale supervision compels the model to learn an inherently anti-aliased representation that remains coherent across scales, while the size regularization imposes a physical lower bound on Gaussian dimensions to prevent the formation of degenerate, view-dependent primitives, thereby reducing jagged edges and stabilizing optimization in demanding 4K rendering scenarios. At a more fundamental level, the choice of optimization objective itself can influence aliasing. A multi-sample anti-aliasing (MSAA) approach integrates adaptive blending of quadruple subsamples to compute pixel colors, effectively reducing aliasing in high-frequency regions. This is coupled with dual geometric constraints—an adaptive weighting strategy that prioritizes under-reconstructed regions and gradient differential constraints that enforce geometric regularization at object boundaries [74]. This targeted approach ensures that computational resources are allocated to critical regions requiring refinement while maintaining global consistency. These scale-aware strategies resonate with the textured and anisotropic primitives introduced earlier, as spatial internal variation within a primitive inherently provides a form of scale adaptation, complementing the explicit multi-scale supervision described here.

**Frequency-Domain Supervision and High-Frequency Detail Modeling.** The standard pixel-space losses (L1, SSIM) used in 3DGS optimization constrain only the aggregate reconstruction error, permitting the redistribution of error across frequency scales. This often leads to oversmoothing and a loss of fine details—precisely the limitation that Gabor-based and Fourier-encoded primitives aim to overcome. To address this, a significant body of work has shifted supervision into the frequency domain, providing an external constraint that complements the internal frequency expressivity of these enhanced kernels. A foundational strategy is progressive frequency regularization, as exemplified by FreGS [75]. FreGS performs coarse-to-fine Gaussian densification by exploiting low-to-high frequency components extracted via low-pass and high-pass filters in the Fourier space. By minimizing the discrepancy between the frequency spectrum of the rendered image and the ground truth, it effectively alleviates over-reconstruction and produces sharper images. Building on this, KC-3DGS [76] provides a theoretical analysis showing that pixel-space losses admit a family of indistinguishable perturbations under wavelet redistribution. To exclude these degenerate solutions, it introduces a multi-scale wavelet coefficient alignment loss to explicitly penalize missing high-frequency detail, a supervised kurtosis concentration loss to match the heavy-tailed frequency statistics of natural images, and a cross-band covariance penalty to promote frequency specialization. This wavelet-domain supervision leads to significant improvements in perceptual quality (e.g., DreamSim) with minimal impact on standard metrics.

The choice of frequency transform is crucial. While Fourier transforms provide global frequency information, they lack spatial locality. In contrast, the Discrete Wavelet Transform (DWT) offers both frequency and spatial information, making it more suitable for identifying and supervising localized high-frequency features. DWTGS [77] argues that Fourier-based regularization is difficult to tune and can be biased towards detrimental high-frequency learning in sparse-view settings. Instead, it proposes a wavelet-space loss that supervises only the low-frequency LL subbands at multiple DWT levels while enforcing sparsity on the high-frequency HH subband in a self-supervised manner. This low-frequency-centric strategy improves generalization and reduces high-frequency hallucinations. A further advancement is the use of learnable wavelet transforms. Learnable Multi-level DWT [78] introduces a multi-level DWT-based frequency modulation framework. By recursively decomposing the low-frequency subband, it constructs a deeper curriculum that provides progressively coarser supervision during early training, which consistently reduces the final Gaussian count while maintaining rendering quality. This method also simplifies the modulation to a single scaling parameter, improving efficiency and stability over learning the full filter bank. Beyond regularization, frequency-domain thinking can also inform geometry recovery. PSRGS [79] uses spectral residual maps to separate low-frequency and high-frequency regions, applying different densification thresholds and loss functions to each region. This progressive scheme ensures that geometry is initialized in low-frequency regions and refined in high-frequency areas, leading to better recovery of fine-grained details in large-scale remote sensing scenes. The synergy between these frequency-domain supervision losses and the frequency-adaptive primitives from the previous subsection is evident: the former provides the optimization signal, while the latter offers the representational capacity to absorb it effectively.

**Multi-View Consistency and Advanced Transparency.** Ensuring multi-view consistency is a prerequisite for coherent rendering, especially in dynamic or sparsely sampled scenes. While frequency-domain losses contribute to consistency by preventing the model from fitting view-specific high-frequency noise, explicit methods are also needed. For instance, the geometry-constrained framework in GSM-GS [80] employs a geometry-guided cross-view point cloud association method combined with a dynamic weight sampling strategy to construct 3D structural normal constraints across adjacent views, reinforcing multi-view consistency for reconstruction. This concern also directly connects to the primitive designs of the previous subsection, particularly dual-opacity primitives and factorization into geometric occupancy and optical opacity, which provide the structural mechanisms for consistent transparency handling.

Aliasing is not only a spatial but also a temporal problem, particularly when sorting transparent primitives. The standard alpha-blending pipeline in 3DGS relies on a fixed depth sorting, which can lead to popping artifacts and incorrect transmittance. This is a specific instance of the order-independent transparency (OIT) problem. While STAR-NT [81] addresses OIT in a different context, the concept is highly relevant to 3DGS, where a high-quality rendering of overlapping surfaces requires a correct handling of transmittance. To eliminate artifacts arising from incorrect sorting, methods that focus on order-independent transparency are crucial. Finally, to further enhance high-frequency detail, Sparse-Up [82] addresses the "pixel-level pain point" of high-frequency detail loss in 3D asset generation by using surface anchoring to constrain voxels to the mesh surface, eliminating redundant voxels, and a view-domain partitioning scheme to supervise only visible local patches. This approach breaks through resolution constraints while maintaining multi-view consistency and enabling high-fidelity texture recovery. Similarly, SubSplat [83] introduces a Sub-pixel Gaussian Reparameterizer to subdivide primary Gaussians into finer-grained primitives, restoring structural density and capturing high-frequency details from low-resolution features in pixel-aligned pipelines. These methods also foreshadow the hybrid mesh-Gaussian strategies of the following subsection, where geometric anchoring and explicit surface constraints become central to achieving consistent, high-fidelity reconstructions. In summary, the field is moving towards a holistic approach where anti-aliasing, high-frequency detail recovery, and multi-view consistency are jointly addressed through a combination of scale-aware regularization, sophisticated frequency-domain supervision in the wavelet domain, and more robust rendering algorithms.

### 2.3 Hybrid Representations with Meshes, Implicit Fields, and Volumetric Grids

Hybrid representations have emerged as a cornerstone of modern 3D scene modeling, seeking to combine the complementary strengths of disparate primitives while mitigating their individual weaknesses. Within the realm of 3D Gaussian Splatting, this fusion strategy addresses fundamental tensions between rendering fidelity, geometric accuracy, storage efficiency, and compatibility with established graphics pipelines. By integrating 3D Gaussians with triangle meshes, implicit fields, and volumetric grids, researchers have developed unified frameworks that leverage the explicit, real-time rendering capabilities of Gaussians alongside the structural coherence and compactness of other representations. While the preceding discussions have focused on refining the Gaussian representation itself—through scale-aware regularization, frequency-domain supervision, and multi-view consistency—this subsection shifts the perspective outward, examining how Gaussians can be embedded within larger, multi-primitive ecosystems. Such hybridization not only addresses the limitations of pure Gaussian representations but also positions 3DGS as a versatile component compatible with established graphics pipelines, a theme that will be further explored in the subsequent discussion on non-pinhole camera adaptations and performance optimizations.

A significant body of work focuses on hybridizing Gaussians with triangle meshes, motivated by the need for explicit geometry that is compatible with traditional graphics engines and simulation pipelines. The core challenge lies in enabling joint optimization and seamless rendering between these fundamentally different primitives. [84] presents a unified framework that rasterizes mesh triangles and 3D Gaussian fragments in a single-pass, anti-aliased manner, using alpha-blending to achieve visually coherent results with precise occlusion handling. It further introduces a Gaussian-centric binding strategy that associates Gaussians with mesh faces for deformation, reducing artifacts and enabling the unified manipulation of objects represented by either modality. Similarly, [48] and [85] advocate for a "triangle comeback," arguing that triangles are the fundamental primitive of computer graphics. They propose directly optimizing triangles within a differentiable splatting framework, treating them as splats to combine the efficiency of Gaussian-like rendering with the inherent compatibility of meshes. [86] develops a differentiable renderer that optimizes triangles via end-to-end gradients, rendering each triangle as a differentiable splat. This approach achieves higher visual fidelity and faster convergence than Gaussian methods while producing outputs directly usable in standard graphics engines, as highlighted by [48]. [85] replaces 3D Gaussian primitives with 2D triangles that naturally form a discrete mesh-like structure, using controlled annealing of a compactness parameter to ensure differentiability during training while converging to fully opaque triangle meshes. [87] tightly integrates Gaussian and mesh representations by analytically deriving each Gaussian primitive from its corresponding mesh triangle, preserving structural fidelity and enabling smoother gradients more conducive to optimization with small batch sizes. [88] uses a collection of disconnected translucent triangle primitives, termed triangle soup, and introduces soft connectivity forces between them to encourage explicit surface continuity during optimization, improving geometric accuracy without sacrificing visual fidelity. [89] argues for a loose coupling of coarse triangles for geometry and Gaussians for appearance, yielding a compact and expressive model that adapts to varying scene complexity. [47] introduces a hybrid representation that uses textured meshes for texture-rich flat areas and retains Gaussians for intricate geometries, employing a joint optimization with transmittance-aware supervision to balance their contributions, resulting in higher FPS with fewer Gaussians. [90] binds 3D Gaussians to mesh faces for an end-to-end learnable representation, enabling direct information pathways for jointly learning explicit geometry and renderable appearance, which supports efficient local scene modifications. [91] presents a hybrid representation combining Bézier triangle-based vector graphics with Gaussian probabilistic models, maintaining accurate shape modeling, especially at sharp boundaries, while enabling resolution-independent differentiable rendering. [92] employs structured tetrahedral meshes, reparameterizing their geometric attributes into Gaussian primitives for differentiable rendering, and introduces an inversion-free homeomorphism to maintain a conformal, self-intersection-free mesh throughout optimization. In defense-related applications, [93] argues that a hybrid approach augmenting a mesh scaffold with neural representations like 3DGS offers the most promising trade-off between geometric integrity for functional tasks and photorealistic visuals for situational awareness. While meshes provide explicit, topologically sound structure, their integration with Gaussians often requires careful handling of transparency and occlusion, as well as robust optimization strategies to avoid conflicts between the two primitives, as addressed in [84] and [47].

Another major axis of hybridization integrates Gaussians with implicit fields, most notably signed distance fields (SDFs) and neural feature grids, to improve geometric fidelity and compactness. This direction is particularly complementary to the frequency-domain regularization discussed earlier, as implicit fields provide a natural mechanism for enforcing global structural coherence that complements the local, explicit nature of Gaussian primitives. [94] embeds SDF learning directly into the 3DGS pipeline by associating each Gaussian with a learnable SDF value, explicitly aligning primitives with underlying geometry and improving cross-view consistency, while employing a geometry-guided grid growth strategy for dense coverage. [95] tethers scaffold-anchored Gaussians to a jointly optimized sparse voxel scaffold, confining the Gaussians to a narrow band around surfaces defined by voxelized SDFs, thereby improving representation efficiency and condensing floating primitives without sacrificing geometric accuracy. [96] integrates explicit point clouds with implicit feature embeddings via a multi-level tri-plane architecture, using 2D feature grids at various resolutions to enhance spatial correlations among Gaussians, which enables high-quality rendering with only a few megabytes of storage. [97] and [98] propose a compact representation using a learnable mask to reduce the number of Gaussians and a grid-based neural field for view-dependent color, achieving substantial storage reduction while maintaining rendering quality. [99] introduces a decoupled neural field architecture that separately models geometry and view-dependent color, combining explicit Gaussians with grid-based neural fields for a compact yet expressive representation. [100] implicitly encodes a point cloud within a continuous octree-based probability field and a multi-resolution hash grid, combining the fast rendering of rasterization with the ability to preserve fine geometric details without relying on structure-from-motion priors. These implicit integrations often face challenges in balancing the expressiveness of the neural field with the explicit control of the Gaussian primitives, and require carefully designed optimization schedules to ensure coherent learning across both components.

The integration of volumetric grids provides a third avenue for hybrid 3DGS systems, primarily targeting global coherence and improved initialization. [52] combines a grid-based volume with the 3DGS pipeline to eliminate the dependency on SfM point initialization and improve rendering in distant, sky, and low-texture areas, incorporating a point densification strategy for enhanced quality. [101] and [92] also explore grid-based structures for feature encoding and geometric regularization. Furthermore, hybrid approaches extend to other representations, such as superquadrics, as in [102], which integrates 2D Gaussians with superquadric meshes for interpretable, part-aware scene decomposition. The success of these hybrid systems highlights a key insight: the future of high-fidelity 3D reconstruction may not lie in a single universal primitive, but in the principled combination of multiple representations, each contributing its unique strengths to the unified goal of photorealistic and geometrically accurate scene modeling. This insight becomes particularly salient as we move to consider how these hybrid representations can be adapted to non-pinhole camera models and optimized for real-time performance on diverse hardware platforms, topics that form the foundation of the subsequent section.

### 2.4 Adaptations for Diverse Camera Models and Real-Time Rendering Efficiency

The default 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) pipeline is fundamentally designed around the pinhole camera model, which assumes a linear perspective projection. While this model is appropriate for standard narrow field-of-view (FoV) images, it becomes a limiting factor when applying 3DGS to the increasingly prevalent non-pinhole camera systems found in consumer devices, robotics, and autonomous driving. This subsection surveys the key adaptations required to extend 3DGS to fisheye, panoramic, and omnidirectional cameras, and then examines performance-oriented enhancements that ensure real-time rendering efficiency on large-scale and mobile platforms. These efforts complement the hybrid representation strategies discussed previously, as they collectively broaden the applicability of 3DGS beyond controlled, pinhole-based scenarios, paving the way for deployment in diverse real-world settings.

A primary challenge in adapting 3DGS to non-pinhole cameras lies in the projection step. The naive approach of undistorting images before training introduces significant artifacts. For fisheye cameras, the wide FoV is a key advantage, yet undistortion can introduce black borders and resample the image, diluting high-frequency detail and leading to blurry or floating artifacts. To address this, methods like Fisheye-GS and DirectFisheye-GS [103; 104] have been developed to integrate the fisheye camera model directly into the 3DGS optimization pipeline, recalculating the projection transformation and its gradients natively. This approach preserves the full FoV and detail of the original images. However, DirectFisheye-GS [104] also identifies a novel issue: the standard per-iteration random-view selection in 3DGS neglects cross-view correlations, leading to extreme Gaussian shapes and floaters, particularly at the highly distorted image periphery. Their proposed feature-overlap-driven cross-view joint optimization establishes consistent constraints across views to mitigate this. This focus on cross-view consistency directly echoes the multi-view coherence strategies discussed in the context of hybrid representations, underscoring its importance regardless of the underlying primitive.

For panoramic and omnidirectional images, the challenges are even more pronounced. The equirectangular projection (ERP) used for these images lacks a single center of projection, making it incompatible with the perspective rasterizer in vanilla 3DGS. A common strategy to bridge this gap is to project the 360° image onto a multi-face representation, such as a cubemap. However, this approach suffers from discontinuities and projection distortions at face boundaries. The work on TPGS [105] introduces a novel "Transition Plane" between adjacent cube faces to enable smoother splatting directions and reduce optimization ambiguity in boundary regions. Another line of work, exemplified by ODGS [106], takes a more geometrically principled approach. For each Gaussian, a tangent plane is defined on the unit sphere. The Gaussian is then projected onto this tangent plane using a standard perspective rasterizer, and the results are transformed and combined into the final omnidirectional image. This approach provides a formal mathematical interpretation and achieves significant speedups over NeRF-based methods. Similarly, UniTriSplat [107] proposes a unified framework that reformulates Gaussian splatting directly on the unit sphere using HEALPix discretization, leveraging its equal-area property to create a uniform optimization behavior across different camera models, from narrow-FoV to full 360-degree panoramas.

Beyond the core projection, achieving high geometric consistency is critical for panoramic 3DGS. Methods like Spherical-GOF [108] move away from projection-based rasterization altogether, performing ray sampling directly on the unit sphere within a Gaussian Opacity Field framework. This approach derives a conservative spherical bounding rule for efficient ray-Gaussian culling and a spherical filtering scheme that adapts Gaussian footprints to the distortion-varying pixel sampling of panoramic images, leading to substantially improved geometric consistency. The scalability of these methods to large outdoor scenes is addressed by frameworks like PanoLOG [109], which proposes a geometry and gradient-based partitioning strategy for large-scale panoramic reconstruction. This strategy uses parallax-driven uncertainty to build adaptive bounding volumes, avoiding the pitfalls of naive partitioning methods that fail with omnipresent visibility. Such geometric rigor is reminiscent of the structural coherence advocated by implicit-field hybridizations, suggesting a convergence of design principles across different adaptations.

In parallel with these representation-level adaptations, significant effort has been dedicated to improving the real-time rendering performance of 3DGS, which is a key advantage over implicit representations. This performance focus is essential not only for standalone Gaussian systems but also for hybrid representations, where the added complexity of multiple primitives must not compromise interactive frame rates. One major bottleneck is the rasterization stage, which is often compute-bound. A promising direction is the use of Tensor Cores, which are high-throughput units for low-precision matrix operations that sit idle in standard 3DGS implementations. Both TensorGS and TC-GS [39; 110] propose to "tensorize" the rasterization process by mapping the alpha-blending computation to matrix multiplications, enabling the use of FP16 arithmetic with negligible quality degradation. TensorGS [39] further introduces a cross-tile grouping strategy to improve Gaussian data reuse and increase Tensor Core utilization. These approaches demonstrate significant end-to-end rendering speedups. Related work on hardware acceleration includes an architecture-algorithm co-design for axis-shared rasterization and order-independent transmittance [111], which removes the need for explicit sorting, a costly operation for large scenes.

Another significant source of inefficiency in 3DGS rendering is poor GPU utilization due to load imbalance and redundant computation. Hierarchical tiling strategies decouple the spatial partitioning and rasterization tile sizes, allowing each to be optimized independently. HiGS [26] partitions space using coarse macro-tiles while rasterizing within fine render tiles, issuing work in proportion to the Gaussians in each macro-tile and preventing dense regions from serializing through a single tile. Similarly, Local-GS [112] focuses on improving SIMT (Single Instruction, Multiple Threads) execution by organizing Gaussian primitives with respect to warp boundaries, reducing warp divergence and redundant computation. At a higher level, CaT-GS [40] identifies inter-frame redundancy and severe tile-level load imbalance as major bottlenecks. It introduces a speculative multi-frame preprocessing method and an inter-frame caching mechanism to eliminate redundant work across frames, refactoring the rasterization tasks to mitigate load imbalance. These optimizations are crucial for scaling 3DGS to large-scale scenes, and they are complemented by workload-balancing frameworks like LoBE-GS [113], which uses a load-balanced KD-tree partitioning scheme to ensure equal per-block camera counts across multiple GPUs.

For resource-constrained platforms like mobile devices, aggressive optimizations are required. Frameworks like SeeLe [114] combine a view-dependent scene representation with online filtering to reduce the number of relevant Gaussians before rasterization, and a contribution-aware rasterization that prioritizes high-contribution Gaussians. On-device training, a much more stringent requirement, is tackled by PocketGS [115], which uses geometry-faithful point-cloud priors and a backpropagation strategy with cached intermediates to enable training within strict memory and time budgets. Finally, exploring the boundaries of 3DGS rendering, CoherentRaster [116] adapts 3DGS for light field displays that require rendering an interlaced image encoding many views. By employing cross-view coherent attribute reuse and view-coherent remapping, it achieves real-time, high-quality light field synthesis on consumer-grade hardware. Together, these advancements in native camera model support and performance-oriented rendering techniques establish 3DGS as a versatile and practical technology across a wide array of real-world applications, while also reinforcing the trend toward principled, multi-representational systems that can adapt to both diverse capture hardware and stringent performance budgets.

## 3 Optimization, Densification, and Training Strategies

### 3.1 Adaptive Density Control and Primitive Management

Adaptive density control (ADC) constitutes the heart of the 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) optimization pipeline, governing the birth, growth, and elimination of Gaussian primitives throughout training. The original 3DGS framework introduced a heuristic yet remarkably effective mechanism that periodically assesses the positional gradients of each Gaussian, leveraging them as a proxy for geometric under-reconstruction, and subsequently performs either cloning or splitting operations to densify the representation in regions of high error, while simultaneously pruning Gaussians whose opacity falls below a threshold [117]. Although this foundational approach has demonstrated impressive results, the research community has extensively analyzed its limitations and proposed a rich taxonomy of refinements that re-engineer various aspects of the densification and pruning pipeline. The primary deficiencies of the original ADC can be distilled into three broad categories: the criteria used to trigger densification, the operational mechanics of clone and split operations, and the strategies for pruning or eliminating redundant or overfitted Gaussians.

A significant body of work focuses on refining the trigger criterion that determines *when* and *which* Gaussians should be densified. The vanilla 3DGS uses the magnitude of the accumulated view-space positional gradient, which has been shown to be vulnerable to a failure mode known as "gradient collision." In regions suffering from over-reconstruction with high-frequency details, conflicting gradient directions from different views can cancel out, preventing large, blurry Gaussians from meeting the splitting threshold. This issue is explicitly analyzed in [118], which proposes the homodirectional view-space positional gradient as a more reliable criterion. By aggregating only the component of the gradient that is coherent across views, this approach effectively identifies large Gaussians in over-reconstructed regions that require splitting, leading to the recovery of fine details. Similarly, the Gradient Coherence Ratio (GCR) is introduced to explicitly discriminate between Gaussians with concordant versus conflicting gradient directions, enabling gradient-direction-aware density control [119]. This allows for prioritized splitting of conflicting-gradient Gaussians to enhance geometry while suppressing the proliferation of redundant concordant-direction Gaussians. Another line of work replaces the positional gradient with a more reliable statistical signal. For instance, an opacity-gradient-based trigger is proposed, where the gradient of opacity serves as a lightweight proxy for rendering error, which when paired with a conservative pruning schedule, yields significant improvements in compactness, especially in few-shot scenarios [120]. Furthermore, reinterpreting densification as a signal verification problem, the CAdam framework leverages the first moment of gradients to exploit the principle of destructive and constructive interference, effectively disentangling geometric signals from the stochastic noise floor introduced by generative guidance, thereby addressing the Densification Dilemma in optimization-based generative distillation [121]. Beyond gradient statistics, some methods incorporate multi-view photometric errors into a probabilistic framework. For example, a unified adaptive sampling approach within a Metropolis-Hastings framework dynamically inserts and relocates Gaussians based on aggregated multi-view errors and opacity scores, significantly reducing reliance on hand-crafted heuristics [122]. Alternatively, the identification of error-contributing zones through multiview geometry constraints and image rendering errors enables localized point management, where densification can occur in regions not adequately captured by global gradient heuristics, such as transparent objects [123].

Once the decision to densify is made, the mechanics of the clone and split operations themselves have been scrutinized. The original random splitting, which divides a large Gaussian into two smaller ones with fixed cardinality and random placement, is inefficient, often requiring many iterations to expose fine details and inducing geometric distortion. To address this, Long-Axis Split was proposed to precisely control the position, shape, and opacity of child Gaussians, minimizing the difference before and after splitting and thereby reducing distortions [124; 42]. A more principled, error-driven adaptive splitting operator, AdpSplit, determines the number of split children and initializes their parameters from L1-pixel-error region statistics, leading to faster geometry discovery and reduced training time [125]. Another technique, which it frames in terms of optimization theory, proposes that splitting is crucial for escaping saddle points in the loss landscape, establishing necessary conditions for densification, determining the minimal number of offspring Gaussians, and providing analytical solutions for normalizing offspring opacity—resulting in a steepest descent density control strategy [126]. A related reformulation treats Gaussian centers as samples from a learnable probability density function over an octree or hashed probability pyramid, integrating densification into a continuous, end-to-end gradient-based optimization process that replaces discrete heuristics entirely [127; 128]. Finally, the volumetric refinement process can be driven by exploring the volume of inertia associated with each Gaussian to guide the refinement process, offering an alternative to purely gradient-based geometry discovery [129].

On the other end of the spectrum, the pruning and elimination of redundant or detrimental Gaussians is critical for maintaining a compact, efficient, and high-fidelity model. A common observation is that naive opacity-based pruning can inadvertently remove beneficial Gaussians or fail to eliminate overfitted ones. Recovery-Aware Pruning leverages the differences in recovery speed after resetting opacity to identify and prune Gaussians that are overfitted to training views, thereby improving generalization [124; 42]. A significance-aware pruning strategy, which considers the contribution of Gaussians to the rendering of background regions, was proposed to avoid background artifacts [130]. More advanced methods formulate pruning as an optimization problem. For instance, Gradient-Driven Natural Selection models survival pressure as a regularization gradient field applied to opacity, allowing the optimization gradients themselves to autonomously determine which Gaussians to retain or prune, making the process fully learnable and human-free [131]. Similarly, a variational framework formulates Gaussian pruning as variational free energy minimization, using prediction-error gating for asynchronous pruning decisions [132]. Others have focused on the granularity of ranking, distinguishing between scene-level and pixel-level pruning; it has been shown that pixel-level pruning, which ranks primitives based on their contribution to specific rendering equations, not only sustains higher quality under extreme decimation but also provides an inherent pruning boundary that prevents catastrophic scene destruction [133]. To further enhance spatial consistency, an evidence lower bound-based adaptive densification strategy combined with an opacity-aware progressive pruning strategy and a graph-based feature encoding module has been proposed to dynamically adjust the spatial distribution and remove low-opacity Gaussians [134]. Beyond individual heuristics, some frameworks treat the global primitive distribution as a resource allocation problem. For instance, the global budget-constrained optimization formulation in SPARE-GS derives an optimality condition requiring marginal utility to be balanced across spatial regions, dynamically aligning the distribution of primitives with regional representational demand and coordinating densification, pruning, and adaptive termination [135].
