Another approach, by treating the existing Gaussian set as an empirical probability field and resampling centers from it, rebuilds the correlation among primitives, changing the gradient accessibility under alpha compositing and weakening local parameter coupling to enable further optimization of converged models [136].

In summary, the field of adaptive density control has evolved from a single, rigid heuristic towards a diverse ecosystem of sophisticated, often principled, mechanisms. These advances collectively aim to strike a delicate balance between representation fidelity and compactness, addressing long-standing issues like over-reconstruction, floaters, memory bloat, and slow convergence, and paving the way for more efficient and robust deployment of 3DGS across a wide range of applications.

### 3.2 Initialization Strategies and Coarse-to-Fine Optimization Schedules

The quality and convergence behavior of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) are profoundly influenced by how the initial set of Gaussian primitives is established and how the optimization process unfolds over time. While the adaptive density control mechanisms discussed previously govern the *birth, growth, and elimination* of Gaussians during training, the *starting conditions* and the *temporal schedule* of optimization determine the efficiency and stability of this entire process. Standard 3DGS relies on Structure-from-Motion (SfM) point clouds for initialization; however, the fidelity and density of these initial points often prove inadequate, particularly in large-scale, textureless, or sparsely observed regions. Recognizing the critical role of initialization, the research community has developed a spectrum of techniques ranging from enhanced SfM pipelines and learned initialization strategies to dense, correspondence-based seeding. Concurrently, the optimization schedule itself has been rethought, transitioning from monolithic, high-frequency supervision toward coarse-to-fine paradigms that modulate input signal frequency, rendering resolution, or wavelet subbands for more stable and efficient convergence, thereby establishing a favorable landscape for the parameter optimization strategies examined in the next section.

A primary line of inquiry addresses the deficiencies of standard SfM. In sparse-view settings, where feature matching becomes unreliable, SfM often yields poor point clouds, leading to overfitting and artifacts. To counter this, frequency-aware SfM has been proposed, which augments the input images with low-frequency versions and relaxes multi-view correspondences to improve coverage in low-texture areas [137]. However, even with enhanced SfM, the initial point cloud remains sparse. To supplement these regions, 3DGS self-initialization techniques have been developed, which lift photometric supervision from training images into additional learned Gaussian centers, thereby compensating for SfM-sparse regions [137]. Furthermore, point-cloud regularization using geometric and visibility priors can enforce multi-view consistency and uniform spatial coverage, yielding a cleaner and more reliable starting point [137].

A more radical departure from incremental densification is the concept of eliminating the densification phase altogether through dense initialization. EDGS proposes a one-step approximation of scene geometry by triangulating pixels from dense image correspondences, providing each Gaussian with well-informed color, scale, and position [138]. This dense initialization ensures more uniform detail across the scene, particularly in high-frequency regions where sparse keypoint-based methods struggle, and dramatically shortens the optimization path [138]. In the realm of surface reconstruction, learned data-driven priors can generate dense initializations for 2D Gaussian splatting optimization, providing a strong starting point that accelerates convergence on large-scale indoor scenes and improves geometry on flat wall structures, as demonstrated by QuickSplat [139]. This work also proposes a learned densifier network that predicts new Gaussians based on rendering gradients, removing the need for heuristic-based densification [139].

Beyond initialization, the optimization schedule itself is pivotal. A major challenge in 3DGS training is the over-reconstruction of high-frequency image regions by a few large Gaussians, leading to blur and artifacts. To address this, coarse-to-fine optimization schedules have been widely adopted. A foundational approach is the use of progressive frequency regularization, which guides Gaussian densification from low-to-high frequency components by minimizing the discrepancy between the frequency spectrum of rendered and ground-truth images (FreGS) [75]. Signal structure-aware scheduling offers a more principled approach by deriving the average sampling frequency and bandwidth of 3D representations, then regulating both the training image resolution and the Gaussian densification process based on scene frequency convergence, a method that has shown state-of-the-art performance in large-scale scenes [140].

Wavelet-domain methods have emerged as a powerful tool for implementing these frequency-based schedules. The discrete wavelet transform (DWT) provides spatial and frequency localization simultaneously, making it ideal for constructing curriculum-based training. AutoOpti3DGS uses a learnable DWT with a fixed low-pass filter and a learnable high-pass filter, activating fine frequencies gradually in a coarse-to-fine manner that delays redundant Gaussian formation [141]. A subsequent extension demonstrates that modulating the low-frequency subband recursively with a multi-level DWT creates a deeper curriculum, consistently reducing Gaussian counts with only a single scaling parameter for the high-pass filter [78]. From a different angle, DWTGS rethinks sparse-view frequency regularization by supervising only the low-frequency LL subbands at multiple DWT levels while enforcing self-supervised sparsity on the high-frequency HH subband, avoiding the inherent bias of Fourier transforms toward detrimental high-frequency learning [77]. Building on such analyses, wavelet-domain supervision can be formulated as a plug-and-play regularization strategy that combines multi-scale wavelet coefficient alignment, kurtosis concentration, and cross-band covariance penalties to explicitly enforce frequency statistics of rendered images to match ground-truth, as proposed in KC-3DGS [76].

An alternative yet complementary scheduling paradigm involves dynamically modifying the rendering resolution during training. DashGaussian formulates 3DGS optimization as progressively fitting higher-frequency components of training views, using a dynamic rendering resolution scheme to reduce optimization complexity while synchronizing the growth of Gaussian primitives with the rendering resolution for a better trade-off between computing redundancy and fitting quality [142]. Energy-guided coarse-to-fine multi-resolution training frameworks further refine this idea by gradually increasing resolution based on the energy density in 2D images, cooperating with global-to-local densification strategies to promote efficient Gaussian growth across the scene space [143]. A particularly elegant synthesis of frequency and resolution scheduling is found in the Opti3DGS framework, which leverages image frequency modulation to enforce a coarse scene representation first, then progressively refines it by modulating frequency details; this approach yields an average 62% reduction in Gaussians and a 20% reduction in optimization time while naturally producing a level-of-detail representation [144]. Similarly, frequency-aware Gaussian decomposition organizes primitives into groups corresponding to Laplacian-pyramid subbands, enabling both interpretable level-of-detail representations and progressive training schedules [145].

Taken together, these initialization and scheduling techniques represent a fundamental shift in how 3DGS models are trained. Rather than treating all Gaussians and all image frequencies equally, modern approaches recognize that a data-adaptive, frequency- and resolution-aware curriculum—coupled with geometric and semantic priors for initialization—substantially improves convergence speed, model compactness, and rendering fidelity, especially in challenging sparse-view, large-scale, and high-frequency regimes. Moreover, by establishing a well-conditioned optimization landscape and a structured progression of learning targets, these strategies create the necessary preconditions for the advanced first- and second-order optimizers and stability-enhancing techniques discussed in the subsequent section, which further refine the convergence trajectory and final model quality.

### 3.3 First- and Second-Order Optimization Methods

The optimization of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) parameters is a critical determinant of both the final reconstruction quality and the computational cost of training. Building upon the initialization and scheduling strategies discussed previously, the choice and design of the optimizer itself plays an equally pivotal role in determining how efficiently and effectively the Gaussian parameters converge to a high-fidelity solution. While the original 3DGS framework relies on the Adam optimizer, a substantial body of research has explored both first- and second-order alternatives to accelerate convergence, improve fidelity, and enhance robustness, often drawing on and adapting advances from the broader deep learning optimization literature. This subsection systematically examines these optimization strategies, connecting them to the training pathologies and mitigation techniques covered in the subsequent discussion on stable convergence.

**First-Order Methods and Their Enhancements**

The default choice for 3DGS training has historically been Adam and its variants, owing to their adaptive per-parameter learning rates and robust performance across diverse scenes [146]. However, the general-purpose nature of these optimizers, which produce independent parameter updates, fails to capture the structural and spatial relationships inherent in a 3D scene, leading to inefficient optimization and slow convergence [147]. In response, researchers have proposed a range of first-order refinements. For instance, a detailed re-examination of the 3DGS optimization pipeline has revealed two overlooked coupling issues: update-step coupling, which induces optimizer state rescaling and costly attribute updates outside viewpoints, and gradient coupling in the moment, which can lead to under- or over-effective regularization. The resulting recomposed framework, termed AdamW-GS, demonstrates that decoupling and then selectively re-coupling beneficial components yields better optimization efficiency and representation effectiveness [148]. Furthermore, the initialization of the second-order moment estimation is a known pain point; setting the initial value to zero in Adam has been identified as a significant factor contributing to suboptimal generalization and instability. Simple data-driven or random non-zero initialization strategies for the second moment can stabilize convergence and enhance final performance [149]. Building on this, the substitution of the raw gradient with its momentumized version in the second raw moment estimate—termed AdaMomentum—has been shown to improve generalization while maintaining training speed [150]. Memory constraints have also motivated innovations such as SlimAdam, which applies a layer-wise Signal-to-Noise Ratio analysis to identify dimensions where second-moment tensors can be safely replaced by their means, achieving up to 98% memory savings in the second moment without sacrificing performance [151]. Beyond these architectural tweaks, gradient regularization (GR) has been demonstrated to improve generalizability. In a broader context, the integration of explicit gradient regularization into natural gradient descent leads to a family of scalable second-order optimizers (GRNG) that outperform first-order baselines and some second-order methods on vision and language benchmarks [152]. Moreover, in the specific context of 3DGS, a "warped gradient descent" concept from meta-learning can be integrated into Adam by introducing a learnable distortion matrix to linearly transform gradients, enhancing adaptation to distinct dataset characteristics [153].

**Flat-Minima Optimization and Generalization**

A significant challenge in sparse-view 3DGS is the tendency to overfit to the limited observed images and generalize poorly to unseen viewpoints, an issue that directly connects to the sparse-view regularization techniques elaborated in the following subsection. This issue has been addressed from the perspective of flat minima optimization, which seeks solutions that remain stable under small parameter perturbations [154]. By viewing Gaussian parameters as trainable weights, this approach regularizes optimization with controlled perturbations that account for each Gaussian's anisotropy and the training progress. The incorporation of a periodic reinitialization step further stabilizes the process, leading to sharper and more stable reconstructions under sparse supervision [154]. This focus on flat minima aligns with broader findings in deep learning that adaptive methods often converge to sharper basins of the loss landscape, hurting generalization. Memory-augmented versions of Adam that promote exploration toward flatter minima by using a buffer of critical momentum terms have shown to improve performance on supervised tasks [155]. Similarly, research on quantization-aware training for domain generalization highlights a "scale-gradient conflict" problem, where task loss and smoothness loss induce conflicting gradients for quantizer scaling factors; selectively freezing these gradients stabilizes training and enhances out-of-domain generalization, a principle applicable to the mixed-precision training of 3DGS models [156].

**Second-Order Optimization for 3DGS**

To address the slow convergence of first-order methods, a growing body of work has introduced second-order optimizers tailored to the 3DGS problem, offering a complementary strategy to the coarse-to-fine scheduling discussed earlier for improving computational efficiency. The Levenberg-Marquardt (LM) algorithm has emerged as a particularly promising approach. The 3DGS-LM framework replaces the Adam optimizer with a tailored LM method, enabling efficient GPU-parallelized computation of update directions from multiple image subsets. This approach achieves a 20% speedup over the original 3DGS while maintaining identical reconstruction quality, and is agnostic to other acceleration methods, thus enabling further compound speedups [157]. A further advancement, 3DGS²-TR, proposes a scalable second-order trust-region method that approximates curvature using only the diagonal of the Hessian matrix, computed efficiently via Hutchinson's method. This fully matrix-free approach has the same \(O(n)\) computation and memory complexity as Adam, but achieves better reconstruction quality in 50% fewer iterations, with a peak GPU memory overhead of less than 1GB [158]. Another line of work exploits the significant sparsity in the 3DGS Jacobian—since each Gaussian affects only a limited number of pixels—to develop a matrix-free, GPU-parallelized LM optimizer with Conjugate Gradient (CG). This method, which also introduces sampling strategies for camera views and loss functions, achieves roughly a 4x speedup over standard LM and approximately 5x over Adam when the Gaussian count is low, while using 3.5x less memory than 3DGS-LM [159]. These developments reflect a broader trend in deep learning toward quasi-Newton and Hessian-free methods. For instance, symmetric rank-one quasi-Newton approaches with adaptive regularized cubics allow for indefinite Hessian approximations, enabling the exploitation of directions of negative curvature [160]. Hessian-free methods using block-diagonal approximations of the generalized Gauss-Newton matrix have also shown better convergence and generalization compared to standard approaches [161].

**Trust-Region and Learned Optimizers**

Trust-region methods have demonstrated strong performance in non-convex optimization, particularly for ill-conditioned problems [162]. In the context of deep learning, stochastic trust-region and cubic regularization methods have been shown to escape saddle points and exhibit robust convergence to second-order stationary points [163]. Sub-sampled variants of these methods reduce computational overhead while retaining strong convergence guarantees [164]. For 3DGS, the parameter-wise trust-region technique based on the squared Hellinger distance, as used in 3DGS²-TR, regularizes updates to Gaussian parameters, ensuring stable optimization despite the strong non-linearity of the rasterization process [158]. In parallel, learned optimizers offer a compelling alternative, especially in the context of the learned initialization strategies discussed in the previous subsection. Unlike conventional optimizers, learned optimizers can predict correlated updates informed by inter-parameter and inter-Gaussian dependencies. The Learn2Splat framework introduces a learned optimizer that avoids degradation over extended optimization horizons by using a checkpoint buffer and an optimizer rollout strategy, and it demonstrates improved early novel view synthesis quality with zero-shot generalization to unseen reconstruction settings [147]. Furthermore, optimization-aware training frameworks like ForeSplat have been developed to ensure that the predicted initializations from feed-forward 3DGS models are explicitly designed for rapid, effective refinement by downstream optimizers. At its core is a lightweight multi-anchor meta-gradient training rule that bypasses costly higher-order differentiation, enabling high-quality reconstruction within seconds after a few refinement steps [165]. This concept is extended by Diff3R, which explicitly bridges feed-forward prediction and test-time optimization by incorporating a differentiable 3DGS optimization layer into the training loop, computing gradients via the Implicit Function Theorem and a scalable, matrix-free PCG solver, while simultaneously predicting uncertainty to mitigate overfitting [166].

**Hybrid and Adaptive Optimizer Designs**

Given the complementary strengths and weaknesses of first- and second-order methods, hybrid designs have become increasingly common, often motivated by the same instability concerns that drive the densification and pruning refinements in the next subsection. For example, dynamic momentum and learning rate adjustments, as seen in DWMGrad, allow optimizers to adapt to changing environments and task complexities [167]. The use of projected gradients combined with adaptive moment estimation, as in PadamP, accelerates training and improves generalization for unconstrained non-convex problems [146]. The observation that loss functions often transition from non-convexity to local convexity near optima has led to two-phase algorithms that detect the transition point and switch from Adam to conjugate gradient methods, yielding improved convergence and accuracy [168]. In the realm of second-order optimization, many practical implementations remain computationally prohibitive due to large matrix inversions. Techniques that approximate the Fisher information matrix using a diagonal representation, integrated with an adaptive trust-region mechanism, reduce the complexity from \(O(n^2)\) to \(O(n)\) and demonstrate faster, more stable convergence compared to Adam [169]. Similarly, the efficient computation of matrix square roots and the use of low-rank approximations in full-matrix preconditioning methods have made them more practical for large-scale problems [170]. The Frequent Directions sketch can be used to maintain low-rank matrix preconditioners, reducing memory and compute requirements while matching the regret guarantees of full-matrix adaptive methods up to an additive error in the bottom eigenvalues [171]. In summary, the landscape of 3DGS optimization is evolving rapidly, moving from general-purpose first-order methods toward specialized, second-order, and learned optimizers that exploit the unique structure of the Gaussian representation to achieve faster convergence, lower memory footprints, and higher reconstruction fidelity. These advances in the optimizer itself, when combined with the initialization and scheduling techniques of the previous subsection, set the stage for the targeted strategies to mitigate over-reconstruction and degenerate convergence that are examined next.

### 3.4 Mitigating Over-Reconstruction and Degenerate Convergence

The optimization of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) is fundamentally a non-convex, high-dimensional problem. As discussed in the previous subsection, the choice of optimizer—whether first-order, second-order, or learned—largely dictates the efficiency and trajectory of parameter convergence. However, even with a well-designed optimizer, standard training pipelines remain susceptible to a range of training pathologies that can trap the optimization in poor local minima, leading to visible artifacts and degraded rendering quality. These pathologies include over-reconstruction, persistent floater artifacts, unstable densification cycles, and overfitting in under-constrained settings. This subsection surveys the key strategies developed to diagnose and mitigate these issues, ensuring more stable and robust convergence for 3DGS models. Where the previous subsection focused on the optimizer as the primary driver of convergence, the techniques discussed here act as complementary safeguards, targeting the structural and statistical failure modes that optimizers alone cannot address.

One of the most fundamental challenges is the implicit gradient bias that causes 3DGS optimization to converge to suboptimal local solutions, a phenomenon characterized as the "Blur Trap" [172]. This trap is rooted in the optimization formulation, where the gradient signal for large Gaussians can vanish when a pseudo-equilibrium is reached, preventing further refinement. To escape these sharp minima, simple explicit exploration operators, such as random seeding to mitigate the "Far-Side Blur Trap" and random splitting to address the "Near-Side Blur Trap," have been proposed [172]. This finding highlights that deterministic densification strategies are insufficient and that stochastic exploration is crucial for navigating the complex loss landscape.

A closely related problem is over-reconstruction, where regions with high-frequency details become blurry. This issue is often traced back to a flaw in the adaptive density control strategy, where "gradient collision" prevents large Gaussians in over-reconstructed regions from being split [118]. To overcome this, novel densification criteria have been introduced, such as the homodirectional view-space positional gradient, which more effectively identifies and splits under-split Gaussians [118]. Other approaches refine the densification process itself, for instance, by using a pixel-aware gradient that considers the number of pixels a Gaussian covers, prompting the growth of large Gaussians that are observable from many viewpoints [173]. Furthermore, techniques like Edge-Aware Score for selecting split candidates, a Long-Axis Split strategy to reduce geometric distortions, and Recovery-Aware Pruning to remove overfitted Gaussians have been shown to collectively enhance rendering fidelity [42]. The use of an opacity-gradient-based densification trigger, paired with a more conservative pruning schedule, has also proven effective in preventing destructive optimization cycles and promoting more compact reconstructions in few-shot scenarios [120].

The instability of the densification process itself is another major source of degenerate convergence. Standard 3DGS often suffers from "stubborn floater" artifacts whose root cause is the vanishing opacity gradients when a floater's color blends into a canceling error with the background. To resolve this, a Dual Opacity architecture has been proposed, which decouples geometric regularization from final appearance rendering, allowing a "Geometric Regularization Path" to bear strong depth-based constraints and an "Appearance Refinement Path" to focus on high-fidelity details [174]. Similarly, when a converged model is poorly parameterized due to high-opacity floaters that attenuate gradients to true surfaces, an equivalent distribution reorganization technique has been suggested. This method resamples Gaussian centers from the existing set, estimates local anisotropic covariances, and re-initializes low opacity, effectively rebuilding the visibility structure to improve gradient accessibility and reduce parameter coupling [136]. Another strategy proposes localizing point management (LPM) based on multiview geometry constraints and rendering errors to identify error-contributing zones for densification and opacity reset, which is a versatile plugin for both static and dynamic models [123].

Sparse-view settings are particularly prone to overfitting, where the model memorizes training views and produces artifacts like blurring and floaters in novel views. A significant body of work addresses this through regularization, often drawing inspiration from dropout. Early approaches like DropoutGS proposed a Random Dropout Regularization (RDR) to benefit from the lower overfitting of smaller models, combined with an Edge-guided Splitting Strategy (ESS) to recover high-frequency details [175]. However, a more recent framework, PairDropGS, identifies that random dropout can lead to inconsistencies between different dropped subsets, causing unstable reconstruction. It introduces a Paired Dropout-induced Consistency Regularization (PairDropGS) that constructs pairs of dropped subsets and applies a low-frequency consistency regularization between them, along with a progressive scheduling strategy for stability [176]. Another perspective on this issue frames the core challenge as the unobservability of Gaussian primitive reliability. The DOC-GS framework models this via a Continuous Depth-Guided Dropout (CDGD) strategy and uses a Dark Channel Prior (DCP) from the observation domain to identify and prune low-confidence, haze-like Gaussians [177]. To further stabilize the optimization, some methods use Fisher Information to provide principled guidance for both prior-based augmentation and dropout regularization, actively selecting the most informative supporting views and adapting the removal probability of each Gaussian [178].

Beyond regularization, efficient training strategies have also been developed to mitigate overfitting and promote convergence. For instance, an alternating densification framework interleaves high-densification phases to capture details with low-densification phases that focus on aggressive opacity pruning and geometry regularization through pseudo-view consistency [179]. The initialization of Gaussians is also critical; a dense initialization from triangulated pixels eliminates the need for incremental densification, bypassing a lengthy optimization path and reducing the chance of converging to a poor local optima [138]. During the refinement phase after densification, significant redundancy in backward computation has been observed. A view-adaptive backward gating mechanism called SkipGS selectively skips backward passes for views whose losses have plateaued, reducing post-densification training time by 42% without sacrificing quality [180].

Finally, co-regularization approaches offer a complementary means to identify and suppress inaccurate reconstruction. By training two 3D Gaussian radiance fields simultaneously and observing their point and rendering disagreements, one can predict and identify inaccurate regions, which can then be pruned or regularized [181]. For in-the-wild scenes, where transient distractors and appearance inconsistencies generate conflicting cross-view gradients, a conflict-aware approach harmonizes gradients from different views by rotating them into an orthogonal configuration, reducing directional interference and leading to more stable optimization [182].

In summary, mitigating over-reconstruction and degenerate convergence in 3DGS requires a multi-faceted approach that complements the optimizer-level strategies discussed previously. It involves refining the densification and pruning criteria to be more sensitive to detail and artifacts, introducing explicit exploration and stochastic perturbation to escape local traps, and applying sophisticated regularization techniques, often guided by uncertainty, consistency, or geometric priors, to counter overfitting in data-sparse scenarios. The strategies discussed here are not mutually exclusive and are often combined to create robust, efficient, and high-fidelity 3D reconstruction systems.

## 4 Geometry Reconstruction and Surface Extraction

### 4.1 Surface Regularization and Geometric Priors for Gaussian Alignment

The raw optimization of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) is driven predominantly by photometric reconstruction loss. While this approach yields impressive novel-view synthesis, it frequently leads to geometrically inaccurate reconstructions. The explicit, point-based nature of Gaussians provides no inherent surface constraint; the optimizer is free to place primitives anywhere that minimizes the rendering error, which can result in non-physical configurations. A significant body of research has therefore emerged to introduce geometric priors and regularization terms that steer the optimization process toward a more accurate alignment of Gaussian primitives with the underlying scene surfaces. These techniques address a range of artifacts, from the formation of "needle-like" splats to the presence of floating, semi-transparent debris ("floaters"), which plague naive 3DGS reconstructions. This line of work forms the foundation for the subsequent development of more sophisticated hybrid representations, which couple explicit Gaussians with implicit surface models to achieve globally coherent geometry.

A primary source of geometric guidance comes from monocular depth and normal estimators. Off-the-shelf models provide dense priors that can be used to supervise the geometry of the reconstructed scene. However, these priors are not without their challenges; they suffer from scale ambiguity, cross-view inconsistency, and local inaccuracies, as highlighted by research into reliable monocular supervision [183; 184]. A naive application of these priors as hard ground-truth can actually degrade the reconstruction. To address this, a common strategy is to distill confidence or uncertainty from the monocular predictions and use this to weight the geometric loss, preventing the propagation of inaccurate depth values into well-constrained regions [183; 184; 185]. Similar confidence-based filtering approaches are proposed in systems designed for indoor reconstruction, where supervision is adaptively applied by comparing the consistency of prior estimates during optimization to identify and filter out low-quality depth and normal information [186].

Beyond simple depth supervision, higher-order geometric constraints have proven powerful. The effective rank of a Gaussian's covariance matrix is a revealing statistic; many optimized Gaussians degrade into anisotropic "needle-like" shapes, which are suboptimal for surface representation [9]. This phenomenon is directly linked to the geometry of the splats themselves, as an extreme anisotropy with one dominant variance contributes little to surface fidelity and can produce inaccurate normals. To combat this, the effective rank has been introduced as a regularization term, discouraging extreme anisotropy and promoting more balanced, disk-like forms that better align with flat surfaces [9; 187]. This regularization has been shown to not only reduce needle-like artifacts but also to improve the quality of reconstructed normals and geometry across various 3DGS variants [9]. Such per-primitive shape control is a critical step toward making individual Gaussians more surface-compliant, yet it remains a local constraint; the next logical step is to couple these regularized primitives with a global surface representation.

The integration of explicit surface representations with the Gaussian cloud presents another avenue for enforcing global geometric coherence. By coupling Gaussian primitives with a neural Signed Distance Function (SDF), optimization can be guided by a continuous surface prior. The SDF is regularized with an Eikonal loss to maintain proper distance properties, which in turn provides a global context that guides the Gaussians toward a consistent and coherent surface, addressing issues of local misalignment [187; 188]. This hybrid methodology has demonstrated significant improvements in generating watertight and topologically consistent meshes compared to using Gaussians alone [188]. This same philosophy—leveraging an external global structure to correct local Gaussian behavior—extends beyond SDFs to other forms of scene-level priors, such as multiview geometry and planar constraints.

Multiview consistency serves as a crucial, self-supervised signal for correcting geometric errors. The principle is that points on the true surface should be consistently visible and accurately reconstructed across different viewpoints. However, relying on depth-based reprojection to infer visibility can create a circular dependency where visibility estimation requires accurate depth, and depth supervision is conditioned on visibility [189]. To break this cycle, more recent approaches model visibility explicitly at the level of Gaussian primitives rather than pixels. By aggregating cross-view visibility of the same Gaussian, one can construct reliable supervision over co-visible regions, mitigating the ambiguity and improving surface reconstruction accuracy [189]. Other methods leverage multi-view stereo (MVS) priors that are robust in regions with significant color variation, complementing the strengths of 3DGS near object boundaries and low-texture regions to produce a more reliable overall geometry [190]. This complementary nature of priors is also exploited in dynamic scenes, where the distinct advantages of monocular and multi-view geometric depths are combined for more robust 4D reconstruction [191].

Stereoscopic priors offer a powerful alternative to monocular cues, providing absolute scale and binocular consistency that monocular methods inherently lack. By constructing virtual stereo pairs during optimization and using a foundation stereo model, methods like StereoGS enforce stronger geometric constraints, leading to more accurate geometries in sparse-view settings [192]. This is in contrast to scale-agnostic monocular supervision, which can lead to defective geometry due to its inherent ambiguity [192]. This approach can be further augmented with a dense initialization strategy that uses zero-shot multi-view depth estimation to anchor primitives to accurate scene surfaces [192].

Planar priors are particularly effective in man-made environments like indoor and urban scenes, where surfaces are often dominated by flat regions. By leveraging off-the-shelf segmentation and normal prediction models, systems like GSPlane can extract robust planar priors to structure the representation of Gaussian coordinates on these planes, enforcing geometric consistency during training [193]. Such methods advocate for representing scenes using planes as fundamental primitives, which is beneficial not only for geometric accuracy but also for downstream applications like scene editing and physical simulation [193].

Finally, a key challenge in applying external priors is dealing with their inherent unreliability, especially in real-world scenarios where geometric information, such as LiDAR data, is sparse and unevenly distributed. Instead of enforcing geometry as a hard constraint, recent methods propose modeling the reliability of these priors. This is achieved by representing partially observable geometry as an energy field, where the confidence of the prior guides the optimization process, providing soft guidance that steers the Gaussians without directly restricting the solution space. This prevents potentially detrimental constraints from harming the reconstruction in areas where the prior is untrustworthy [194]. This philosophy of treating geometric priors as informative but uncertain signals, rather than ground truth, is a central theme in creating robust and geometrically accurate 3DGS frameworks. Collectively, these regularization strategies—ranging from local shape control to global surface coupling—establish the critical groundwork for the hybrid Gaussian-SDF approaches that follow, which seek to unify the strengths of explicit and implicit representations for high-fidelity surface reconstruction.

### 4.2 Hybrid Gaussian-SDF Representations for Implicit Surface Reconstruction

The discrete, explicit nature of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) provides exceptional rendering efficiency and quality but introduces significant challenges for recovering accurate, continuous 3D geometry. The absence of an implicit global field means that surfaces extracted from raw Gaussians are often fragmented, noisy, and riddled with floaters, limiting direct mesh generation. To bridge this gap, a prominent line of research integrates implicit Signed Distance Fields (SDFs) with the explicit Gaussian representation, creating hybrid frameworks that aim to combine the rendering strengths of 3DGS with the geometric robustness of implicit surfaces. These hybrids enforce global surface continuity, improve watertight mesh extraction, and enable accurate reconstruction across diverse indoor, outdoor, and reflective scenarios. This paradigm directly extends the regularization strategies discussed in the previous subsection, moving from external geometric priors as supervision toward a fully coupled, differentiable joint representation where the SDF serves as an internal, learnable prior that continuously guides Gaussian placement and shape.

A foundational approach in this domain involves linking Gaussian primitives to an SDF through a differentiable SDF-to-opacity transformation. The 3DGSR method introduces a transformation function that converts SDF values into Gaussian opacities, enabling joint optimization of the SDF and the Gaussians within a unified framework [195]. While optimization of the Gaussians provides supervisory signals for the SDF, these signals are sparse and limited to locations occupied by discrete Gaussians. To address this, 3DGSR incorporates volumetric rendering and aligns the rendered geometric attributes—depth and normal—with those derived from the Gaussian splatting, providing dense supervision for learning a continuous SDF and eliminating redundant surfaces outside the Gaussian sampling range [195]. Similarly, a line of work attaches a discretely sampled SDF value to each Gaussian, linking it to opacity via an SDF-to-opacity transformation to avoid the computational cost of ray marching [196]. This strategy introduces a projection-based consistency loss that projects Gaussians onto the zero-level set of the SDF, ensuring alignment between the discrete samples and the underlying continuous surface [196]. Another hybrid technique, GSurf, integrates SDFs into the splatting pipeline, leveraging the continuous nature of SDFs to regularize Gaussian primitives, which effectively suppresses noise and fills geometric holes, resulting in compact and high-quality surfaces [197]. Furthermore, DiGS directly embeds SDF learning into the 3DGS pipeline by associating each Gaussian with a learnable SDF value, which aligns primitives with underlying geometry and improves cross-view consistency, complemented by a geometry-guided grid growth strategy for dense coverage [94].

A common strategy to enhance the mutual benefits of both representations is to employ bidirectional guidance between the Gaussian and SDF branches. For instance, GSDF proposes a dual-branch architecture where the efficient 3DGS representation and the neural SDF leverage and enhance each other's strengths through mutual guidance and joint supervision [198]. In the context of reflective objects, GS-ROR² guides 3DGS and SDF bidirectionally: an SDF-aided Gaussian splatting improves the efficiency of relighting optimization through mutual depth and normal supervision, while a GS-guided SDF refinement enhances the quality of the final mesh [199]. This approach also introduces an SDF-aware pruning strategy to remove Gaussian outliers that are distant from the SDF-defined surface, mitigating floater artifacts [199]. A similar bidirectional philosophy is seen in GaussianRoom, which integrates neural SDFs with 3DGS for indoor scenes. The SDF guides the densification and pruning of Gaussians, allowing for accurate modeling even with poor point cloud initialization, while the Gaussians improve SDF efficiency by piloting its point sampling. Additionally, it introduces normal and edge priors to resolve geometric ambiguities in textureless areas [200]. In dynamic urban environments, leveraging 2D priors for depth and point tracking, coupled with an SDF representation for dynamic objects, has been shown to enhance geometric accuracy and enable editing tasks such as scene decomposition and composition [201]. A more tightly coupled approach aligns Gaussians directly onto the SDF's zero-level set. This technique, termed neural pulling, dynamically positions 3D Gaussians on the surface defined by the SDF; the aligned Gaussians are then rendered through differentiable rasterization, and the neural SDF is updated by pulling neighboring space towards these aligned Gaussians. This joint optimization of Gaussians and SDF, guided by both RGB and geometry constraints, progressively refines the signed distance field near the surface, achieving accurate and complete reconstructions [202]. To improve the surface alignment of individual Gaussians, ARGS builds upon SuGaR by introducing an effective rank regularization that discourages extreme anisotropy in Gaussian shapes, promoting more stable "disk-like" forms, and integrating a neural SDF regularized with an Eikonal loss to provide a continuous global surface prior that guides Gaussians toward better geometric alignment [187].

In complex scenes with strong lighting or specular highlights, hybrid Gaussian-SDF representations are particularly valuable. The GS-Octree method combines an octree-based implicit surface representation with Gaussian splatting. In this approach, a coarse SDF is first reconstructed via volume rendering and encoded in a low-resolution octree. 3D Gaussians are then introduced as additional degrees of freedom, guided by the SDF, to recover finer geometric details, and the refined SDF in turn optimizes the Gaussians further [203]. To improve robustness, MonoGSDF couples Gaussian primitives with a neural SDF, where the SDF guides the Gaussians' spatial distribution during training, and the Gaussians serve as priors for surface reconstruction at inference, eliminating the need for memory-intensive Marching Cubes. A multi-resolution training scheme and monocular geometric cues further enhance the reconstruction quality [188]. A related framework, Gaussian-Voxel Duet, uses a sparse voxel scaffold that is jointly optimized with scaffold-anchored Gaussians, confining them to a narrow band around surfaces defined by voxelized SDFs, thus improving representation efficiency and condensing floaters while maintaining fast convergence [95]. For large-scale urban scenes, the GSSR method has also shown the potential for accurate geometric precision by distributing Gaussians evenly along latent surfaces, guided by per-Gaussian single-view normal consistency and multi-view photometric consistency, along with a surface-aligned optimization objective [204].

The integration of depth and normal priors is a critical component in stabilizing the joint optimization of Gaussians and SDFs. SplatSDF accelerates the convergence of an SDF-NeRF by fusing a pre-trained 3DGS model at the architectural level, injecting neural embeddings of the Gaussians into the SDF-NeRF around the object surface [205]. This method demonstrates faster convergence to a given geometric accuracy compared to baselines, showcasing a practical use of 3DGS as a geometric prior. Another approach, GSDF, which uses depth and normal alignment between the rendered Gaussians and the SDF-derived geometry, has shown state-of-the-art performance in both rendering and reconstruction, validating the effectiveness of geometric consistency regularizers [198]. Furthermore, methods like GLS jointly optimize surface reconstruction and open-vocabulary segmentation, where a surface normal prior and depth error optimization are used to improve the geometric quality of the Gaussian field [206]. The EnerGS framework takes a different approach by treating partial geometric priors from LiDAR as a continuous energy field that provides soft guidance for Gaussian optimization, rather than a hard constraint, thus steering the optimization towards geometrically plausible solutions [194].

Hybrid Gaussian-SDF representations also offer strong advantages in robotics and SLAM applications, where both dense reconstruction and rendering are required. GS-SDF exploits the geometry from a trained SDF to initialize Gaussians physically and provide comprehensive geometric regularization, achieving superior reconstruction accuracy and rendering quality across different trajectories [207]. Similarly, in the context of robotic navigation, frameworks that parameterize Gaussian elements to directly recover a distance field, such as SplatlessDF, demonstrate that Gaussian-style representations can be used not only for photometric rendering but also as a continuous mapping representation suitable for downstream tasks [208]. The CarGS framework learns an adaptive contribution for Gaussian primitives, squeezing the knowledge from geometry regularization into a compact MLP, which improves the mutual learning between rendering and reconstruction tasks in a unified model [209]. Finally, methods that leverage normal information extracted from an SDF to regularize Gaussian splatting have been shown to improve rendering quality while also facilitating direct mesh extraction [210]. Collectively, these works demonstrate that the integration of SDFs within the Gaussian splatting framework is a powerful paradigm for overcoming the geometric limitations of explicit representations, establishing a trajectory towards high-fidelity 3D reconstruction that is both accurate and computationally efficient. This hybrid paradigm directly sets the stage for the geometry-aware rendering and meshing techniques discussed in the following subsection, which further refine how continuous fields are extracted and optimized for direct, high-quality surface generation from these coupled representations.

### 4.3 Geometry-Aware Rendering and Differentiable Rasterization for Surface Extraction

The efficacy of surface extraction from 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) is fundamentally contingent upon the design of the rendering pipeline and the geometric interpretation of its primitives. While the standard alpha-blending pipeline of 3DGS is engineered for photorealistic appearance, it does not inherently provide a mechanism for querying a continuous geometric field, such as a signed distance or occupancy function. This subsection reviews the key advances in geometry-aware rendering that bridge this gap, enabling the extraction of accurate, watertight, and high-fidelity meshes, and thereby complementing the implicit SDF-based hybrids discussed earlier. These innovations span from modifying the shading and attenuation models to developing novel, differentiable rasterization strategies and associated mesh extraction algorithms.

A central challenge in deriving geometry from 3DGS is that the standard Gaussian kernel and its blend of opacity, color, and depth do not naturally align with a well-defined surface. To address this, several works have re-engineered the rendering process to be geometry-aware. One prominent approach involves deriving an analytical occupancy field directly from the Gaussian primitives. For instance, [22] introduces a principled occupancy field for Gaussian Splatting by incorporating a learnable oriented normal for each Gaussian and modifying the attenuation formulation. This yields closed-form expressions for the normal and occupancy at arbitrary spatial locations, providing a continuous geometric field over which an isosurface can be extracted. Similarly, [211] defines a GOF derived from ray-tracing-based volume rendering of 3D Gaussians. This formulation enables direct geometry extraction by identifying the level-set of the opacity field, bypassing the need for error-prone post-processing steps like Poisson reconstruction or TSDF fusion. The key is that these methods move beyond the simple aggregation of depth maps to establish a coherent, global, and differentiable volumetric field based on the Gaussian distribution.

The precision of the extracted geometry is also heavily influenced by the shading and normal models used during rendering, especially for scenes with complex lighting and view-dependent effects. Methods that rely on opaque Gaussians often struggle to separate a surface's albedo from its lighting-induced appearance, leading to inaccurate normal estimation and flawed geometry. To mitigate this, [212] proposes a self-supervised confidence framework that dynamically balances photometric and geometric supervision, along with losses that penalize per-primitive color and normal variance. This approach improves surface extraction by reducing ambiguity in areas with abundant view-dependent effects. Another notable direction is the integration of physically-based shading into the rasterization pipeline. [213] explicitly integrates normals into the rendering equation by re-parameterizing surface colors as a product of normals and an Integrated Directional Illumination Vector (IDIV), thereby ensuring that the optimized normals are directly linked to the rendering process and not merely an auxiliary output. This direct coupling, exemplified by [214], is crucial for ensuring that geometric attributes and visual appearance are jointly optimized without conflict, which is particularly important for robust reconstruction of highly reflective surfaces.

Beyond the shading model, the differentiable rasterization step itself is a focal point for innovation. The standard per-pixel alpha-blending of sorted Gaussians is an approximation that can produce artifacts at object boundaries and is not a direct measure of a surface intersection. To overcome these limitations, [215] introduces hierarchical resorting and a robust formulation of Gaussian depth that aligns better with the level-set, in conjunction with a parallelized Marching Tetrahedra algorithm tailored to their opacity formulation. This improves both the accuracy of the extracted surface and the speed of the meshing step. Furthermore, the emergence of order-independent transparency (OIT) techniques offers a significant advantage for surface extraction in complex scenes. Unlike depth-sorted methods, which can be prone to errors with overlapping primitives, OIT rendering as seen in [32] provides a more robust foundation for computing and reasoning about depth ordering, potentially leading to more stable and consistent geometric inference. The challenge of reconstructing semi-transparent surfaces is a testament to the need for more sophisticated rendering strategies. For instance, [216] moves away from the single-depth-per-pixel assumption, uniformly sampling transmittance to model a multi-modal distribution of opacity and depth. This probabilistic approach is critical for accurately reconstructing surfaces where multiple layers are visible, such as laboratory glassware, an issue that standard depth-accumulation methods fundamentally cannot resolve. Relatedly, [71] disentangles each Gaussian's geometric occupancy from its optical opacity to create a unified surface-volume representation. This factorization captures high-frequency reflections as surfaces while preserving clear transmission through the volume, and their hybrid renderer, augmented with specular-aware gradient gating, enables robust reconstruction of scenes with complex reflections.

Finally, the ultimate goal of geometry-aware rendering is to facilitate direct and high-quality mesh generation, thereby bridging the gap between the discrete Gaussian representation and the continuous surface outputs required by downstream applications. The strategies for extracting the final surface are diverse and have evolved significantly. Early and still common approaches treat rendered depth maps as geometry proxies and fuse them using Truncated Signed Distance Functions (TSDF). Methods like [186] refine this process by developing scale-aware meshing strategies inspired by TSDF and octree-based isosurface extraction to recover finer details. Others have proposed using the Gaussian primitives' points as a dense point cloud to be processed by Marching Cubes. For example, [217] demonstrates that with its optimized normal calculation and depth-guided densification, direct mesh extraction from the dense Gaussian point cloud using Marching Cubes becomes a viable and high-fidelity option. However, the most advanced methods establish a continuous field from the Gaussians prior to meshing. [22] introduces Primal Adaptive Meshing for Region-of-Interest meshing at arbitrary resolutions from their continuous occupancy field, allowing for the recovery of thin structures like bicycle spokes. Similarly, [193] leverages the optimized planar priors to refine mesh layouts, significantly improving topological structure and reducing vertex counts, showcasing that integration of structural cues can directly enhance the meshing output. For scenarios requiring topology-adaptive optimization, methodologies such as [218] estimate a continuous opacity field for differentiable element pruning to construct a unified, topologically coherent tetrahedral mesh, streamlining downstream physical simulations. When viewed together, these approaches illustrate a clear progression from geometric proxy fusion toward direct, field-based isosurface extraction, enabled by increasingly geometry-aware rendering and rasterization pipelines that are deeply integrated with the final meshing stage. This progression not only addresses the limitations of raw Gaussian geometry but also establishes a foundation for the domain-specific adaptations and system-level integrations explored in the following subsection, wherein these geometry-aware rendering and meshing techniques are tailored to large-scale, dynamic, and real-time applications.

### 4.4 Domain-Specific Geometry Reconstruction and System Integration

Domain-specific adaptations of Gaussian-based surface reconstruction have become essential for translating general-purpose methods into practical systems that operate under real-world constraints. While the geometry-aware rendering and meshing techniques discussed in the preceding subsection establish a robust foundation for extracting high-fidelity surfaces in controlled settings, their direct application to large-scale outdoor environments, dynamic scenes, and sensor-constrained platforms often requires substantial architectural and algorithmic modifications. This subsection examines these domain-specific extensions, focusing on large-scale urban and road surface modeling, dynamic object reconstruction via deformation fields, sparse-view and feed-forward pipelines leveraging multi-modal priors, and system-level integration with SLAM and real-time frameworks.

**Large-Scale Urban and Road Surface Reconstruction.** Urban and road environments present unique challenges for surface reconstruction, including elongated camera trajectories, sparse and occluded observations, textureless regions, and the need for scalable memory management—conditions that push the limits of the field-based isosurface extraction methods previously described. Several methods have been developed to address these issues. For instance, [219] introduces a hybrid implicit-explicit approach that decomposes unbounded street views into close-range, distant, and sky regions with aligned cuboid boundaries, using cuboid hash-grids and road-surface initialization to achieve fine-grained reconstruction without LiDAR. Building on the explicit advantages of Gaussian primitives, [220] employs a planar-based octree representation with segmented training to reduce memory costs and handle long, narrow camera trajectories, augmented by guided smoothing and dual-step matching strategies for depth accuracy and multi-scale robustness. [221] and [222] both propose placing 2D Gaussian surfels on a uniform meshgrid, which aligns with the thin-surface property of roads, reduces primitive overlap, and significantly accelerates reconstruction. To further respect structural semantics, [221] incorporates a road-structure-aware adaptive meshgrid that allocates denser surfels to complex regions such as lane markings and boundaries, alongside a trajectory-consistency-guided pose-refinement strategy. Complementing these geometric approaches, [223] integrates segmentation-guided video inpainting and semantic-aware color correction to handle occlusions from dynamic agents and static obstacles, producing visually coherent and geometrically faithful road surfaces. For large-scale scene completeness, [224] proposes a hybrid Gaussian representation that models different scene parts (e.g., near, far, sky) with distinct Gaussian properties, achieving complete reconstruction beyond LiDAR coverage while using 66% of the Gaussians and a 20% faster reconstruction speed. Similarly, [225] introduces depth-consistent normal regularization and spatially adaptive pruning to balance geometric accuracy and memory efficiency, while [226] leverages a distributed 2D Gaussian representation, structured dense enhancement from SfM priors, and progressive hybrid geometric optimization for stable reconstruction across complex urban scenes. For city-scale digital twins, [227] combines textured proxy geometry with residual Gaussians, enabling real-time rendering on mobile GPUs with significantly reduced memory and energy consumption. Meanwhile, feed-forward approaches like [228] demonstrate that geometric foundation models can predict dense Gaussian attributes directly from multi-view images and pose/depth observations, eliminating per-scene optimization and enabling scalable road surface mapping. Similarly, [229] predicts geometry and texture parameters from a single frame in a bird's-eye-view paradigm, achieving real-time road reconstruction with elevation errors reduced to 1.73 cm.

**Dynamic and Articulated Object Reconstruction.** Extending Gaussian surface reconstruction to dynamic scenes requires modeling temporal deformations while preserving the surface fidelity that geometry-aware rendering pipelines strive to maintain. [230] tackles this by incorporating Gaussian features from 4D neural voxels into planar-based Gaussian splatting, combined with normal regularization and an as-rigid-as-possible constraint to maintain local rigidity and smooth surfaces across timesteps. For articulated objects, [231] introduces a category-agnostic framework that infers optimal sparse viewpoints via a Gaussian information field, constrains Gaussians to planar primitives for accurate normals and depth, and leverages a Vision-Language Model for open-vocabulary part segmentation and joint parameter estimation. In the context of urban dynamics, [232] distinguishes static and potentially dynamic regions using 2D semantic maps, applying global consistency for definite static areas and learnable time embeddings for dynamic objects, thereby preserving static content while capturing deformations. Another approach, [233], injects task-aware semantic priorities into the pipeline, employing semantically guided pruning and priority-driven rendering to allocate resources to safety-critical objects while maintaining over 350 FPS. [234] further addresses dynamic scenes in highway settings by enabling both image and LiDAR rendering, with improvements in reconstruction through LiDAR supervision in sparse-view and monotone-background environments.

**Sparse-View and Feed-Forward Reconstruction with Multi-Modal Priors.** In many practical settings, dense multi-view observations are unavailable, necessitating sparse-view or feed-forward approaches that bypass the per-scene optimization typical of the methods discussed earlier. [139] accelerates large-scale indoor reconstruction by learning data-driven priors for Gaussian initialization and densification, achieving an 8x runtime speedup and a 48% reduction in depth errors compared to state-of-the-art methods. In the autonomous driving domain, [235] formulates Gaussian prediction as a joint learning framework of visual and sparse LiDAR depth features, with multi-modal feature matching and multi-scale decoding for zero-shot novel-view synthesis. [236] reformulates generalizable dynamic reconstruction as an iterative process of optimization and densification, using self-supervised policies and geometric regularization to improve fidelity with fewer primitives. For surround-view reconstruction, [237] distills geometric priors from a pre-trained VGGT model into a lightweight geometry branch, uses a Gaussian head to fuse multi-scale tokens, and jointly supervises semantic refinement, significantly improving novel-view quality on nuScenes. [238] moves beyond per-pixel paradigms by unifying volume-based and pixel-based Gaussian prediction across three branches (close-range static, dynamic actors, far-field), ensuring stable 4D reconstruction despite noisy motion priors. [239] employs a controllable LiDAR generation model conditioned on coarsely extrapolated rendering, producing geometry-consistent scans for expansive reconstruction and improving extrapolative novel view synthesis. [240] initializes dense point clouds via multi-view metric depth predictions, refines them with a progressive pruning strategy, and jointly optimizes Gaussian geometry with diffusion-prior-enhanced dense depths, outperforming methods that use ground-truth LiDAR. [241] integrates a multi-modal video diffusion model with 3DGS, synthesizing RGB, depth, and semantic outputs for novel viewpoints to provide comprehensive supervision and robustness under extreme viewpoint changes.

**System Integration with SLAM and Real-Time Pipelines.** The integration of Gaussian-based surface reconstruction into SLAM systems has enabled simultaneous tracking, mapping, and photorealistic rendering in real time, thereby operationalizing the continuous-field and meshing strategies from the previous subsection within an online, resource-constrained framework. [242] proposes a real-time system with robust sparse-to-dense tracking and geometry-aware Gaussian surfel mapping, achieving a surface reconstruction error of 0.6 cm on Replica and ScanNet++ while running at 24 FPS. [243] leverages feed-forward visual geometry models for camera and scene priors, jointly optimizing poses and map with photometric and geometric losses, and achieves superior rendering and tracking accuracy online. Conversely, [244] demonstrates that geometry-only Gaussians can reconstruct scene geometry without appearance modeling, reducing per-primitive parameters by over 80% and enhancing robustness to illumination variations. For large-scale outdoor environments, [245] introduces a decoupled system with sparse anchor grids, geometry-based flow-densification, and scene partitioning, achieving a 15% improvement in reconstruction quality while maintaining real-time performance. [246] integrates dense SLAM with adaptive densification and geometry-guided optimization, achieving state-of-the-art PSNR, SSIM, and LPIPS on Replica and TUM-RGBD. [247] extends Gaussian SLAM to multi-camera setups with multi-camera bundle adjustment and scale consistency, enabling reconstruction of side-view regions that monocular setups miss. [248] proposes a hybrid Gaussian-SDF representation that combines a colorized SDF for smooth geometry with 3D Gaussians for details, achieving over 150 FPS on real-world sequences—an order-of-magnitude improvement over existing methods. [249] combines NeRF priors with 3DGS, using sparse keyframes to densely populate maps and fill gaps, while integrating loop closure and bundle adjustment for improved tracking. In the LiDAR-centric domain, [250] tightly couples LiDAR-inertial-visual data with surface Gaussian scenes, while [251] introduces plane-constrained multimodal Gaussian Mixture Models for continuous geometric supervision, improving accuracy by 52.6% over LiDAR-based and 68.7% over Gaussian-based methods. [252] directly aligns sparse LiDAR data with continuous Gaussian maps, achieving 7.98 FPS novel view synthesis and demonstrating cross-modal localization potential. Finally, [253] and [254] illustrate system-level integration where Gaussian-based reconstructions are incorporated into broader multi-modal pipelines for dynamic urban scenes and configurable sensor fusion. Collectively, these domain-specific adaptations underscore the importance of tailoring Gaussian representations to application constraints, ensuring that geometric accuracy, training efficiency, and rendering quality are balanced for practical deployment across robotics, autonomous driving, and large-scale scene understanding—thereby extending the geometry-aware principles established earlier into operational, real-world systems.

## 5 Robustness and Handling Imperfect and Sparse Inputs

### 5.1 Sparse-View and Few-Shot Reconstruction

Sparse-view and few-shot reconstruction represents one of the most challenging and practically significant frontiers in 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) research. The fundamental difficulty stems from the ill-posed nature of the problem: with only a handful of input images, the geometric and appearance information required to constrain the optimization is severely insufficient, leading to overfitting, structural artifacts, and poor novel-view generalization [255]. The foundational 3DGS pipeline relies on dense multi-view inputs and Structure-from-Motion (SfM) point clouds for initialization, both of which degrade catastrophically under sparse-view conditions—SfM often fails to produce sufficient reliable feature matches, resulting in sparse or incomplete initial point clouds that propagate errors throughout the optimization [256; 257]. Building upon the pose-estimation challenges discussed in the previous section, sparse-view reconstruction introduces an additional layer of difficulty: even when camera poses are known, the sheer lack of visual overlap and geometric redundancy renders the optimization severely under-constrained. Consequently, the research community has developed a diverse arsenal of strategies that can be broadly categorized into robust initialization methods, depth and stereo prior injection, and advanced regularization techniques aimed at mitigating overfitting while suppressing characteristic artifacts such as floaters and background collapse [258]. These categories are deeply interconnected with the pose-free and feed-forward paradigms, as many of the learning-based initialization tools introduced for SfM-free reconstruction also serve as powerful priors for sparse-view scenarios.

**Robust Initialization Strategies.** The quality of the initial point cloud is widely recognized as the decisive factor for sparse-view 3DGS performance. Controlled ablations have demonstrated that initialization determines the attainable performance band, while training-time constraints provide only modest improvements within that band [137]. Since SfM inevitably struggles with sparse inputs, a prominent line of work replaces or supplements it with learning-based alternatives, echoing the feed-forward approaches highlighted in the pose-free reconstruction paradigm. One approach leverages DUSt3R, a vision foundation model, to generate dense and non-redundant Gaussian point clouds during initialization, effectively overcoming the limitations of traditional SfM under sparse-view constraints [256]. Similarly, the π³ network, a reference-free point cloud estimation network, has been integrated into pipelines to provide dense initialization without requiring reliable reference views, combined with uncertainty-guided depth supervision, normal consistency losses, and depth warping regularization to handle geometric inaccuracies [259]. Another strategy proposes eliminating densification entirely by using triangulated pixels from dense image correspondences to estimate scene geometry in a single step, providing each Gaussian with well-informed color, scale, and position while achieving uniform detail coverage across the scene [138]. Segmentation-driven approaches offer a complementary direction: by leveraging region-based segmentation to identify structurally significant regions, these methods selectively downsample dense point clouds, reducing Gaussian counts by up to 50% while preserving scene fidelity and substantially lowering memory footprints [260]. For extreme cases where SfM fails entirely, SfM-free frameworks jointly estimate camera poses and reconstruct scenes by using dense stereo modules to progressively recover pose information and build global dense point clouds, supplemented by coherent view interpolation modules that generate viewpoint-consistent content as additional supervision [261]. Additionally, frequency-aware SfM techniques that augment low-texture regions via low-frequency view augmentation and relaxed multi-view correspondences, combined with 3DGS self-initialization that lifts photometric supervision into additional points, have proven effective in compensating SfM-sparse regions [137]. This tight coupling between robust initialization and pose estimation underscores the importance of addressing both problems jointly, as the accuracy of one directly impacts the feasibility of the other.

**Depth and Stereo Priors for Geometric Supervision.** Geometric priors, particularly monocular depth estimates and stereo-derived cues, have become indispensable for constraining the ill-posed sparse-view optimization. These priors serve as a form of external geometric knowledge that compensates for the missing multi-view redundancy, and they resonate closely with the dense correspondence and trajectory-based methods discussed in the pose-free section. Early methods such as SparseGS incorporate depth priors with novel depth rendering techniques and pruning heuristics to mitigate floater artifacts, alongside unseen viewpoint regularization modules to alleviate background collapse [258]. The integration of stereo geometry-texture alignment has been shown to bridge rendering quality and geometry estimation, jointly enhancing both surface reconstruction and novel view synthesis, while pseudo-feature-enhanced geometry consistency enforces multi-view consistency by incorporating both training and unseen views [262]. For large-scale outdoor scenes, geometric supervision from LiDAR or monocular depth is often spatially incomplete and uneven; energy-based frameworks model partially observable geometry as a continuous energy field induced by geometric evidence, providing soft geometric guidance that steers optimization without directly restricting the solution space [194]. Depth-based regularization has also been enhanced through confidence-aware mechanisms that leverage multi-cue confidence maps from monocular depth estimators and sparse SfM depth to adaptively adjust supervision during optimization, improving geometric detail preservation and convergence stability [263]. The depth-and-density guided dropout strategy addresses two distinct failure modes in sparse-view settings: overfitting in regions with excessive Gaussian density near the camera, and underfitting in distant areas with insufficient coverage, using adaptive masking and distance-aware fidelity enhancement modules [264]. A particularly promising direction combines stereo priors for robust initialization with diffusion-based refinement, where stereo priors aid in recovering camera poses and reliable point clouds, and diffusion priors preserve intricate scene details during optimization [257]. Additionally, hierarchical depth-guided frameworks employ cascade Pearson correlation losses to progressively refine geometry from coarse to fine scales, enforcing multi-scale depth consistency that improves structural fidelity [265]. Probabilistic joint flow-depth optimization frameworks further leverage the complementarity between optical flow and depth through pixel-level mechanisms that scale information fusion based on matching probability, combined with multi-view depth-consistency losses that suppress misleading gradients in uncertain areas [266]. These approaches collectively illustrate how dense geometric cues not only enable pose-free reconstruction but also provide the robust supervision needed to stabilize sparse-view optimization.

**Regularization Techniques for Overfitting Mitigation.** A rich body of work addresses the overfitting problem through sophisticated regularization schemes that impose structural, statistical, or frequency-domain constraints on the Gaussian representation. The unobservability of Gaussian primitive reliability has been identified as a core challenge, motivating dual-domain observation and calibration frameworks that model reliability through the synergy of optimization-domain inductive bias and observation-domain evidence; these employ continuous depth-guided dropout strategies as explicit proxies for primitive reliability, combined with dark channel priors as structural consistency cues to identify and accumulate anomalous regions for reliability-driven geometric pruning [177]. Fisher information provides a principled foundation for guiding both the utilization of geometric priors and regularization in sparse-view settings. By leveraging Fisher information to actively select the most informative supporting views and quantitatively measuring the uncertainty of each Gaussian to adaptively adjust removal probabilities, these methods reduce randomness in augmentation and improve stability and rendering fidelity [178]. Structured Gaussian representations that enforce spatial coherence have proven effective, including supergaussian groupings of Gaussians based on local geometric cues and appearance features, with inter-group global self-attention and sparse local attention enabling the integration of global and local spatial information [267]. Similarly, coherent Gaussian representations constrained through implicit convolutional decoders and total variation losses, supported by monocular depth-based initialization, prevent Gaussians from moving independently during optimization [268]. Loop-based frameworks introduce progressive Gaussian initialization that iteratively densifies the initial point cloud using rendered pseudo-images, combined with depth-alignment regularization that leverages both reliable SfM depth and window-based dense monocular depth for precise geometric supervision [269]. Multimodal-prior-guided importance sampling fuses photometric rendering residuals, semantic priors, and geometric priors to produce robust local recoverability estimates that directly drive where fine Gaussians should be injected, enabling hierarchical coarse-to-fine representations that selectively refine geometrically critical regions [270]. Signal-structure-aware scheduling frameworks address the mismatch between supervising low-frequency sparse points with high-frequency images by deriving the average sampling frequency and bandwidth of 3D representations and regulating training image resolution and densification processes based on scene frequency convergence, achieving frequency-consistent and floater-free training in large-scale scenes [140]. Finally, comprehensive surveys of sparse-view reconstruction emphasize that while geometry-based, neural implicit, and generative (diffusion-based) methods each offer distinct trade-offs between reconstruction accuracy, efficiency, and generalization, the persistent challenges of domain generalization and pose-free reconstruction remain open, pointing toward the development of 3D-native generative priors and real-time unconstrained sparse-view reconstruction as key future directions [255]. These directions align directly with the feed-forward and learning-based pose-estimation trends highlighted in the following subsection, suggesting that the convergence of robust pose estimation, sparse-view reconstruction, and generative modeling will define the next generation of 3DGS systems.

### 5.2 Pose-Free and SfM-Free Pipelines

The reliance of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) on accurate camera poses, typically derived from Structure-from-Motion (SfM) pre-processing, constitutes a critical bottleneck that limits its application to casual, unconstrained, and large-scale real-world data. Classical SfM pipelines are notoriously fragile in the presence of textureless regions, repetitive patterns, large viewpoint changes, or significant object motion, often leading to catastrophic failure or pose drift. This fragility is further exacerbated under the sparse-view conditions discussed in the previous subsection, where the limited visual overlap provides insufficient feature matches for reliable pose estimation, creating a compounding failure mode: without trustworthy poses, the already ill-posed sparse-view optimization becomes intractable. To overcome this dependency, a significant body of research has emerged to eliminate the need for a separate, offline SfM stage, giving rise to pose-free and SfM-free pipelines that jointly estimate camera poses and scene geometry. Notably, many of the learning-based initialization tools developed in these pose-free paradigms serve a dual purpose: they not only enable SfM-free reconstruction but also provide robust priors for sparse-view scenarios, forging a tight coupling between pose estimation and sparse-view reconstruction. These methods can be broadly categorized into joint optimization frameworks, dense correspondence-based approaches, and feed-forward or learning-based paradigms, each offering distinct trade-offs between robustness, accuracy, and computational efficiency that become particularly consequential when deployed under the sparse-view and degraded-imaging conditions examined in this survey.

A foundational class of pose-free methods formulates the problem as a joint, end-to-end optimization where camera poses are treated as learnable parameters alongside the scene representation. This approach has been widely explored in the context of neural rendering and extends naturally to 3DGS, enabling the simultaneous refinement of camera poses and Gaussian parameters from a purely photometric loss.
