For instance, in the context of dynamic scenes, a unified optimization framework that incorporates camera poses as learnable parameters complementary to 3DGS attributes has been proposed, where both camera and object motion are recast as per-primitive SE(3) affine transformations on 3D Gaussians [271]. This design allows for the joint refinement of poses and scene structure, which is particularly beneficial when motion blur compromises the accuracy of pre-estimated poses—a scenario directly connected to the degraded-imaging challenges detailed in the following subsection. Such joint optimization strategies are conceptually rooted in the classical analysis-by-synthesis paradigm, which casts the inference of 3D structure and camera poses as a single optimization problem aimed at explaining the observed pixels. This classical framework has been modernized with expressive neural field representations and gradient-based pose refinement, and recent instantiations that explicitly incorporate generative priors for novel view synthesis have proven effective in sparse-view settings [272]. This highlights that even in the absence of reliable initial poses, a well-initialized and regularized joint optimization can converge to accurate solutions, a property that becomes especially valuable when SfM fails entirely, such as in low-texture or heavily blurred inputs.

While joint optimization approaches are promising, their performance is often contingent on good initialization. To mitigate this, a second category of methods leverages dense geometric cues, such as optical flow and point trajectories, as a more robust alternative to sparse keypoint matching. The use of dense correspondence is a powerful strategy for pose-free reconstruction, especially in dynamic scenes where classical SfM methods fail. One prominent line of work exploits long-range point trajectories, which are optimized and then classified into static and dynamic parts using a specifically designed neural network architecture; the static trajectories are then used for robust bundle adjustment and camera pose estimation [ParticleSfM  Exploiting Dense Point Trajectories for Localizing Moving
  Cameras in the Wild]. This trajectory-based approach is more resilient to dynamic objects than sparse keypoint matching and offers a strong foundation for pose-free 3DGS. The concept of leveraging dense visual correspondence as a substitute for explicit pose estimation has been further advanced by the rise of "tracking-any-point" and even dense tracking models. Building on these foundations, a reconstruction framework has been introduced that makes cross-frame 3D correspondence an explicit prediction by estimating camera-coordinate 3D trajectories, coupling sparse trajectories, per-frame local point maps, and relative camera poses with geometric consistency objectives to ensure coherent global alignment [273]. By explicitly avoiding the need for accurate initial poses, these methods provide a robust alternative for deriving camera motion directly from dense pixel-level relationships. This dense-correspondence paradigm resonates strongly with the depth and stereo priors discussed in the previous subsection, as both strategies leverage dense geometric cues to compensate for the absence of reliable sparse feature matches, underscoring a broader theme of replacing fragile sparse correspondences with dense, learnable geometric signals.

The most disruptive shift in pose-free reconstruction, however, has come from the emergence of large-scale, feed-forward 3D foundation models. Unlike traditional iterative pipelines, these models are trained to directly regress camera poses and dense geometry from a set of images in a single forward pass. This paradigm, popularized by models like DUSt3R, has led to a new family of SfM systems that are orders of magnitude faster and more robust than their classical counterparts, particularly in textureless and low-overlap scenarios [274]. A prominent example is the MASt3R-SfM pipeline, which leverages a foundation model to robustly produce local 3D reconstructions and accurate matches, using a low-memory approach to align these local reconstructions into a global coordinate system, and even serving as an efficient image retriever to reduce complexity [275]. However, these feed-forward models are not perfect; they often generate incorrect correspondences for non-overlapping image pairs, which can degrade the final pose estimation. To address this, the G-MASt3R-SfM pipeline introduces a graph-based view pruning module to geometrically prune outlier views and a multi-stage optimization module that refines camera parameters progressively from local to global consistency [276]. Furthermore, a fully differentiable deep pipeline, VGGSfM, has been proposed where every component—from point tracking to bundle adjustment—is differentiable, enabling end-to-end training of the entire SfM process [277]. The robustness of these feed-forward models to challenging imaging conditions—such as extreme motion blur, as seen in methods like GeMS [278]—demonstrates a direct synergy with the degraded-imaging challenges explored in the next subsection, where learning-based pose estimation often serves as the only viable initialization strategy.

These feed-forward models have also inspired hybrid strategies that combine the robustness of learning-based initialization with the precision of classical optimization. A key insight is that while feedforward approaches excel in challenging conditions, they often fall short of classical methods in standard settings; therefore, a systematic combination of both strengths has been proposed to achieve state-of-the-art results across a wide range of scenarios [279]. Similarly, the "Benchmarking Efficient & Effective Camera Pose Estimation Strategies" study demonstrates that using feed-forward networks to obtain initial estimates and refining them with classical SfM techniques leads to the best efficiency-effectiveness trade-off [280]. This suggests a growing consensus that the integration of learning-based initialization with geometric refinement is the most practical pathway for robust, pose-free reconstruction. This is particularly relevant for 3DGS, where the quality of the final reconstruction is highly sensitive to the accuracy of the camera poses used for optimization—a sensitivity that is amplified in sparse-view settings, where the optimization has little redundancy to absorb pose errors, and in degraded-imaging scenarios, where the photometric loss itself is unreliable.

Finally, a key challenge in pose-free pipelines is handling long video sequences, where small per-frame errors can accumulate into significant pose drift. To ensure global consistency over hundreds of frames, a novel system has been developed that combines the strong sequential constraints of SLAM with the flexibility and global optimization of offline SfM, treating temporal ordering as a first-class citizen for reliable loop closure and augmenting global optimization with metric monocular depth priors [281]. In the context of dynamic scenes, a large-scale reconstruction framework has also been proposed that processes long videos in overlapping chunks, connects chunk-local coordinate systems through confidence-weighted registration, and associates dynamic identities across chunk boundaries to recover coherent long-range motion [282]. These system-level approaches are crucial for scaling pose-free pipelines to real-world, casual video capture, where the lack of a pre-computed SfM model means that pose estimation and scene reconstruction must be performed jointly and robustly over time. The ability to maintain global consistency without an initial pose estimate is a testament to the maturity of these methods and their suitability for powering next-generation 3DGS applications, particularly as they must contend with the temporal variations in illumination and transient distractors discussed in the following subsection. Collectively, pose-free reconstruction represents a fundamental paradigm shift in 3DGS, transforming the pipeline from a two-stage process dependent on robust offline preprocessing into a unified, joint optimization that is inherently more resilient to the imperfections of real-world capture. The convergence of joint optimization, dense correspondence, and feed-forward foundation models is not only enabling SfM-free reconstruction but also providing the robust initialization and geometric priors that are indispensable for sparse-view reconstruction—and, as the next subsection will demonstrate, for handling degraded imaging and adverse conditions.

### 5.3 Robustness to Degraded Imaging and Adverse Conditions

### 5.3 Robustness to Degraded Imaging and Adverse Conditions

While the previous subsection addressed the challenge of unknown or inaccurate camera poses, a complementary and equally critical challenge for deploying 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) in the wild is the degradation of the input imagery itself. In real-world deployment, 3DGS is frequently confronted with input imagery that deviates significantly from the idealized sharp, well-exposed, and static conditions assumed by its core optimization pipeline. Robustness to degraded imaging and adverse conditions is therefore a critical requirement for practical applications ranging from handheld phone captures to autonomous driving. Research in this area addresses a spectrum of challenges, including motion blur, low-light and high-dynamic-range (HDR) scenes, adverse weather, noise, and the presence of transient distractors that violate the multi-view consistency assumption central to 3DGS.

**Motion Blur and Event-Camera Assistance.** Motion blur, often caused by camera shake or object movement during the exposure time, is one of the most pervasive forms of degradation. Early approaches attempted to model the blur process directly within the 3DGS optimization. For instance, a common paradigm involves modeling the camera trajectory during exposure using continuous neural ordinary differential equations (ODEs) and applying rigid body transformations to the Gaussians to synthesize a blurred image, as demonstrated in CoMoGaussian [283]. Similarly, CRiM-GS extends this concept with an adaptive distortion-aware transformation to account for nonlinear effects like rolling shutter [284]. Another approach, BSGS, employs a bi-stage strategy that first refines camera poses and then applies a global rigid transformation to correct residual motion-induced distortions [285]. However, these methods often struggle with severe blur, where conventional pose estimation like COLMAP fails, leading to unreliable initialization. GeMS addresses this by leveraging a deep learning-based SfM pipeline (VGGSfM) and 3DGS-MCMC for more robust scene initialization directly from extremely blurred inputs [278]. This connection to learning-based pose estimation echoes the feed-forward paradigms discussed in the previous subsection, highlighting how advances in pose-free reconstruction directly bolster robustness to degraded inputs.

Event cameras, with their microsecond temporal resolution and high dynamic range, have emerged as a powerful complement to standard frame-based cameras for handling motion blur. These sensors capture per-pixel brightness changes asynchronously, providing the temporal information lost during exposure integration. A significant body of work integrates event streams into the 3DGS framework. EBAD-Gaussian constructs a blur formation model by synthesizing multiple latent sharp images during the exposure time and uses the event-based double integral (EDI) prior to supervise intensity changes, leading to sharper reconstructions [286]. DiET-GS builds on this by using a two-stage training strategy with event double integral constraints and a diffusion prior to further enhance edge details [287]. EvFlow-GS proposes an end-to-end learnable double integral (LDI) and a novel event-residual prior to strengthen supervision, while jointly optimizing camera poses and 3DGS [288]. The joint optimization of poses and scene in these methods also aligns with the pose-free optimization strategies surveyed earlier, demonstrating a convergence of solutions across different robustness challenges.

Addressing the practical limitations of existing event-based methods, AsyncEvGS introduces a flexible, high-resolution asynchronous RGB-Event dual-camera system, reconstructing sharp images from event data and using a cross-domain pose estimation module for robust 3DGS initialization [289]. JADE-GS takes a different perspective by jointly allocating deblurring evidence from two complementary priors—the analytical EDI inversion and a learned restoration network—using a lightweight Spatial Prior Router to fuse them into a supervision target, achieving leading perceptual quality without generative decoding at inference [290]. For dynamic scenes, BARD-GS explicitly decomposes motion blur into separate camera and object motion components, enabling robust reconstruction from handheld monocular captures [291], while Deblur4DGS extends this concept to 4D reconstruction from blurry monocular video by estimating exposure time and using variable canonical Gaussians [292]. UPGS further proposes a unified pipeline where camera poses are learnable parameters optimized jointly with 3DGS attributes using a three-stage training schedule [271].

**Low-Light, Exposure, and Illumination Robustness.** Low-light conditions present a distinct set of challenges, including high noise, significant information loss, and color distortion. Direct application of 3DGS to such inputs often fails due to the limited number of SfM points and the ambiguity of the optimization. LITA-GS tackles this with an illumination-agnostic novel view synthesis method that extracts an illumination-invariant physical prior, develops a lighting-agnostic structure rendering strategy, and includes a progressive denoising module to achieve normally-exposed representations without reference images [293]. The reliance on physical priors here parallels the integration of geometric priors discussed in the following subsection, illustrating a broader trend of using domain knowledge to regularize ill-posed inverse problems.

To handle the combination of low-light and motion blur, FLED-GS proposes a fast framework that reformulates 3D scene restoration as an alternating cycle of enhancement and reconstruction, using intermediate brightness anchors for progressive recovery and a noise-aware 3DGS reconstruction to suppress noise [294]. The use of event cameras is also explored for this challenging scenario. Dark-EvGS is a framework specifically designed for event-assisted 3DGS in low-light environments, introducing triplet-level supervision and a color tone matching block to ensure color consistency in the reconstructed bright frames [295]. E-3DGS leverages a transmittance adjustment device at the hardware level to capture both motion and exposure events, enabling high-quality reconstruction in conditions where frame-based cameras fail [296]. Thermal imaging provides another avenue for robustness; DTGS couples Retinex-inspired illumination decomposition with thermal-guided 3DGS, where the thermal supervisory branch stabilizes both color restoration and geometry learning under extreme low-light conditions [297]. This multi-modal fusion strategy foreshadows the LiDAR- and radar-augmented approaches detailed in the next subsection, where auxiliary sensors provide complementary cues that are robust to the failure modes of RGB-only pipelines.

Beyond low-light, the varying exposure and tone mapping across different cameras in a multi-camera setup, common in urban reconstruction, can also degrade 3DGS quality. P2GS addresses this by jointly decomposing a view-invariant linear HDR radiance field along with per-view exposure scales and tone-mapping functions, grounded in the physical image-formation process, to ensure photometric consistency [298].

**Adverse Weather and Transient Distractors.** Outdoor scenes are often subject to adverse weather conditions like rain, snow, and haze, as well as transient objects such as pedestrians or vehicles that violate the static scene assumption. WeatherGS explicitly categorizes multi-weather artifacts into dense particles (snowflakes, raindrops) and lens occlusions, proposing a dense-to-sparse preprocessing strategy to remove them and a mask-based training procedure to reconstruct a clear scene [299]. For transient distractors, a wide range of methods has been developed to identify and suppress their influence. A common strategy is to use semantic cues from vision-language models to identify distractors. For example, a semantic filtering framework accumulates CLIP similarity scores per Gaussian and applies opacity regularization and periodic pruning to remove transient objects [300].

Another line of work focuses on modeling the transient elements explicitly with additional learnable components, such as ForestSplats, which introduces a deformable transient field and a superpixel-aware mask to separate static scenes from distractors without heavy vision foundation models [301]. To address the circular dependency between accurate transient detection and clean reconstruction, DualSplat converts first-pass reconstruction failures into explicit priors, using photometric residuals, feature mismatches, and SAM2 instance boundaries to construct object-level pseudo-masks and guide a clean second-pass optimization [302]. More advanced optimization-based techniques, such as 3DGS-HPC, combine a patch-wise classification strategy with a hybrid photometric and perceptual metric to more reliably separate static and transient regions [303]. HarmoGS addresses conflicting cross-view gradients caused by transient distractors and illumination inconsistencies through a dual-view Conflict-Aware Gradient Harmonization strategy, which reconciles view-specific gradients by rotating them into an orthogonal configuration [182]. With a focus on large-scale unconstrained environments, GS-Diff leverages a multi-view diffusion model to guide the 3DGS optimization, transforming under-constrained reconstruction problems into well-posed ones and integrating appearance embedding, depth priors, and dynamic object modeling to handle both transients and appearance variability [304]. Finally, for photo collections with significant appearance variability, Wild-GS decouples the appearance of each Gaussian into material attributes, global illumination, and per-image camera properties, explicitly aligning pixel appearance features to 3D space using triplanes extracted from reference images [305].

Collectively, these methods demonstrate a clear trend toward integrating physical priors, complementary sensor modalities (e.g., events, thermal), and powerful generative priors to build 3DGS systems that are robust to the imperfections and variability of real-world captures. Moreover, the strategies surveyed here—particularly the use of auxiliary sensors and pre-trained priors—establish a natural bridge to the next subsection, which explores how multi-modal sensor fusion and geometric priors from LiDAR, radar, and foundation models further enhance the geometric fidelity and metric accuracy of 3DGS reconstructions. Together, these two dimensions of robustness—degraded imaging and geometric grounding—form a comprehensive toolkit for deploying 3DGS in challenging real-world scenarios.

### 5.4 Multi-Modal Sensor Fusion and Geometric Priors

The reconstruction of accurate and robust 3D scenes from imperfect or sparse inputs is fundamentally an ill-posed problem, and the integration of auxiliary sensor data and pre-trained geometric models has emerged as one of the most effective strategies to address this challenge. In the context of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS), multi-modal sensor fusion and geometric priors serve a dual purpose: they provide metric-scale anchors that mitigate the inherent scale ambiguity of monocular pipelines, and they supply dense or structured supervision that regularizes the optimization of Gaussian primitives in under-constrained regions. While the previous subsection examined robustness to degraded image quality—where complementary sensors like event cameras and thermal imaging were introduced to compensate for RGB failures—this subsection extends that multi-modal philosophy by systematically leveraging range sensors and geometric priors to anchor the metric scale and structural fidelity of Gaussian reconstructions. This subsection surveys the landscape of techniques that leverage LiDAR, depth sensors, radar, and foundation model priors to enhance the robustness and geometric fidelity of 3DGS-based reconstruction.

A primary avenue of research involves the use of LiDAR and depth-sensor-derived supervision to anchor the metric scale and geometry of Gaussian scenes. A fundamental insight is that monocular depth foundation models, while providing dense and coherent relative geometry, often lack reliable metric scale [306; 307]. Conversely, LiDAR provides precise but sparse metric measurements. Several approaches have been developed to bridge this gap, and notably, these strategies directly complement the deblurring and low-light methods discussed earlier—where RGB-only reconstruction may fail, LiDAR-anchored geometry provides a stable scaffold that is invariant to photometric degradation. For instance, the GTLR-GS framework explicitly incorporates metric geometric priors into the entire Gaussian optimization process, treating LiDAR not as a passive initialization source but as a geometry-conditioned allocation and refinement problem. It introduces a geometry-texture-aware allocation strategy and a confidence-aware metric depth regularization that anchors the reconstructed geometry to absolute scale, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance on metric-scale reconstruction [308]. Similarly, the Geometry-Preserving in 3D Gaussian Splatting for LiDAR-Camera Extrinsic Calibration work addresses a critical issue in using 3DGS as a geometric proxy for calibration: since 3DGS is designed for novel view synthesis, it tends to prioritize rendering quality, causing the proxy geometry to drift from the true LiDAR structure. Their solution aggregates multi-view LiDAR observations for dense depth supervision while blocking photometric gradients from updating Gaussian spatial parameters, thus preserving metric geometry and improving calibration accuracy [309]. Another approach, EnerGS, tackles the challenge of partial geometric priors in outdoor scenes. Recognizing that LiDAR measurements are often spatially incomplete and uneven, EnerGS models partially observable geometry as a continuous energy field, providing soft geometric guidance to the optimization of Gaussian primitives rather than enforcing geometry as a hard constraint [194]. The Targetless LiDAR-Camera Calibration with Neural Gaussian Splatting method also exemplifies this fusion, jointly optimizing sensor poses with a neural Gaussian-based scene representation, freezing reliable LiDAR points as anchor Gaussians to preserve global structure, and using auxiliary Gaussians to prevent local overfitting [310].

Beyond LiDAR, the fusion of other range sensors such as radar has been explored to improve depth completion and scene reconstruction, particularly in conditions where cameras and LiDAR are unreliable—echoing the adverse weather robustness challenges surveyed previously. The RaCalNet framework proposes a novel paradigm that eliminates the need for dense supervision by using sparse LiDAR to supervise the learning of refined radar measurements, achieving a supervision density of merely around 1% compared to dense-supervised methods. It uses a Radar Recalibration module for point screening and displacement refinement, followed by a Metric Depth Optimization module that fuses radar priors with monocular depth predictions for metrically accurate outputs [311]. Similarly, the work on Sparse Beats Dense explores why sparse supervision can outperform dense supervision in radar-camera depth completion. The authors identify the Projection Transformation Collapse phenomenon, where sparse supervision leads the model to learn collapsed projection transformations between Image/Radar/LiDAR spaces, and propose a Disruption-Compensation framework to handle this collapse while leveraging 3D spatial and 2D semantic information [312]. Additionally, the "Sparse-LiDAR Prompting of Monocular Geometry Foundations" study introduces SLIM, the first adaptation of a point-map foundation model (MoGe-2) to accept truly sparse LiDAR input. By integrating a partial-convolution sparse encoder with a multi-scale fusion neck, SLIM demonstrates substantial improvements in absolute relative error at long-range distances (50-150m), highlighting the value of geometric priors in extreme sparsity scenarios [313].

Multi-modal registration and calibration form another critical pillar of this research area, as accurate extrinsic parameters are a prerequisite for effective sensor fusion. This concern directly parallels the pose-estimation challenges addressed in the robustness-to-pose-errors subsection, yet here the emphasis shifts from recovering camera trajectories from degraded imagery to aligning complementary sensor modalities that jointly constrain the scene. Many targetless calibration methods have been developed, often leveraging differentiable scene representations like 3DGS. The UniCalib work reformulates calibration as an intra-modality depth flow estimation problem, estimating a dense depth map from the camera image and completing the sparse LiDAR projected depth map, while introducing a reliability map to prioritize valid pixels [314]. The iterative surrogate diffusion framework demonstrates a versatile approach to enhance any calibration method without architectural modifications, using the original method as a denoiser to iteratively refine extrinsic estimates, showing improvements in accuracy and robustness across multiple state-of-the-art baselines [315]. In a related vein, the Native-Domain Cross-Attention for Camera-LiDAR Extrinsic Calibration work addresses the limitations of projecting LiDAR points into 2D depth maps, which distorts 3D geometry, by directly aligning image patches and LiDAR point groups in their native domains, injecting extrinsic hypotheses into the correspondence modeling process for geometry-consistent interaction [316]. Furthermore, the DST-Calib network introduces a dual-path, self-supervised calibration framework with double-sided data augmentation to enhance generalization, demonstrating that cross-modal feature association can be improved through difference map construction instead of traditional dual-branch feature extraction [317]. The simultaneous estimation of spatial and temporal parameters is addressed by SST-Calib, which uses segmentation masks for pixel-wise bidirectional loss and incorporates velocity information from optical flow for temporal parameter estimation [318].

The fusion of diffusion or vision-language model priors represents a frontier for geometric and appearance regularization in under-constrained scenarios, and it naturally bridges the generative-prior strategies used for deblurring and transient-object removal with the geometric grounding goals of this subsection. The Veila framework, for instance, introduces a conditional diffusion framework for panoramic LiDAR generation from monocular RGB images, integrating a Confidence-Aware Conditioning Mechanism to strengthen RGB conditioning and a Geometric Cross-Modal Alignment for robust alignment under noisy diffusion [319]. The AURORA-KITTI benchmark introduces a distillation-based baseline that leverages depth foundation models to inject clean structural priors into in-the-wild depth completion training, demonstrating that weather-aware, physically consistent data contributes more to robustness than architectural modifications alone [320]. The COPS framework for thermal-LiDAR depth completion uses contrastive learning and pseudo-supervision, leveraging a depth foundation model to enforce a depth-aware contrastive loss that sharpens depth boundaries and provides dense depth priors to mitigate incomplete supervision in adverse weather [321]. The "Depth Anything with Any Prior" framework presents Prior Depth Anything, which combines incomplete but precise metric information from depth measurements with relative but complete geometric structures from depth prediction. It introduces pixel-level metric alignment and distance-aware weighting to pre-fill diverse metric priors, followed by a conditioned monocular depth estimation model to refine prior noise and implicitly merge complementary depth sources [322]. This is complemented by the AnchorD method, which proposes a training-free depth grounding framework that anchors monocular depth estimation priors from a foundation model in raw sensor depth through factor graph optimization, performing patch-wise affine alignment to locally ground monocular predictions in metric depth while preserving geometric detail [323]. Finally, the Prompting Depth Anything framework introduces the new paradigm of prompting depth foundation models with low-cost LiDAR, guiding the Depth Anything model for accurate metric depth output up to 4K resolution, achieving state-of-the-art results on ARKitScenes and ScanNet++ [307]. Together, these works demonstrate that the strategic integration of multi-modal sensor data and pre-trained geometric models is essential for overcoming the inherent limitations of 3DGS in sparse, degraded, and under-constrained real-world scenarios, and they collectively establish a geometric grounding layer that complements the photometric robustness achieved by the methods in the previous subsection.

## 6 Dynamic Scenes, 4D Reconstruction, and Temporal Modeling

### 6.1 Temporal Representation Paradigms: Canonical Deformation Fields, Per-Frame Models, and Explicit 4D Primitives

The extension of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) to dynamic scenes represents one of the most active and consequential research frontiers in modern computer graphics and vision. Building upon the static representation foundations established in earlier sections, the central challenge here lies in augmenting a static, explicit representation with a temporal dimension while preserving the real-time rendering capabilities and high visual fidelity that have made 3DGS the de facto standard for static novel view synthesis. A survey of the literature reveals that approaches to this problem can be broadly categorized into three foundational paradigms: structure-guided methods that rely on canonical deformation fields, per-frame or per-primitive temporal models, and native 4D primitive designs that treat time as an intrinsic spatial dimension. Each paradigm embodies distinct trade-offs among reconstruction fidelity, rendering speed, storage requirements, and the ability to represent complex or abrupt motions, and recent work has increasingly sought to hybridize these strategies to exploit their complementary strengths. The choice of paradigm also fundamentally shapes the downstream motion modeling strategies discussed in the next section, determining whether motion is learned implicitly through deformation networks, explicitly through per-primitive dynamics, or inherently through the slicing of higher-dimensional primitives.

**Structure-Guided Paradigms: Canonical Deformation Fields and Embeddings.** The most widely adopted paradigm for dynamic 3DGS is the structure-guided approach, which maintains a canonical set of 3D Gaussians in a reference space and learns a deformation field that maps these canonical primitives to each observation frame. The seminal formulation of this idea, exemplified by deformable Gaussian frameworks, established a template in which a coordinate-based neural network takes a canonical position and a time index as input and outputs per-Gaussian deformations [324]. This design inherits the compactness of a single canonical representation while leveraging the expressiveness of neural fields for motion modeling. However, subsequent studies have identified a critical architectural weakness: modeling deformation purely as a function of coordinates is conceptually mismatched with the mixture-of-fields nature of 3DGS. To address this, [325] introduce per-Gaussian embeddings as an additional input to the deformation network, effectively allowing each primitive to maintain a learnable identity that conditions its motion, along with a coarse-to-fine decomposition of temporal embeddings to separately capture slow and fast dynamics. This embedding-based strategy is further refined by [326], which accelerates rendering through early fusion of temporal embeddings and introduces depth- and error-guided sampling to improve robustness to sparse initializations, as well as opacity modulation to escape local optima in poorly lit regions.

Despite their elegance, deformation-based methods encounter persistent challenges. A primary issue is optimization difficulty in scenes with high-frequency or abrupt motions, where a single unified deformation network struggles to represent diverse motion patterns. [327] directly confronts this by proposing a motion-aware partitioning strategy that recursively duplicates deformation networks for temporally segmented groups of high-dynamic Gaussians while treating low-dynamic primitives as static, augmented by a cross-frame consistency loss to mitigate discontinuities. An alternative line of work addresses redundancy and computational efficiency. [328] introduces a deformable anchor grid, a hierarchical scene representation in which each anchor derives multiple Gaussians within a local spatiotemporal region, paired with a deformation-aware densification strategy that adaptively grows anchors only in under-reconstructed dynamic areas; this achieves an average 60% model size reduction and a 100% FPS improvement over state-of-the-art methods. Similarly, [329] proposes a global-to-local motion decomposition, employing Global Canonical Scaffolds with anchor deformation to model coarse motion and Local Canonical Scaffolds with explicit Gaussian deformation for fine corrections, together with temporal interval adjustment for optimal segment allocation, yielding approximately a 70% reduction in model size on real-world dynamic videos. In a related vein, [330] combines a hierarchical anchor scaffold with per-Gaussian deformation, using a static warm-up stage and a stop-gradient operation to stabilize canonical positions while enabling fine-grained local motion predictions; this framework achieves 36.98 dB PSNR on synthetic monocular benchmarks at 435.6 FPS with only 4.67 MB of storage. For long-range sequences, [331] offers a novel perspective by treating motion as continuous rigid-body transformations, integrating SE(3) transforms with hierarchical Bézier residuals and learnable local anchors, which preserves temporal identity and avoids the unbounded Gaussian proliferation characteristic of piecewise-linear or short-window approximations.

**Gaussian-Centric and Per-Frame Temporal Models.** A second paradigm shifts the locus of temporal modeling from a global deformation field to the individual primitives themselves. In these Gaussian-centric approaches, each Gaussian is endowed with an explicit time-dependent behavior, either through learnable temporal activation profiles or through per-primitive motion functions. The primary motivation is flexibility: because each primitive carries its own dynamics, the representation can adapt more readily to spatially heterogeneous motion and sudden changes. For instance, [332] proposes a representation in which Gaussian primitives are allowed to appear at arbitrary positions and times, rather than being anchored in a canonical space, and are each assigned a motion function that enables movement to neighboring regions over time, substantially improving the modeling of complex dynamic scenes. The concept of temporal lifespan has also emerged as a powerful tool. [333] introduces a learnable lifespan parameter that reforms temporal visibility into a flat-top profile, allowing primitives to remain consistently active over their duration; the lifespan simultaneously modulates primitive motion, decoupling motion magnitude from temporal duration and improving long-term stability without sacrificing dynamic fidelity. A grow-and-prune philosophy underlies several other works; [334] proposes temporal sensitivity pruning to remove low-impact Gaussians based on temporally aggregated sensitivity metrics, along with temporal sensitivity sampling to suppress floaters and improve coherence, delivering up to a 13.71× rendering speedup with 10× fewer primitives. Similarly, [335] identifies two causes of temporal redundancy—short-lifespan Gaussians and inactive Gaussians at render time—and introduces a Spatial-Temporal Variation Score to prune the former and an active mask to skip the latter, resulting in a 41× storage reduction and a 9× faster rasterization speed. Hybrid 3D-4D approaches have also been proposed to exploit static-dynamic separation; [336] begins with a fully 4D representation and iteratively converts temporally invariant Gaussians into static 3D primitives, significantly reducing parameters and accelerating training while maintaining high visual quality. Likewise, [337] employs radial basis function modeling with time-dependent RBFs for dynamic regions and shared temporally invariant parameters for static regions, achieving up to a 98% model size reduction and real-time rendering at 125 FPS at 4K resolution.

**Native 4D Primitives: Time as a Spatial Dimension.** A third, more radical paradigm treats a dynamic scene as an intrinsically 4D spatiotemporal volume, where Gaussian primitives are defined in XYZT space rather than in a canonical 3D space with a separate motion model. This approach is epitomized by [338], which introduces anisotropic 4D XYZT Gaussians. In this formulation, dynamics at each timestamp are obtained by temporally slicing the 4D Gaussians, naturally composing dynamic 3D Gaussians that can be projected into images via a tailored rendering routine. A key advantage is its capacity to handle abrupt motions and scene discontinuities that often confound deformation-based methods, and the implementation achieves real-time inference speeds of up to 277 FPS on an RTX 3090 GPU. A subsequent work by the same group, [339], extends the representation with 4D spherindrical harmonics for view- and time-dependent appearance and introduces compact variants that substantially reduce the memory footprint, tackling the primary bottleneck of this paradigm. Building on the native 4D formulation, [340] makes strides in storage efficiency by decomposing color attributes into a three-parameter direct color component and a shared lightweight alternating-current color predictor, eliminating the need for high-dimensional spherical harmonic coefficients; combined with an entropy-constrained deformation technique and opacity-based entropy loss, this framework achieves approximately a 190× storage reduction on the Technicolor dataset and a 125× reduction on Neural 3D Video datasets while preserving comparable rendering speed and quality.

**Comparative Analysis and Emerging Synthesis.** Across these paradigms, a clear pattern of trade-offs emerges. Structure-guided methods that rely on canonical deformation fields generally yield superior reconstruction fidelity and compact model sizes, because the shared canonical space imposes a strong regularization that improves generalization across frames [341]. However, this fidelity comes at the cost of rendering speed and can be brittle when confronted with complex or abrupt motions. Gaussian-centric and per-frame models, by contrast, offer considerably higher rendering speeds and greater flexibility for locally heterogeneous motion, though they may suffer from quality variability and, in some cases, voluminous storage requirements [341]. Native 4D primitives provide a conceptually elegant and highly parallelizable framework, but historically have struggled with large model footprints due to the explosion of primitives in the temporal dimension. The most recent trends, however, point toward hybrid and adaptive designs that blur the boundaries between these categories. Multi-level competitive allocation frameworks, such as [342], deliberately distribute modeling capacity across three structured levels—static structure, persistent dynamic geometry, and transient appearance primitives—and use shared rasterization with residual-driven optimization to adaptively specialize each level, preserving long-term motion consistency while capturing fine dynamic detail. Dynamic-static decomposition strategies have also proven fruitful; [343] integrates a probabilistic dynamic perception coefficient directly into the Gaussian pipeline to adaptively separate static and dynamic components, enabling explicit motion learning for dynamic elements while maintaining geometric stability for static structures. Finally, [344] offers a plug-and-play module that learns spatio-temporal probability distributions for each Gaussian, disentangling spatial and temporal patterns to improve both reconstruction quality and spatio-temporal consistency when inserted into existing frameworks. Together, these works indicate that the field is moving toward unified, adaptive representations that can dynamically allocate resources based on scene content, promising robust and efficient performance across the full spectrum of dynamic scene complexity. The hierarchical, frequency-domain, and ODE-based motion decomposition strategies explored in the subsequent section build directly upon these representation paradigms, offering complementary tools for capturing the intricate temporal dynamics that these foundational architectures enable.

### 6.2 Motion Modeling and Decomposition Strategies for Dynamic Gaussians

Motion modeling represents the central challenge in extending 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) from static scenes to dynamic, time-varying environments. Building upon the representation paradigms established in the previous subsection—canonical deformation fields, per-primitive temporal models, and native 4D primitives—the choice of how to represent, decompose, and predict motion determines not only visual fidelity but also the capability for temporal extrapolation, physical plausibility, and long-range consistency. While the representation paradigms define *where* motion is encoded, the strategies reviewed here address *how* motion is modeled, decomposed, and predicted. A broad spectrum of strategies has emerged, ranging from frequency-domain decompositions and ordinary differential equation (ODE)-based formulations to hierarchical, rigid-body, and flow-guided approaches. These approaches directly complement the training strategies described in the following subsection, as the choice of motion model fundamentally shapes the optimization schedules, regularization terms, and robustness requirements discussed there.

**Fourier-Based and Frequency-Domain Motion Models**  
A natural approach for capturing periodic or quasi-periodic dynamics is to represent motion in the frequency domain. In the context of generative image dynamics, [345] proposes a Fourier-domain prior learned from real video sequences, where a spectral volume encodes dense, long-term motion trajectories for natural oscillatory dynamics such as swaying trees or flickering candles. Extending this to Gaussian-based dynamic scenes, FAST-GS [346] introduces a Fourier Motion Modeling module that decomposes complex motion into frequency-based sinusoidal components. This decomposition separates low-frequency global trajectories from high-frequency local motion details, enabling more accurate fitting of complex dynamic scenes while suppressing temporal jitter through frequency-dependent regularization. The frequency-view also benefits motion representation learning more broadly; FLD [347] learns structured motion representations in a parameterized latent space, enhancing interpolation and generalization for periodic motion patterns.

**ODE-Based and Dynamical-System Formulations**  
Beyond frequency analysis, continuous-time dynamical systems modeled by neural ordinary differential equations (ODEs) provide a principled framework for motion extrapolation and physically plausible dynamics. ParticleGS [348] reformulates dynamic 3D scenes as physically grounded particle systems, employing a Neural ODE-based evolver to learn continuous-time dynamics for predicting future scenes beyond observed timeframes. Similarly, ODE-GS [349] integrates latent neural ODEs with 3DGS, modeling Gaussian parameter trajectories as continuous-time latent dynamics. By aggregating past trajectories into a latent state and evolving it via an ODE solver, ODE-GS enables arbitrary future timestamp rendering without explicit timestamp conditioning. The latent ODE paradigm has been further refined by path-minimizing formulations [350], where an ℓ2 penalty on the latent path length improves extrapolation accuracy and inference efficiency. At the world-model level, ODEWorld [351] learns a continuous latent velocity field parameterized by an ODE in physical time, enabling arbitrary temporal resolution, backward prediction, and improved planning-oriented representation for robotic control.

**Global-to-Local and Hierarchical Motion Decomposition**  
Real-world scenes often exhibit motions at multiple scales, motivating hierarchical decomposition strategies that align naturally with the multi-level motion representations introduced in the previous subsection. HEIR [352] learns structured, interpretable motion hierarchies from data, representing observed motions using graph-based structures where vertices denote elemental motions and directed edges capture parent-child dependencies via graph neural networks. This approach explicitly decomposes global absolute motions into parent-inherited patterns and local residual motions, enabling more realistic dynamic Gaussian deformation. RiGS [353] introduces three types of Gaussian primitives—static, rigid, and transient—to represent long-term low-frequency motions and short-term high-frequency dynamics separately, with rigid Gaussians transitioning into transient Gaussians based on temporal duration. DynaSplat [354] also classifies elements as static or dynamic through deformation offset statistics and 2D motion flow consistency, then applies a hierarchical motion modeling strategy capturing both coarse global transformations and fine-grained local movements. A related frequency-domain hierarchical approach, the Long-Range Trajectories system [355], integrates motion from local and global representations to produce long-range trajectories that outperform trajectory-based methods for human activity analysis.

**Rigid-Body and SE(3)-Transformation-Based Dynamics**  
A powerful inductive bias for motion decomposition is the assumption that scene elements undergo rigid-body transformations governed by the Special Euclidean group SE(3). This geometric perspective connects directly to the canonical deformation fields discussed earlier, offering a more constrained and physically interpretable alternative to free-form neural deformations. Shape of Motion [356] represents scene motion with a compact set of SE(3) motion bases, expressing each point's motion as a linear combination of these bases and thereby enabling soft decomposition into multiple rigidly-moving groups. LieFlow [357] explicitly models motion within the SE(3) Lie group, unifying translation and rotation learning in a coherent geometric space, which enforces physically inspired constraints for motion continuity and geometric consistency. SIRE [358] learns intrinsic rigidity embeddings through a self-supervised framework, where an image encoder estimates scene rigidity and geometry, and a least-squares solver lifts 2D point tracks into SE(3) tracks for supervision—providing strong data efficiency for object segmentation and rigid motion estimation. The rigid-deformation decomposition framework [359] decouples global rigid-body motion from local deformation using two scale-specific networks, employing a frozen-anchor training strategy with quaternion-incremental schemes to improve kinematic separation and prediction accuracy. More generally, the structured motion geometry perspective [360] argues that real-world objects obey rigid-body kinematics, representing scene dynamics as a compact set of motion bases, each a temporal sequence of 6D twists in SE(3), with dense motion recovered via sparse, time-shared per-pixel assignment weights.

**Flow-Guided and Multi-Scale Motion Decomposition**  
Optical flow and scene flow provide dense motion cues that can guide Gaussian deformation, offering a complementary signal to the photometric losses emphasized in the training strategies of the next subsection. DynMF [361] decomposes a dynamic scene into a few neural trajectories, binding each point to motion coefficients that enforce sharing of basis trajectories; a sparsity loss on coefficients enables motion disentanglement and novel motion combinations. H3D-DGS [362] proposes heterogeneous 3D control points obtained via a hybrid strategy combining optical flow back-projection for directly observable components and gradient-based optimization for occluded ones, achieving rapid convergence. SplitGaussian [363] explicitly decouples static and dynamic components, preventing motion artifacts in static regions while allowing view- and time-dependent appearance refinement in the dynamic branch. At the video level, decomposition into static scenes and dynamic objects with quasi-3D canonical volumes, as in DecoMotion [364], facilitates affine transformations for camera motion and rectified non-rigid transformations for dynamic content. The training-free motion factorization framework [365] decomposes complex motion into motionlessness, rigid motion, and non-rigid motion, modulating synthesis of distinct motion categories in a disentangled manner. The Multi-Level Semantic Motion Encoder in [366] progressively masks features to capture multi-granularity spatiotemporal relationships, while the decoupled spatio-temporal framework DeST [367] performs once unified spatial modeling with joint-decoupled temporal modeling to capture distinctive motion patterns of each joint.

**Motion Composition and Interpretable Representations**  
Beyond decomposition, motion can be composed from reusable primitives or represented via interpretable embeddings, offering a bridge to the semantic and control-oriented applications discussed later in this survey. The Motion Poke Transformer [368] directly predicts distributions of local motion conditioned on sparse interactions termed "pokes," enabling interpretable, multimodal scene dynamics. Lagrangian Motion Fields [369] treat each joint as a Lagrangian particle with uniform velocity over short intervals, condensing motion into "supermotions" that integrate static spatial information with interpretable temporal dynamics, enabling efficient long-term generation. Motion Inversion [370] introduces motion embeddings—explicit, temporally coherent one-dimensional embeddings—that integrate with temporal transformers of video diffusion models, allowing complex motion manipulation through vector arithmetic. The decomposition of video content into foreground, background, and motion flow components [371] further enables fine-grained control over each component. Finally, the Flow Poke Transformer [368] and the compositional flow matching framework [372] highlight the benefit of explicit, reusable motion primitives for embodied trajectory generation, improving sample efficiency and accuracy. These diverse motion modeling and decomposition strategies collectively provide a rich toolkit for representing dynamic Gaussian scenes, balancing expressiveness, physical plausibility, interpretability, and computational efficiency. Critically, the choice among these motion models directly impacts the training dynamics and optimization strategies detailed in the next subsection—frequency-domain approaches lend themselves to regularized optimization, ODE-based formulations require careful temporal sampling schedules, and rigid-body models benefit from geometric consistency losses—thereby establishing a tight coupling between motion representation and the practical trainability of dynamic 3DGS systems.

### 6.3 Training Dynamics and Optimization for Long-Range and Sparse-Input Dynamic Scenes

Training 4D Gaussian representations for dynamic scenes presents optimization challenges that extend well beyond the static 3DGS case, yet the strategies developed to address these challenges are what ultimately make the downstream applications discussed in the next subsection—animatable avatars, dynamic SLAM, and real-time streaming—practically achievable. The core difficulty lies in the joint estimation of appearance, geometry, and—critically—motion over time, all while managing the computational and memory costs associated with extended sequences. This subsection reviews the practical training strategies that have emerged to address these issues, focusing on adaptive densification, progressive optimization schedules, motion-aware losses, and robustness to sparse or imperfect inputs.

**Adaptive Densification and Primitive Management for Motion-Dense Regions.** A fundamental issue in dynamic 3DGS training is the uneven distribution of motion across a scene. Naively applying densification strategies designed for static scenes often leads to a proliferation of Gaussians in static regions and insufficient capacity in fast-moving areas. A growing body of work addresses this imbalance. [373] introduces a lifespan-aware densification strategy that explicitly allocates more Gaussian capacity to regions with pronounced motion while keeping static areas compact, mitigating optimization imbalance. [374] tackles this by decomposing the scene into static, persistent dynamic, and transient appearance primitives, allowing the optimization to dynamically allocate modeling capacity based on photometric errors. This competitive allocation prevents the over-parameterization of static regions while preserving dynamic fidelity. [375] proposes separating static and dynamic Gaussians during training and explicitly sampling positions and rotations of dynamic Gaussians at sparse timestamps, which are then interpolated, using a point-backtracking technique to detect and remove erroneous Gaussians. Similarly, [376] shows that exploiting hierarchical structural patterns—where groups of Gaussians share similar transformations—allows for more efficient convergence with fewer iterations. [377] directly tackles the allocation mismatch by using semantic and motion priors from vision foundation models to concentrate control points in dynamic regions while suppressing redundancy in static backgrounds. [143], while focused on static scenes, provides a foundational analysis of split and clone operations that informs these dynamic approaches.

**Progressive and Coarse-to-Fine Optimization Schedules.** Long-range dynamic scenes are notoriously difficult to optimize from scratch; the loss landscape is highly non-convex, and the optimizer can get trapped in poor local minima. Progressive and coarse-to-fine strategies have proven crucial for stable convergence. [375] proposes a progressive training scheme where the model is initially trained using short timestamps and then progressively extended, allowing it to work effectively with sparse point clouds. [378] employs a hierarchical divide-and-conquer learning strategy to guide optimization toward globally coherent motion solutions. [379] introduces a progressive optimization strategy with two decoupled propagation stages—a background extension stage and a foreground propagation stage—to ensure robust reconstruction over long durations. For sparse-frame inputs, [380] introduces texture-aware deformation regularization and canonical optimization to handle the ill-posed nature of the problem, focusing on texture-rich areas that are particularly prone to failure. The need for such schedules is also highlighted in [381], which notes that initialization problems are exacerbated in fast-moving regions where SfM fails.

**Motion-Aware Consistency Losses and Regularization.** Recent methods emphasize that photometric loss alone is insufficient to learn physically plausible motion. Imposing motion-aware consistency losses is critical for temporal coherence and preventing drift. [382] proposes a mutual information shaping technique that enforces movement resonance between correlated Gaussians, an idea that can be integrated into the training loss to improve compositional control. [383] introduces a view-space ray grouping strategy to explicitly preserve local geometric structure of Gaussians over time, eliminating reliance on external flow priors. [384] argues that uncertainty matters—Gaussians with recurring observations should act as reliable anchors—and propagates reliable motion cues to enhance 4D reconstruction. [385] explicitly models per-Gaussian motion and uses optical flow on sparse key views as a lightweight motion cue to regularize against the tendency of photometric loss to chase pixel residuals. [386] introduces a physics-grounded supervised fine-tuning and a simulation-grounded reinforcement learning stage to correct residual physical violations, representing an extreme form of motion-aware regularization. [387] uses Cubic Hermite Splines with Temporal Curvature Regularization to ensure smooth motion evolution and prevent artifacts during temporal interpolation.

**Handling Sparse, Monocular, and Noisy Inputs.** A major driver of instability in dynamic 3DGS is the under-constrained nature of monocular or sparse-view input. Several methods explicitly address this challenge. [378] demonstrates that existing 4DGS methods dramatically fail under monocular settings, introducing isotropic Gaussian "marbles" to reduce degrees of freedom and adding image-level and geometry-level priors, such as tracking losses, to constrain optimization. [381] introduces uncertainty-aware regularization to identify uncertain regions with few observations and selectively imposes diffusion-based and depth-smoothness priors on them. [380] is specifically designed for scenarios where only sparse frames are accessible, proposing new regularization terms for both the deformation network and the canonical Gaussian field. [388] uses an occlusion-aware optimization with a view-conditioned diffusion prior to `sculpt` an initial reconstruction, dealing with severe occlusions, a common failure mode in monocular input. The challenge of noisy pose estimates is addressed by [389], which uses a symmetry-aware temporal encoding to absorb camera estimation drift. [390] introduces an adaptive densification strategy specifically to mitigate unstable Gaussian gradients during optimization of 4D generation, a challenge also present in reconstruction tasks.

**Optimization Stability and Long-Range Coherence.** Beyond the specific techniques above, ensuring stable convergence and temporal continuity over extended sequences is a critical concern, particularly as these reconstructed sequences are increasingly targeted for streaming and compression pipelines. [391] proposes progressive construction of local canonical anchor spaces to enhance temporal coherence and mitigate memory explosion and flickering. [392] explores keyframe-constrained streaming optimization under a max-points budget to improve long-horizon stability. [393] points out that piecewise linear velocity approximations lead to temporal fragmentation, and proposes a continuous SE(3) formulation that preserves temporal identity and mitigates unbounded memory growth. [394] presents a framework to construct a compact set of salient Gaussians via progressive pruning and merging, directly addressing the storage and optimization overhead of large dynamic models. [395] highlights the importance of jointly optimizing representation with rate-distortion trade-offs to reduce redundancy. Finally, the work on fast spatial memory of [396] is motivated by the observation that plastic inference-time updates are vulnerable to catastrophic forgetting, and proposes an elastic test-time training with a Fisher-weighted prior to stabilize long-context reconstruction, a concept directly applicable to online dynamic scene training. Collectively, these training strategies—adaptive capacity allocation, progressive schedules, motion-aware regularization, and robustness to imperfect inputs—form the optimization backbone that enables the efficient, stable, and deployable dynamic 3DGS systems explored in the following subsection.

### 6.4 Applications, Temporal Compression, and System-Level Integration

The practical utility of dynamic 3D Gaussian Splatting (4DGS) is ultimately determined by its ability to power real-world applications while overcoming the substantial storage, bandwidth, and computational overheads inherent to spatio-temporal representations. Building on the training strategies discussed previously—adaptive capacity allocation, progressive optimization, and motion-aware regularization—this subsection examines the primary application domains—animatable avatars, dynamic SLAM, and real-time streaming—alongside the critical enabling technologies of temporal compression and system-level integration that make these applications feasible on resource-constrained platforms.

**Animatable Avatar Reconstruction.** One of the most compelling applications of dynamic 3DGS is the creation of high-fidelity, animatable human avatars, directly leveraging the deformation and motion modeling capabilities developed in the training phase. These systems replace static representations with deformable Gaussian primitives that can be driven by pose parameters or expression codes. For clothed avatars, methods such as [397] leverage 2D Gaussian surfels to better align with surface geometry, improving both geometric accuracy and appearance fidelity over 3DGS-based counterparts. For head avatars, the challenge lies in modeling the distinct dynamics of deformable regions (e.g., skin) versus rigid regions (e.g., teeth). The Conditionally-Adaptive Gaussian Avatars (CAG-Avatar) framework [398] addresses this by using a cross-attention fusion module where each Gaussian acts as a query, selectively extracting relevant driving signals from a global expression code based on its canonical position. This localized conditioning, reminiscent of the motion-aware consistency losses discussed earlier, significantly reduces blurring and distortion in challenging regions. In the generative domain, AGORA [399] extends GAN-based generation to produce animatable avatars, combining spatial shape conditioning with a dual-discriminator strategy and achieving real-time performance on both GPU and mobile platforms. For streamable avatars, HGC-Avatar [400] introduces a hierarchical compression framework that disentangles the representation into a structural layer (StyleUNet-based generator) and a motion layer (SMPL-X), enabling layer-wise compression and progressive decoding for efficient transmission.

**Dynamic SLAM and Robotics.** Dynamic 3DGS is increasingly integrated into Simultaneous Localization and Mapping (SLAM) systems to enable dense, photorealistic mapping in dynamic environments, where the robustness to sparse and noisy inputs emphasized in the previous subsection becomes critical. Early methods like Dy3DGS-SLAM [401] were the first to use 3DGS SLAM with monocular RGB input, fusing optical flow and depth masks to filter dynamic objects. Building on this, D4DGS-SLAM [402] represents the first SLAM method based on a 4DGS map representation, incorporating a dynamics-aware InfoModule to filter unstable points and applying isotropic regularization based on dynamic characteristics. The challenge of computational efficiency is tackled by RTGS [403], an algorithm-hardware co-design framework that reduces redundancies via adaptive Gaussian pruning and dynamic downsampling, achieving real-time performance (≥30 FPS) on edge GPUs. For dynamic object handling, DAGS-SLAM [404] maintains per-Gaussian motion probability states and triggers semantic segmentation on demand via an uncertainty-aware scheduler, balancing accuracy and compute. UP-SLAM [405] further improves robustness with a probabilistic octree for primitive management and a training-free uncertainty estimator for open-set dynamic object detection. Memory-efficient variants like MemGS [53] address GPU memory constraints by merging redundant Gaussians in voxel space, while DiskChunGS [406] enables large-scale reconstruction by storing inactive spatial chunks on disk. The survey by [407] provides a comprehensive technical reference for this rapidly evolving area.

**Real-Time Streaming and On-the-Fly 4D Reconstruction.** The ability to stream dynamic scenes in real-time is critical for volumetric video and immersive communication, and it directly benefits from the long-range coherence and optimization stability techniques described earlier. Online reconstruction methods have made significant strides in reducing per-frame training time. 3DGStream [408] achieves on-the-fly per-frame reconstruction within 12 seconds using a Neural Transformation Cache. HiCoM [409] improves efficiency by introducing a Hierarchical Coherent Motion mechanism and perturbation smoothing, reducing training wall time to less than 2 seconds per frame. For arbitrary-length sequences, SwinGS [410] integrates spacetime Gaussians with Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) and a sliding window strategy, reducing transmission costs by 83.6% and enabling WebGL-based playback on mobile devices. ClipGStream [411] offers a hybrid clip-level stream optimization for long sequences, while StreamSplat [412] provides a fully feed-forward framework that instantly transforms uncalibrated videos into dynamic 3DGS representations. The DASS pipeline [413] introduces a three-stage framework (selective inheritance, dynamics-aware shift, error-guided densification) for iterative streamable reconstruction. For bandwidth-adaptive delivery, PD-4DGS [414] proposes progressive compression with hierarchical deformation decomposition, reducing first-frame latency from 73-930 seconds to ~1.7 seconds on a 2 Mbps link, while TIGAS [415] offloads rasterization to a backend and streams view-dependent 2D projections over QUIC for thin-client rendering.

**Temporal Compression and Rate-Distortion Optimization.** A central challenge for deployment is the enormous storage footprint of 4DGS models, which the memory-management strategies of the previous subsection help mitigate but do not fully resolve. The survey by [416] provides a unified overview of compression techniques, categorizing them into parameter and restructuring compression. Significant progress has been made in rate-distortion (RD) optimized compression. P-4DGS [417] designs a 3D anchor point-based spatial-temporal prediction module combined with adaptive quantization and context-based entropy coding, achieving up to 90× compression. RD4DGS [418] employs a wavelet transform to exploit temporal smoothness priors, achieving up to 91× compression. MEGA [419] decomposes color attributes into a direct component and a shared predictor, eliminating spherical harmonics, and reduces storage by approximately 190×. TC3DGS [420] selectively prunes Gaussians based on temporal relevance and uses gradient-aware mixed-precision quantization along with an adapted Ramer-Douglas-Peucker algorithm for trajectory interpolation, achieving up to 67× compression. 4DGC [395] introduces a motion-aware representation with a compact motion grid and an end-to-end compression scheme for variable bitrate support. For motion decoupling, 4D-MoDe [421] separates static backgrounds from dynamic foregrounds, achieving as low as 11.4 KB/frame, while ComGS [422] uses keypoint-driven motion representation to achieve over 159× storage reduction. Additionally, CDGS [423] formulates dynamic scene reconstruction as a budget-constrained optimization problem, enforcing strict Gaussian budgets for edge deployment. Finally, 4DGS-CC [424] provides a contextual coding framework using neural voxel and vector quantization coding, while EvolvingGS [425] exploits temporal coherence between frames to achieve over 50× compression. The system-level latency analysis by [426] provides critical insights into optimizing the application, transport, and network layers for end-to-end responsiveness. Together, these applications and compression frameworks demonstrate a clear trajectory toward scalable, real-time, and deployable dynamic 3DGS systems, closing the loop from the optimization foundations established in the previous subsection to practical, resource-aware deployment.

## 7 Compression, Acceleration, and Scalability

### 7.1 Model Compression Techniques for Static 3DGS

### 7.1 Model Compression Techniques for Static 3DGS

The substantial storage footprint of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) remains a primary barrier to its widespread deployment. A single high-fidelity scene, represented by millions of Gaussian primitives each carrying up to 59 floating-point parameters, can consume roughly one gigabyte of memory [427; 98]. This challenge has driven a rich body of research on reducing model memory and storage requirements while preserving rendering quality—a pursuit that is closely intertwined with the Level-of-Detail (LOD) and progressive rendering techniques discussed in the following subsection. Indeed, effective compression not only enables efficient storage and transmission but also underpins scalable rendering by providing compact, multi-resolution representations that can be selectively activated based on viewing conditions. This subsection offers a comprehensive overview of model compression techniques for static 3DGS, broadly categorized into primitive-level pruning, attribute-level quantization and coding, and hybrid frameworks that integrate multiple strategies.

**Pruning Strategies**

A foundational approach to reducing model size is eliminating redundant or insignificant Gaussian primitives. A common strategy is to prune Gaussians that contribute little to the final rendering. This can be achieved through post-hoc, gradient-informed iterative pruning, which identifies and removes redundant information to enable memory and computational scalability [428]. Similarly, ELMGS integrates differential quantization and entropy coding estimators into the optimization strategy alongside an iterative pruning approach to enhance both compressibility and scalability [429].

More sophisticated pruning methods move beyond simple heuristics. For instance, some frameworks learn an importance score for each Gaussian to automatically identify the least significant primitives for a given scene, enabling more effective pruning than manual criteria [430]. A similar philosophy is adopted by the Gradient-Driven Natural Selection framework, which models survival pressure as a regularization gradient field on opacity, allowing the optimization process to autonomously determine which Gaussians to retain [131]. VEDAL formalizes this concept further by casting pruning as a variational free energy minimization problem, using a prediction-error gating mechanism to activate pruning asynchronously [132]. Other work focuses on the *timing* and *method* of densification, introducing an Edge-Aware Score for selecting candidates for splitting and a Long-Axis Split strategy to improve the quality of the cloned Gaussians, coupled with overfitting mitigation techniques like Recovery-Aware Pruning to achieve a more compact and higher-fidelity model [42].

An interesting direction is geometry-driven pruning, where primitives are ranked based solely on their 3D attributes without relying on 2D image information, as seen in the PointSplat framework, which uses a dual-branch encoder to re-weight geometry and appearance [431]. Furthermore, pruning can be combined with more expressive primitives, such as the Difference-of-Gaussians, to reduce the total number of primitives required while maintaining visual quality [432]. Some methods also exploit redundancy in the function space, where strongly overlapping and non-orthogonal Gaussian basis functions contribute to representational redundancy, using quantum-inspired eigendecomposition to select a representative subset of primitives [433].

**Quantization and Attribute Coding**

After reducing the number of primitives, the remaining storage overhead is dominated by the 45–59 floating-point parameters per Gaussian. Quantization is a powerful tool to reduce the bit-width of these attributes. A simple yet effective technique is vector quantization based on K-means, which clusters similar Gaussian parameters and stores a small codebook along with the index for each Gaussian, achieving significant compression while maintaining quality [434]. To ensure consistency between the continuous optimization and discrete parameter assignment, the noise-substituted vector quantization technique is used to jointly train codebooks and model features [427].

More advanced methods explore different quantization spaces and strategies. For instance, a data-independent approach based on random rotations has been proposed, which transforms parameter vectors into a space with a known distribution, enabling near-optimal Lloyd-Max quantization without any per-scene training or calibration data [435]. Another line of work replaces uniform scalar quantization with lattice vector quantization (LVQ), which can be adapted to the scene-specific characteristics of the data, improving rate-distortion efficiency while maintaining low complexity [436]. More comprehensive approaches, such as GETA-3DGS, propose an end-to-end automatic framework for joint structured pruning and mixed-precision quantization, treating each Gaussian as a group with heterogeneous per-attribute bit-widths optimized under a target storage budget [437].

**Entropy Coding and Context Modeling**

To squeeze out the final bits, many compression pipelines incorporate entropy coding. This is often enhanced by sophisticated context models that estimate the probability distribution of the quantized attributes for more efficient arithmetic coding. A pioneering work in this direction introduced a hash-grid assisted context framework, which leverages the structured nature of a binary hash grid to model the spatial relations between unorganized anchors or Gaussians [438]. This was later extended to HAC++, which further captures intra-anchor contextual relationships and uses an adaptive quantization module for higher-precision quantization, achieving over 100x compression [439]. Building on these concepts, ContextGS introduced the first autoregressive model at the anchor level, dividing anchors into different levels and predicting uncoded anchors based on already-coded ones to improve coding efficiency [440].

Another line of work focuses on integrating prediction techniques borrowed from video compression. For example, a spatial condition-based prediction module can be used to predict attributes of Gaussian primitives from the surrounding scene context, with residual compensation learning fine-grained missing information [441]. A more recent framework, MesonGS++, proposes a size-aware post-training codec that combines joint importance-based pruning, octree geometry coding, attribute transformation, selective vector quantization for higher-degree spherical harmonics, and group-wise mixed-precision quantization with entropy coding [442]. Prior to this, MesonGS laid the groundwork with a post-training codec that used a measurement criterion and attribute transformations like replacing quaternions with Euler angles to improve compression rates [443].

The choice of entropy model is critical. The Mixture of Priors (MoP) strategy addresses limitations in existing entropy models by using a Mixture-of-Experts paradigm to process hyperprior information, and introduces a prior-guided Coarse-to-Fine Quantization (C2FQ) strategy for element-wise quantization [444].

**Codebook-Based and Hybrid Frameworks**

Many state-of-the-art methods combine the aforementioned techniques into a unified, hybrid framework. The compact 3D Gaussian representation C3DGS proposes a learnable mask to prune redundant Gaussians and a grid-based neural field to replace spherical harmonics, before applying residual vector quantization to geometric attributes [98; 97]. ContraGS takes a different approach by enabling training directly on codebook-compressed representations, using MCMC sampling to solve the problem of learning non-differentiable parameters, which drastically reduces the peak memory during training [445]. Another training-in-the-loop method, OMG (Optimized Minimal Gaussians), focuses on reducing the number of Gaussians to a minimal set while using a compact and precise attribute representation with sub-vector quantization [446]. MEGS² also contributes to this area by replacing memory-intensive spherical harmonics with spherical Gaussian lobes and proposing a unified soft pruning framework that jointly optimizes the number of primitives and parameters [447].

Beyond these, there are hybrid approaches that integrate diffusion priors for image-level restoration after heavy compression [448], or leverage the powerful representational capacity of learned image priors to recover compression-induced quality degradation [449]. Finally, some methods use dictionary learning to compress the parameters of a pre-trained 3DGS model directly, without re-training or architectural modifications [450]. Collectively, these techniques demonstrate a clear trend toward more holistic and integrated compression pipelines, effectively balancing compression ratios, rendering fidelity, and computational efficiency [451]. Importantly, the compact and hierarchical representations produced by these compression methods naturally complement the LOD and progressive rendering strategies explored next, enabling adaptive quality scaling and efficient bandwidth utilization across diverse deployment scenarios.

### 7.2 Level-of-Detail (LOD) and Progressive Rendering

### 7.2 Level-of-Detail and Progressive Rendering

While the compression techniques discussed in the previous subsection primarily target static storage reduction, real-time interactive applications impose an additional, orthogonal challenge: rendering must gracefully adapt to changing viewing conditions, hardware capabilities, and bandwidth constraints without compromising the user experience. Level-of-Detail (LOD) techniques address this by managing the computational and memory costs of rendering complex 3D scenes at runtime. Within the context of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS), LOD frameworks have been developed to enable scalable rendering across diverse hardware capabilities and bandwidth constraints, leveraging the explicit primitive-based nature of Gaussians to achieve what was difficult with implicit neural representations. These approaches generally fall into two broad paradigms: discrete LOD, which offers a finite set of quality levels, and continuous LOD, which provides a seamless spectrum of detail from a single unified model.
