Notably, the compact hierarchical representations produced by the compression methods in Section 7.1—such as learnable masks, codebooks, and multi-resolution structures—provide a natural substrate upon which many LOD systems build, while the progressive streaming mechanisms described here also complement the storage-oriented goals of compression by enabling adaptive bandwidth utilization.

Discrete LOD methods traditionally build distinct, pre-optimized model versions that are swapped based on viewing conditions. However, this paradigm suffers from significant storage overhead and visual "popping" artifacts during transitions. In contrast, the explicit and primitive-based nature of 3DGS enables a more elegant solution. For instance, CLoD-GS introduces a continuous LOD mechanism by attaching a learnable, distance-dependent decay parameter to each Gaussian primitive. This parameter dynamically modulates the primitive's opacity based on viewpoint proximity, allowing for the progressive and smooth filtering of less significant primitives and creating a continuous spectrum of detail within a single model. The framework employs a virtual distance scaling mechanism and a coarse-to-fine training strategy with rendered point count regularization to ensure robustness across all distances [452]. Similarly, Matryoshka Gaussian Splatting (MGS) achieves continuous LOD through stochastic budget training, where each optimization iteration samples a random splat budget and optimizes both the corresponding prefix and the full set of ordered Gaussians. This strategy allows the model to render any prefix of the Gaussian set as a coherent reconstruction with smoothly improving fidelity as the budget increases, effectively establishing a continuous speed-quality trade-off without requiring architectural modifications [453].

Hierarchical multi-level representations form another key strategy for LOD. These approaches typically construct a pyramid of detail, where each level represents the scene at a different granularity, and they bear a strong conceptual resemblance to the multi-resolution quantization and codebook hierarchies introduced in Section 7.1. The Iterative Gaussian Synopsis framework, for example, employs a top-down "unfolding" scheme. It begins with a full-resolution 3DGS model and iteratively derives coarser LODs using an adaptive, learnable mask-based pruning mechanism. This process constructs a multi-level hierarchy that integrates hierarchical spatial grids with a shared Anchor Codebook to capture both global structure and localized details, promoting inter-layer reusability and minimizing data overhead for progressive refinement [454]. FLoD proposes a flexible level-of-detail approach that constructs a multi-level 3DGS representation through level-specific 3D scale constraints. Each level independently reconstructs the entire scene, offering varying detail and GPU memory usage, which allows for selective rendering of different image regions at different detail levels and enables real-time rendering under diverse memory constraints [455]. LODGE presents a hierarchical LOD representation for large-scale scenes by iteratively selecting optimal subsets of Gaussians based on camera distance. It constructs each LOD level by applying depth-aware smoothing filters followed by importance-based pruning and fine-tuning, and partitions the scene into spatial chunks for dynamic loading to reduce rendering time and GPU memory [456]. A notable system-level implementation, Virtualized 3D Gaussians, draws inspiration from game engines to construct hierarchical 3D Gaussian clusters and dynamically select only the perceptible clusters during rendering, balancing efficiency and visual quality for composed scenes requiring billions of Gaussians [457]. CityGaussian also incorporates an LoD strategy with block-wise detail level selection for efficient large-scale scene rendering, following a divide-and-conquer training approach [458].

Continuous LOD is also being explored through alternative primitive designs. Fourier Splatting introduces an inherently scalable primitive by parameterizing planar surfels with Fourier encoded descriptors. This allows a single trained model to be rendered at varying levels of detail simply by truncating Fourier coefficients at runtime, providing a versatile solution for bandwidth-constrained rendering [65]. Frequency-aware Gaussian Splatting Decomposition offers another perspective by organizing 3D Gaussians into groups corresponding to Laplacian-pyramid subbands of input images. This explicit frequency decomposition enables dynamic LOD rendering, progressive streaming, and foveated rendering, as well as artistic filtering [145].

Progressive and scalable compression schemes are essential for adaptive streaming and deployment, serving as a bridge between the static compression objectives of Section 7.1 and the real-time rendering demands addressed here. GoDe (Gaussians on Demand) introduces a framework for scalable compression and progressive LOD control. It reorganizes Gaussian primitives into a fixed progressive hierarchy based on aggregated gradient sensitivity, enabling multiple rate-distortion operating points from a single trained model without retraining, and achieves rate-distortion performance comparable to state-of-the-art single-rate methods [459]. For dynamic scenes, PD-4DGS (Progressive Decomposition of 4DGS) presents the first framework for progressive compression and on-demand transmission, decomposing the motion hierarchy into independently transmittable layers to enable any prefix of the bitstream to be immediately renderable and compatible with modern adaptive-bitrate streaming [414]. LapisGS constructs a layered 3DGS structure for cumulative representation, incorporating dynamic opacity optimization and occupancy maps to support adaptive streaming and progressive rendering, effectively balancing visual fidelity with model compactness for bandwidth-aware applications [460]. The concept of progressive rendering can also be extended to neural representations beyond Gaussians, as demonstrated by methods for encoding continuous levels of detail in light field networks [461].

Beyond the core LOD paradigms, several works address the challenge from complementary angles. 3DGS³ proposes a unified post-rendering framework that performs gradient-aware super sampling and temporal frame interpolation on low-resolution outputs to achieve high-resolution and high-frame-rate rendering, a strategy that complements LOD by decoupling rendering resolution from the underlying scene complexity [462]. Techniques from the broader compression field, such as region-adaptive learned hierarchical encoding, can also contribute to scalable representations by designing codecs that overfit latents to each Gaussian attribute under a global rate constraint, exploiting octree structures for hierarchical multi-resolution representation [463]. Finally, the analysis of converged 3DGS solutions has revealed that dense regions of a scene exhibit geometry-correlated parameters amenable to render-free prediction, while sparse regions require multi-view constraints, providing density-aware strategies that can inform how LOD systems allocate primitives for improved robustness and efficiency [464].

In summary, the field of LOD and progressive rendering for 3DGS is rapidly evolving from simple discrete model variants towards sophisticated, single-model continuous representations that support seamless quality scaling, progressive loading, and efficient streaming across a wide range of devices and network conditions. These capabilities not only complement the storage-centric compression methods of Section 7.1 by enabling adaptive runtime selection of detail, but also set the stage for the training-time scalability challenges addressed next in Section 7.3, where efficient production of high-quality large-scale models becomes the enabling factor for the LOD systems described here to operate at their full potential.

### 7.3 Training Acceleration, Out-of-Core, and Distributed Optimization

### 7.3 Training Acceleration, Out-of-Core, and Distributed Optimization

The training of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) models, while significantly faster than NeRF-based counterparts, remains a computationally intensive process, particularly as scene complexity and the number of Gaussian primitives scale. The core bottlenecks are threefold: the cost of the rasterization pass itself, the memory footprint required to store parameters, gradients, and optimizer states for millions of primitives, and the system-level inefficiencies in how data is accessed and computed. This subsection surveys the key innovations in algorithm-system co-design, out-of-core training, and distributed optimization that collectively aim to break these barriers, complementing the level-of-detail and progressive rendering techniques discussed previously by addressing the upstream challenge of efficiently producing the high-quality, large-scale models those methods rely on.

At the foundational level, the efficiency of the rendering and gradient computation pipeline is paramount. The conventional 3DGS training pipeline sequentially executes Gaussian densification, projection, and color splatting, a process that suffers from skewed density allocation, imbalanced computation workloads during projection, and fragmented memory access during color splatting. To address these, algorithm-system co-design has proven highly effective. For instance, **BalanceGS** proposes a holistic optimization: at the algorithm level, it introduces heuristic workload-sensitive Gaussian density control to automatically balance point distributions by removing redundant Gaussians in dense regions while filling gaps in sparse areas, which rectifies the skewed density allocation [465]. At the system level, it replaces the static one-to-one thread-pixel mapping with a similarity-based Gaussian sampling and merging strategy, allowing threads to dynamically process variable numbers of Gaussians based on local cluster density, thereby balancing the computation workload [465]. Furthermore, it employs a reordering-based memory access mapping strategy to restructure RGB storage, enabling efficient batch loading in shared memory and mitigating fragmented memory access [465]. This co-design approach achieves a significant 1.44× training speedup on an NVIDIA A100 GPU with negligible quality degradation [465].

Building upon these principles, more aggressive system-level frameworks have been developed to push training performance further. **LiteGS** presents a high-performance framework that systematically optimizes the training pipeline through a multi-level strategy. At the low-level computation layer, it designs a warp-based raster associated with two hardware-aware optimizations to significantly reduce gradient reduction overhead. At the mid-level data management layer, it introduces dynamic spatial sorting based on Morton coding to enable a highly performant "Cluster-Cull-Compact" pipeline, which improves data locality and reduces cache misses. At the top-level algorithm layer, it establishes a new robust densification criterion based on the variance of the opacity gradient, paired with a more stable opacity control mechanism, to achieve more precise parameter growth. Experimental results demonstrate that LiteGS accelerates the original 3DGS training by up to 13.4× with comparable or superior quality [466]. These works highlight that performance gains are not solely derived from algorithmic improvements but from a deep co-design of the algorithm with the underlying hardware architecture.

While the aforementioned methods target in-memory training, the memory wall presents an insurmountable barrier for scaling to extremely large-scale scenes. The primary limitation is that each Gaussian primitive carries a large attribute vector, and the aggregate parameter table quickly exceeds GPU capacity, limiting prior systems to tens of millions of Gaussians. A key observation is that 3DGS training is inherently sparse and trajectory-conditioned: each iteration activates only the Gaussians visible from the current camera batch. This insight has enabled the GPU memory to be treated as a working-set cache rather than a persistent parameter store. This principle is the foundation of out-of-core training frameworks. **TideGS** introduces an out-of-core training framework that manages parameters across an SSD-CPU-GPU hierarchy. It achieves this via three synergistic techniques: block-virtualized geometry for SSD-aligned spatial locality, a hierarchical asynchronous pipeline to overlap I/O with computation, and trajectory-adaptive differential streaming that transfers only incremental working-set deltas between iterations. This system enables training with over one billion Gaussians on a single 24 GB GPU, achieving the best reconstruction quality among evaluated single-GPU baselines on large-scale scenes [467].

Similarly, **GS-Scale** addresses GPU memory limitations by storing all Gaussians in host memory and transferring only a subset on-demand for each forward and backward pass. To mitigate the slowdowns introduced by CPU-based frustum culling and optimizer updates, GS-Scale employs three system-level optimizations: selective offloading of geometric parameters for fast frustum culling, parameter forwarding to pipeline CPU optimizer updates with GPU computation, and a deferred optimizer update to minimize unnecessary memory accesses for Gaussians with zero gradients. This approach lowers GPU memory demands by 3.3-5.6× while achieving training speeds comparable to GPU-only training, thereby enabling large-scale 3DGS training on consumer-grade GPUs [468]. These out-of-core methods are reminiscent of analogous challenges in large language model (LLM) training, where host memory offloading and heterogeneous memory hierarchies are also crucial. For instance, systems have been developed to offload optimizer states to host memory to reduce GPU memory utilization [469], and more recent works redefine the roles of CPU and GPU for large-model optimization by using host memory as the authoritative parameter store [470]. The success of these approaches in LLMs provides a strong precedent for the ongoing shift towards memory-centric and out-of-core execution paradigms in 3DGS.

At an even larger scale, training must be distributed across multiple GPUs. This introduces challenges related to load balancing, communication overhead, and data partitioning. While specific multi-GPU training systems for 3DGS are still nascent, the principles of load-balanced partitioning and hybrid parallelism from the broader machine learning community are directly applicable. For example, systems like **Zorse** demonstrate that efficient training on heterogeneous clusters requires the integration of pipeline and data parallelism in a manner that is both communication- and memory-efficient, with the capability to flexibly partition GPUs into asymmetric pipeline stages [471]. Similarly, **HETHUB** tackles heterogeneous clusters by introducing a distributed unified communicator and an automatic parallel planner to efficiently train models across AMD, Nvidia, and other GPU types [472]. Furthermore, the **HexiScale** system formalizes the allocation of asymmetric partitioned training computations over heterogeneous GPUs as a constrained optimization problem, proposing an efficient hierarchical graph partitioning algorithm to fully leverage available computational power [473]. These approaches to distributed and heterogeneous training, though developed for LLMs, provide a critical blueprint for adapting 3DGS to multi-GPU and multi-node environments, ensuring that the increased scale of Gaussian primitives can be matched by proportional compute resources without being bottlenecked by communication or load imbalance.

In summary, the path to scalable 3DGS training is being paved through a three-pronged approach: fine-grained hardware-aware kernel and scheduling optimizations (e.g., warp-based rasterization, Morton coding), out-of-core hierarchical memory management to break the GPU memory wall, and large-scale distributed system designs adapted from the broader deep learning community. The convergence of these strategies promises to make the training of billion-primitive, photorealistic scenes both memory-feasible and time-efficient. These training-time advancements are not only essential for producing the high-quality models used in the level-of-detail and progressive rendering systems described earlier, but they also lay the groundwork for the deployment and streaming optimizations discussed in the next subsection, ultimately unlocking new possibilities for large-scale spatial intelligence applications.

### 7.4 Hardware Acceleration, Mobile Deployment, and System Integration

The deployment of 3D Gaussian Splatting in real-world, resource-constrained environments—ranging from embedded devices and mobile platforms to edge servers—represents the final frontier of the system-level challenges introduced in the previous subsection. While the core 3DGS pipeline is designed for GPU-friendly rasterization, achieving interactive frame rates under tight power, memory, and bandwidth budgets necessitates a holistic approach that spans hardware architecture, software optimization, and network-aware delivery—building upon the memory-centric and distributed paradigms previously discussed by addressing the equally demanding constraints of the deployment side.

**Hardware-Accelerated Rasterization and Custom Accelerators**

A primary avenue for enabling real-time 3DGS on edge devices is the development of specialized hardware accelerators, paralleling the algorithm-system co-design philosophy that proved essential for training acceleration. Field-Programmable Gate Arrays (FPGAs) and Application-Specific Integrated Circuits (ASICs) offer the potential for significant gains in both performance and energy efficiency over general-purpose GPUs, and their viability for 3DGS is underpinned by the same hardware-software co-design principles that have driven the training-time optimizations discussed earlier. The design of such accelerators requires careful consideration of the underlying dataflow and memory access patterns, a challenge well-documented in the broader context of neural network acceleration [474; 475]. For 3DGS, this involves architecting pipelines that can efficiently handle the tile-based rasterization, Gaussian sorting, and alpha-blending stages. Custom accelerators can integrate compression-aware pipelines directly into the hardware, reducing memory traffic and power consumption, much like the Morton coding and memory access restructuring used to optimize training. The viability of such approaches is supported by general-purpose edge-AI accelerator designs that demonstrate substantial improvements in throughput and energy efficiency by exploiting algorithmic properties like sparsity or reduced precision [476; 477]. Furthermore, a hardware-software co-design philosophy is crucial, as the most effective solutions jointly optimize the algorithm and the architecture [478; 479]. Techniques such as RISC-V ISA extensions for specific acceleration functions offer a programmable yet efficient middle ground for edge inference [480].

**Mobile and Edge Platform Optimization**

Beyond custom silicon, optimizing 3DGS for existing mobile and embedded GPUs is a critical practical concern on platforms like those from NVIDIA's Jetson family, and these efforts mirror the workload-balancing and cache-locality strategies that proved essential for training efficiency. Profiling studies on these platforms reveal significant underutilization of key computational resources, such as streaming multiprocessors (SMs) and tensor cores, despite high overall GPU utilization, indicating that software optimizations like warp-coherent rasterization and memory access scheduling are essential to unlock true hardware potential [481]. The inherent constraints of such devices, including thermal throttling and limited memory bandwidth, can lead to significant performance degradation during sustained operation, a phenomenon often overlooked in benchmark-centric evaluations [482]. Emulation-based studies that systematically under-clock and power-cap a high-end GPU provide valuable insights into the performance-energy trade-offs that will govern the viability of edge-deployed 3DGS systems, particularly for standalone XR headsets [483]. The "Memory Wall" is frequently a more significant bottleneck than raw compute, especially for high-resolution outputs. Hardware-algorithm co-design strategies that decouple compute-bound and memory-bound components can effectively mask latency and enable higher-resolution real-time generation, a principle directly applicable to optimizing 3DGS pipelines [484].

**On-Device Training and Adaptation**

While the primary goal is often high-performance inference, the ability to adapt or fine-tune a 3DGS model on-device extends the out-of-core and memory-efficient training principles from the previous subsection into the deployment domain, opening up new possibilities for personalization and continual learning. This is an extremely demanding task given the memory and compute constraints of edge hardware, and it inherits the same memory-wall challenges that motivated host-memory offloading and working-set caching in large-scale training. Lightweight adaptation techniques, such as those explored for fine-tuning large language models on a single edge-class GPU [485], can inform strategies for efficient 3DGS fine-tuning. Similarly, frameworks designed for on-device continual learning, which use hyperdimensional computing to provide fast, non-iterative online updates with a small memory footprint, could inspire novel approaches for updating Gaussian parameters in response to new data or changing environments without full retraining [486]. The requirement for timely decision-making in autonomous systems also demands self-adaptive runtimes that can balance real-time performance, task reward, memory, and energy consumption for on-device training and inference loops [487]. The training process itself can be made more efficient and deployable through algorithmic compression, such as tensor-train decomposition, with a co-designed hardware engine to accelerate the computationally intensive intermediate steps [479].

**Streaming and Adaptive Transmission**

For interactive and immersive applications, the ability to stream 3DGS content over bandwidth-constrained networks is as critical as local rendering performance, and it presents a complementary challenge to the out-of-core memory hierarchy discussed in training: just as training moves data between SSD, CPU, and GPU based on access patterns, deployment must move model data between servers and devices based on network conditions. A monolithic 3DGS model must be fully downloaded before any rendering can occur, leading to unacceptable black-screen waits on mobile connections. Progressive compression and transmission frameworks address this by decomposing the model into hierarchy of layers—such as a static scaffold, global deformation, and local refinement—so that any prefix of the bitstream is immediately renderable [414]. This approach, analogous to modern adaptive-bitrate (ABR) video streaming, enables true on-demand delivery. Strategies for rendering content on a server and streaming the resulting video to clients can also be enhanced by adaptively adjusting frame rate and resolution based on scene content, motion, and network bandwidth to optimize perceived quality [488]. At the system level, the high computational demands of 3DGS may necessitate edge offloading, where a portion of the rendering or computation is performed on a nearby server, echoing the distributed and heterogeneous training strategies from the previous subsection but now applied to inference. Reinforcement learning-based frameworks can dynamically allocate network resources and make offloading decisions based on the headset's local computing capabilities, network conditions, and the trade-offs between frame loss rate and energy efficiency, thereby extending coverage area and reducing energy consumption on the mobile device [489]. To further reduce latency, models can be partitioned and partially executed on the edge device with a lightweight on-device encoder, transmitting only compact feature tensors to a remote server for the heavier processing [490]. Finally, for orchestrated systems serving multiple clients, adaptive scheduling frameworks that jointly optimize operator parallelism, placement, and configuration transitions across heterogeneous compute and bandwidth constraints can significantly improve end-to-end throughput for related data-preparation and rendering pipelines [491].

In summary, the deployment-side optimizations for 3DGS form a coherent ecosystem that mirrors and extends the training-side system challenges: custom accelerators and hardware-algorithm co-design address compute and energy efficiency, mobile optimizations tackle memory and thermal walls, on-device adaptation borrows from memory-efficient training paradigms, and streaming frameworks address network bandwidth constraints. Together with the training acceleration, out-of-core, and distributed optimization techniques discussed previously, these deployment strategies complete the full lifecycle of 3DGS—from billion-primitive model creation on multi-GPU clusters to real-time interactive experiences on resource-constrained edge devices—thereby enabling the large-scale spatial intelligence applications that motivate this entire body of work.

## 8 Semantic Understanding, Editing, and Scene Manipulation

### 8.1 Semantic Feature Distillation and Representation

The integration of semantic understanding into 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) represents a pivotal advancement, transforming scenes from mere geometric and appearance models into queryable, interactive environments. The cornerstone of this transformation is the ability to embed high-dimensional semantic features, typically derived from powerful 2D foundation models, into the explicit 3D Gaussian primitives. This subsection surveys the methodologies developed for this crucial process of semantic feature distillation and representation, a field that navigates the inherent challenges of aligning 2D view-dependent features with a 3D world model while maintaining efficiency and fidelity. The approaches explored here—ranging from direct feature injection to compact, decoupled, and hierarchical representations—collectively establish the representational substrate upon which higher-level tasks such as open-vocabulary and instance-level segmentation, detailed in the following subsection, are built.

The prevailing paradigm in the initial wave of research involves distilling features from pre-trained 2D models such as CLIP, SAM, and LSeg into the 3D Gaussian field. The foundational work in this area, [492], demonstrated the feasibility of this approach by establishing the first general framework for distilling arbitrary-dimensional feature fields into 3DGS. This work tackled the initial hurdles of spatial resolution and channel consistency mismatches between RGB images and feature maps, laying the groundwork for subsequent methods. Building on this, approaches like [493] sought to integrate semantics more efficiently, proposing a Semantic Attribute Compactness (SAC) approach to learn compact yet effective semantic representations, achieving real-time rendering speeds (over 100 FPS).

However, a central challenge in this distillation process is the inherent cross-view inconsistency of 2D semantic features. The task of supervising a 3D feature field with inconsistent 2D predictions across different viewpoints is a significant source of error. To mitigate this, methods like [494] integrate vision-language embeddings into 3DGS and introduce a pixel alignment loss to ensure that rendered feature distances for the same semantic entities are close, thereby fostering multi-view consistency and enabling significant speedups in inference. Similarly, [493] introduces a 3D Coherent Self-training (3DCS) strategy, which leverages the multi-view consistency inherent to the 3D model itself to refine pseudo-labels and impose cross-view consistency constraints. Another significant challenge is the granularity inconsistency in 2D segmentations from models like SAM, where an object might be segmented as a whole in one view but into its constituent parts in another. To address this, [495] proposes constructing local graphs to propagate contextual features across Gaussians, reducing noise from inconsistent granularity, and employs mask-centric contrastive learning to smooth features across views. More uniquely, some methods explicitly tackle the view-dependent nature of semantics by decomposing the 3D scene into objects and constructing view-aggregated semantic representations, as proposed in [496].

The storage and computational overhead of embedding high-dimensional features is a significant practical bottleneck. Attaching a high-dimensional CLIP feature to each of millions of Gaussians inflates scene sizes to gigabytes, hindering storage and deployment. This has spurred a wave of research into feature compression and compact representation. One common approach is dimensionality reduction through specialized networks. For instance, [497] not only addresses multiview inconsistency by associating SAM prompt density with camera distance but also learns a granularity factor to guide which 2D features are most consistent for distillation. More advanced methods treat the problem as a rate-distortion challenge. Notably, [498] introduces a paradigm shift by decoupling field construction from storage. It uses a closed-form transmittance-weighted lift to create spatially grounded semantic anchors without per-scene training, and then compresses the per-Gaussian binding to these anchors using a spatially predictive entropy coder. This approach achieves sub-megabyte storage while maintaining high accuracy, demonstrating the power of treating semantic field storage as a data compression problem.

Further avenues for compactness include parametric feature grids and codebook-based learning. [499] proposes a semantic feature grid to save multi-view CLIP features and then maps the grids to low-dimensional features for training, enabling high-quality and real-time open-vocabulary queries. Similarly, [500] introduces extremely low-dimensional semantic bottleneck features processed by a multi-resolution hash encoder, achieving significant improvements in runtime and memory efficiency for large-scale scenes. Codebook-based approaches offer another compelling alternative by learning a shared set of semantic primitives, or "words," to compose semantics. For example, [501] introduces a cross-attention module with learnable codebooks that encode view-independent semantic embeddings, ensuring intra-instance feature similarity while reducing memory. In the domain of 3D segmentation, [502] employs a Contrastive Codebook Learning module to distill discriminative semantic features by enforcing intra-class compactness and inter-class distinctiveness. The integrate-and-fire model of codebooks is pushed further in [503], which encodes each Gaussian’s category with a single integer via binary-to-decimal mapping, drastically reducing memory and enabling a progressive training strategy for fine-grained segmentation.

Given the semantic sparsity in a scene, a major insight is that per-Gaussian high-dimensional features are often unnecessary. This has led to the decoupling of geometry and semantics, a theme explored in several cutting-edge works. [504] capitalizes on this by using a sparse set of semantic Gaussians to represent language information, using only 5% of the language embeddings compared to dense methods. The "extrinsic paradigm" represents a bold step in this direction, as exemplified by [505]. Instead of embedding features, ExtrinSplat clusters Gaussians into 3D object groups and uses a Vision-Language Model to generate lightweight textual hypotheses, creating an extrinsic index layer that supports polysemy and reduces adaptation time from hours to minutes. This is complemented by object-level aggregation methods like [506], which bypass rendering for semantics by using predecomposed object-level Gaussians and aggregating multiview CLIP features into a "bag of embeddings" per object. A training-free framework for scalable retrieval is presented in [507], which decouples semantic understanding from geometric representation by using an instance-level query mechanism and lifting 2D masks into 3D, enabling efficient retrieval on city-scale scenes.

To further improve fidelity, some methods align language features with surface geometry. [508] introduces a Language-Embedded Surface Field (LangSurf) that flattens language Gaussians onto object surfaces using geometry supervision, creating a more accurate 3D language field aligned with object boundaries. This is complemented by a Hierarchical-Context Awareness Module for extracting fine-grained features from SAM masks at different hierarchies. Finally, the concept of hierarchical understanding is crucial for a complete semantic representation. [509] achieves view-consistent 3D hierarchical semantics by constructing a 3D semantic tree with layered instance clustering, capturing both instance-wise and part-wise information. [510] similarly fosters a cohesive, context-aware hierarchical scene representation by disentangling segmentation and language field distillation, using "super-Gaussians" to lift and distill 2D language features efficiently. These hierarchical and geometry-aware representations not only refine the quality of the embedded semantics but also seamlessly bridge into the subsequent stage of open-vocabulary and instance-level segmentation, where such multi-scale, structured features are directly leveraged for object discovery and language-driven querying. In summary, the field of semantic feature distillation has evolved from naive feature injection to sophisticated methods involving compression, decoupling, codebook learning, hierarchical structuring, and a deeper understanding of the geometry-semantics relationship, addressing the core challenges of consistency, efficiency, and fidelity.

### 8.2 Open-Vocabulary Segmentation and Object-Level Perception

Open-vocabulary and instance-level segmentation within 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) represents a critical frontier in scene understanding, moving beyond closed-set paradigms toward flexible, language-driven perception. Building upon the semantic feature distillation techniques discussed previously, which establish the representational substrate of language-aligned features within Gaussian primitives, this subsection addresses the higher-level challenge of organizing those features into discrete, queryable entities. The explicit, primitive-based nature of 3DGS provides a unique substrate for these tasks, enabling methods that range from class-agnostic grouping to hierarchical semantic decomposition. A fundamental challenge lies in reconciling the continuous, appearance-driven nature of Gaussian primitives with the discrete, semantic entities that define objects and their parts.

A dominant paradigm in this area is the aggregation of class-agnostic masks followed by semantic labeling, which directly leverages the distilled feature fields described in the previous subsection. Early approaches in this vein often rely on distilling features from 2D foundation models into the 3D representation. For instance, the method proposed in [511] introduces a zero-shot approach that first generates class-agnostic 3D instance masks, then aggregates per-mask features via multi-view fusion of CLIP-based image embeddings. This decoupling of geometric grouping from semantic classification allows the system to segment arbitrary objects, including those in the long-tail distribution, by querying the aggregated features with free-form text. Building on this idea, some methods have proposed decoupled pipelines to enhance modularity and adaptability. The DCSEG framework [512] learns class-agnostic features through contrastive supervision from a 2D instance proposal network, clusters these features into coarse 3D masks, and then assigns semantic labels using a 2D open-vocabulary segmentation model. This design not only provides a plug-and-play interface for swapping different modules but also ensures multi-object instance segmentation without extra cost, leveraging rich 3D geometry for robust scene understanding. A key insight from such decoupled systems is the recognition that the semantic quality of the final segmentation is heavily dependent on the chosen 2D backbone, highlighting a trade-off between flexibility and fidelity.

To address the limitations of purely feature-embedding paradigms, recent work has proposed an "extrinsic" paradigm that decouples geometry from semantics at a more fundamental level, resonating with the decoupling strategies introduced in the previous subsection. The ExtrinSplat framework [505] argues that embedding high-dimensional features directly into Gaussians leads to geometry-semantic inconsistency, semantic bloat, and rigidity. Instead, it clusters Gaussians into multi-granularity, overlapping 3D object groups. A Vision-Language Model (VLM) then interprets these groups to generate lightweight textual hypotheses, creating an extrinsic index layer. This approach natively supports polysemy and dramatically reduces storage overhead and scene adaptation time, outperforming embedding-based frameworks on open-vocabulary object selection and semantic segmentation tasks.

For achieving precise instance-level discrimination, many methods adopt bottom-up aggregation strategies that merge smaller geometric or semantic units. The use of superpoints, which are geometrically and semantically coherent regions, has proven particularly effective. PanoGS [513] proposes language-guided graph cuts to group Gaussian primitives into super-primitives, which are then clustered into 3D-consistent instances using graph clustering with SAM-guided edge affinity. This synergy between geometric structure and language cues addresses the challenge of distinguishing instances in complex scenes. Expanding on this, training-free methods have been developed to construct superpoint graphs directly from Gaussian primitives, aligning with the efficiency-focused trends in feature representation. The THGS framework [514] partitions the scene into spatially compact and semantically coherent regions, forming view-consistent 3D entities. An efficient reprojection strategy lifts 2D semantic features onto these superpoints, avoiding costly multi-view iterative training. This representation naturally supports hierarchical understanding, enabling both coarse- and fine-grained open-vocabulary perception and achieving state-of-the-art segmentation performance with significantly faster semantic field reconstruction.

Beyond mere object-level segmentation, the field is increasingly addressing the challenge of part-level and hierarchical understanding, which directly sets the stage for the scene-level reasoning and graph-based querying discussed in the following subsection. This requires methods that can reason across multiple scales of granularity. The H2G framework [515] introduces a hyperbolic affinity field for hierarchical 3D grouping. By interpreting foundation-model affinities through Dasgupta's objective for similarity-based hierarchical clustering, it derives semantically organized tree supervision. This supervision is distilled into a single Lorentz hyperbolic feature field, which is well-suited for representing tree-like branching structures, allowing for semantic hierarchical grouping grounded in 2D foundation-model knowledge. This approach moves beyond flat object-centric segmentation to recover scene groups from fine object parts to complete objects. Similarly, the Search3D method [516] constructs hierarchical open-vocabulary 3D scene representations, enabling 3D search at multiple levels of granularity, including fine-grained object parts, entire objects, or regions described by attributes like materials. By shifting toward a more flexible search paradigm, it moves beyond explicit object-centric queries to support a wider range of user intents, bridging the gap toward the interactive querying paradigms of the next subsection.

The integration of large language models (LLMs) and multimodal models is a major trend driving progress in this area, particularly for referring expression grounding and complex query understanding, foreshadowing the reasoning capabilities explored in the subsequent subsection. A key limitation of methods relying solely on CLIP is their difficulty with compositional and functional queries. To address this, OpenTrack3D [517] replaces the CLIP-based classifier with a multimodal large language model (MLLM), significantly enhancing compositional reasoning for complex user queries. This is combined with a novel visual-spatial tracker that constructs cross-view consistent object proposals online from an RGB-D stream, making the pipeline applicable to mesh-free and unstructured environments. The broader impact of MLLMs is evident in works like Group3D [518], which integrates semantic constraints directly into the instance construction process for open-vocabulary 3D detection. By maintaining a scene-adaptive vocabulary derived from an MLLM and organizing it into semantic compatibility groups, the method ensures that 3D fragments are merged only when they satisfy both semantic compatibility and geometric consistency, mitigating over-merging and fragmentation errors.

In the context of open-ended settings, where predefined class names are absent, methods must be capable of discovering and naming objects autonomously. The OE3DIS method [519] formalizes this as a novel problem, eliminating the need for predefined class names during testing. By leveraging 2D multimodal large language models, it aims to generate both the geometric masks and their associated class names, evaluated with an open-ended score that assesses both semantic and geometric quality. This represents a shift towards truly autonomous scene understanding, laying the groundwork for the autonomous reasoning and embodied interaction discussed in the following subsection.

Finally, addressing the challenge of semantic inconsistency and granularity mismatch across views is crucial for robust segmentation, a concern that also permeates the feature distillation methods of the previous subsection. The CAGS framework [495] tackles the issue of cross-view granularity inconsistency caused by 2D segmentation models like SAM, where an object may be segmented as a single entity in one view and multiple parts in another. By constructing local graphs to propagate contextual features across Gaussians and employing mask-centric contrastive learning, CAGS smooths SAM-derived features and reduces fragmentation errors, enabling more cohesive object reasoning. Similarly, COS3D [520] proposes a collaborative prompt-segmentation framework that integrates complementary language and segmentation cues through a collaborative field comprising an instance field and a language field. Its adaptive language-to-instance prompt refinement promotes high-quality prompt-segmentation inference, demonstrating leading performance on standard benchmarks and showing potential for applications like hierarchical segmentation and robotics, thus providing the structured, entity-level understanding that serves as the foundation for the scene graph construction and reasoning techniques to follow.

### 8.3 Scene Understanding, Reasoning, and Interactive Querying

**8.3 Scene Understanding, Reasoning, and Interactive Querying**

Building upon the capabilities of open-vocabulary segmentation and semantic-aware editing, the field of 3D Gaussian Splatting is now advancing toward higher-level scene understanding, reasoning, and interactive querying. While the embedding of features and the segmentation of objects enable perception, true spatial intelligence requires the ability to infer relationships, answer complex queries, and ground natural language instructions in the structured representation of a 3D environment. This subsection examines how 3DGS is being integrated with scene graphs, object attributes, and spatial relationships to facilitate high-level reasoning and interactive querying, spanning from language-guided retrieval to embodied navigation and manipulation.

A central theme in this domain is the construction of hierarchical 3D scene graphs (3DSGs) that organize the environment at multiple levels of abstraction, moving beyond object-centric maps to include rooms, floors, and abstract concepts. Frameworks like [521] leverage Large Language Models to annotate nodes across these hierarchies, creating rich metric-semantic representations. For large environments, efficient management of these graphs is essential, especially as their serialization can easily exceed the context windows of LLMs. To address this, [522] proposes a hierarchical graph structure with keyframe-selected multi-modal information, coupled with a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline to extract relevant context, enabling the processing of complex queries without overwhelming the reasoning model. Relatedly, the work on [523] introduces an asynchronous dual-stream architecture that incrementally builds a RAG-ready knowledge base, decoupling geometric segmentation from semantic reasoning to efficiently handle complex embodied queries. This approach underscores the importance of scalable and updatable knowledge structures for practical navigation and interaction.

The integration of scene graphs with large language models forms the basis of powerful reasoning pipelines. For instance, [524] constructs a learnable representation of a 3D scene graph that explicitly incorporates semantic relationships and feeds it into an LLM for vision-language tasks, demonstrating that relational information improves grounding performance over purely geometric approaches. Similarly, [525] tackles the scalability issue by proposing a structured interface—specifically, the Cypher query language—as a tool for LLMs to retrieve task-relevant information from a graph database. This approach significantly outperforms naive serialization of the scene graph for instruction following and question answering, showcasing a scalable pattern for grounding language in complex environments.

To bridge the gap between abstract reasoning and physical perception, methods have been developed to ground natural language commands directly into the scene graph structure. [526] investigates the impact of adding open- or closed-vocabulary spatial relations to 3DSGs for grounding natural language commands. Their study reveals that explicit spatial relations improve the ability of LLMs to ground objects, highlighting the value of relational information in disambiguating targets. Extending this to real-world navigation, [527] constructs a multi-level semantic scene graph, termed an "Embodied Graph," that integrates map data with object entities to serve as a retrievable knowledge base for semantic navigation. This system explicitly handles dynamic objects across varying spatial granularities, demonstrating the robustness of 3DSG-based reasoning in challenging outdoor environments. The importance of relational knowledge is further emphasized in [528], which learns to generate 3D semantic relations and demonstrates that modeling object relations improves search efficiency in real-world navigation tasks.

Addressing the inherent ambiguity of natural language is another key challenge. Approaches like [529] propose a modular system (BBQ) that constructs a 3D scene graph with metric and semantic edges, enabling the reasoning over complex queries that involve spatial relations, a task where simpler CLIP-based methods fail. Building on this foundation, [530] formalizes an Open-Vocabulary 3D Scene Graph (OVSG) framework for grounding a variety of entities based on free-form queries, facilitating contextual localization such as "the cup on the kitchen table." For more intricate, multi-step reasoning, [531] (OVIGo-3DHSG) and [532] integrate hierarchical scene graphs with LLMs to handle queries involving spatial reference chains. This integration leverages inter- and intra-layer connections to enhance spatial context understanding and enable multistep reasoning for complex instructions.

Recent trends also highlight a move towards more dynamic, interactive, and physically grounded representations. For example, [533] presents a hybrid instance-aware 3D Gaussian dynamic scene graph system, integrating a probabilistic voxel grid with explicit Gaussians for robust instance fusion, and developing a "3D Gaussian Mind" that couples structural relations with spatial-semantic information for multimodal reasoning. The multi-modal aspect is also explored in [534], which argues that text-only relational edges lose critical visual evidence. Their M3DSG preserves visual cues by using dynamically assigned images as relational edges, and the system demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on challenging navigation benchmarks. Furthermore, the integration of physics is a growing area of interest. [535] unifies symbolic reasoning with structured 3D geometry to model kinematic and physical properties, inferring materials and articulations for downstream tasks like affordance prediction. Similarly, [536] and [537] demonstrate the construction of semantic-kinematic 3D scene graphs that encode actionable articulation information, enabling robots to not only see but also physically interact with their environment. These works collectively show a trajectory from static semantic mapping toward dynamic, physically-aware, and interactive 3D scene representations, positioning 3DGS as a central technology for next-generation embodied AI.

### 8.4 Semantic-Aware Editing and Manipulation

Semantic-aware editing and manipulation represent one of the most active and transformative application areas of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS), leveraging the explicit, primitive-based nature of the representation to enable granular control over scene content that was previously difficult to achieve with implicit methods like NeRF. Unlike implicit representations, where editing requires navigating a continuous function, 3DGS allows for direct manipulation of individual Gaussians, facilitating a wide range of operations from simple recolorization to complex geometric deformations. This capability forms the practical foundation for the higher-level reasoning and interactive querying discussed in the previous subsection: while scene graphs and language models provide semantic understanding, editing tools translate that understanding into actionable, user-driven modifications of the environment. Research in this area is broadly characterized by text-guided editing, drag-based manipulation, stylization, and interactive selection tools, often working in concert to deliver a seamless and intuitive user experience.

A dominant paradigm for text-guided editing adopts a render-edit-optimize pipeline, where images are rendered from a 3DGS scene, edited using a 2D diffusion model, and then used to fine-tune the 3D representation. A critical challenge in this pipeline is ensuring multi-view consistency, as independent 2D edits can introduce conflicting information across views, leading to artifacts and mode collapse. To address this, frameworks like EditSplat propose a Multi-view Fusion Guidance (MFG) module to incorporate multi-view information directly into the diffusion process, alongside an Attention-Guided Trimming (AGT) strategy that leverages the explicit nature of 3DGS to prune irrelevant Gaussians, enhancing optimization efficiency and enabling precise local edits [538]. Similarly, GaussCtrl achieves multi-view consistency by using depth-conditioned editing, which enforces geometric consistency across views by leveraging the natural consistency of depth maps, and by employing attention-based latent code alignment to unify the appearance of edited images [539]. Another approach, VcEdit, incorporates a Cross-attention Consistency Module and an Editing Consistency Module to reduce inconsistencies in the guidance images themselves, thereby mitigating mode collapse during the subsequent 3D optimization [540]. For more localized control, TIGER proposes a bottom-up language aggregation strategy to create denser language-embedded Gaussians for accurate retrieval, and a Coherent Score Distillation (CSD) that combines 2D and multi-view diffusion models to produce consistent edits with finer details [541].

Beyond simple appearance changes, enabling geometric modifications such as addition, removal, replacement, and repositioning is a key goal. GaussianEditor enhanced precision by introducing Gaussian semantic tracing to track the editing target, Hierarchical Gaussian splatting (HGS) for stable results, and editing strategies for efficient object removal and integration [542]. A more streamlined, fully 3D-based paradigm is proposed by 3DSceneEditor, which operates directly on Gaussians using a pre-trained instance segmentation model and zero-shot grounding with CLIP to align target objects with user prompts, enabling operations like object addition, repositioning, recoloring, replacing, and removal without the need for iterative 2D-to-3D projections [543]. To overcome the limitations of text prompts alone, some methods integrate generative models more deeply. For instance, SVGS uses a single-view editing strategy grounded in multi-view diffusion models, reconstructing 3D scenes from consistent views to balance editing consistency and efficiency [544]. For complex, multi-part scenes, custom frameworks have been developed for specific tasks, such as G^2Editor for driving videos, which uses a 3D Gaussian representation of the object as a dense prior for pose control and a scene-level 3D bounding box layout to reconstruct occluded areas, supporting object repositioning, insertion, and deletion in a unified framework [545].

Another major strand of research focuses on drag-based editing, which provides a more intuitive and spatially precise alternative to text prompts for geometric deformation. These methods often rely on user-specified control points and masks to define the edit. 3DGS-Drag bridges the gap between deformation-based and 2D-editing-based methods by leveraging deformation guidance for consistent geometric modifications and diffusion guidance for content correction, enabling a wide range of edits including motion change, shape adjustment, and inpainting [546]. DragGaussian also enables drag-style manipulation on pre-trained 3D Gaussian object models, using diffusion models for multi-view consistent editing [547]. Building on the concept of as-rigid-as-possible (ARAP) deformation, ARAP-GS directly applies ARAP constraints to 3D Gaussians, preserving shape coherence and visual continuity during drag-driven geometric transformations, and incorporates a diffusion prior to enhance visual quality [548]. Similarly, DYG integrates an implicit triplane representation as a geometric scaffold to overcome the sparsity of 3DGS in editing regions and incorporates a drag-based Latent Diffusion Model to enable flexible, multi-view consistent editing [549]. To handle more aggressive and topology-changing edits, MVDrag3D employs a multi-view diffusion model as a generative prior, followed by a reconstruction model to create 3D Gaussians, and uses view-specific deformation networks to correct misalignments [550]. For object-level edits in images that can be lifted to 3D, ObjectMorpher lifts target instances into editable 3DGS using an image-to-3D generator, allowing fast manipulation via control points with ARAP constraints for non-rigid deformations [551].

In addition to text and drag, stylization and reference-based editing form an important category. ReGS adapts 3DGS for reference-based stylization by introducing a texture-guided control mechanism that adaptively adjusts local Gaussians to a new geometric arrangement, guided by texture clues and regularized by scene depth to preserve the original geometry [552]. For more generalized and creative edits, frameworks often incorporate 2D diffusion models to generate novel content, but must carefully manage the editing region to preserve the scene. The Mastering Regional 3DGS method leverages 2D diffusion editing to identify modification regions, followed by inverse rendering for 3D localization and a refined coarse 3DGS initialization to support iterative, view-consistent editing [553]. Similarly, GSEditPro introduces an attention-based progressive localization module to add semantic labels to each Gaussian during rendering, enabling precise localization of editing areas based on their relevance to the editing prompts [554]. To preserve non-target regions and reduce edit leakage, TransSplat formulates language-driven editing as a multi-view unbalanced semantic transport problem, explicitly characterizing the semantic relationship between edited 2D evidence and 3D Gaussians and using transport residuals to suppress erroneous edits [555].

Beyond static scenes, semantic-aware editing is increasingly applied to dynamic scenes and 4D representations, extending the scope of manipulation from spatial structures to spatio-temporal coherence. SemDynReg uses SAM to extract semantic features and construct an object-ID map, guiding a semantics-aware regularization that imposes consistency constraints on the deformation parameters of Gaussians belonging to the same object, improving object-level deformation consistency in dynamic 3DGS rendering [556]. Mono4DEditor extends text-driven editing to 4D scenes reconstructed from monocular video by augmenting 3D Gaussians with quantized CLIP features, enabling point-level localization and targeted edits using diffusion-based video editing with flow and scribble guidance for temporal coherence [557]. For more complex and interactive control, 4DGS-Craft introduces a 4D-aware InstructPix2Pix model that incorporates 4D VGGT geometry features to ensure view and temporal consistency, along with a Gaussian selection mechanism to preserve non-edited regions and an LLM-based module for user intent understanding to decompose complex instructions into atomic operations [558]. Similarly, Catalyst4D transfers high-quality 3D edits to dynamic 4D Gaussian scenes using an Anchor-based Motion Guidance (AMG) module that establishes correspondences between original and edited Gaussians via optimal transport, and a Color Uncertainty-guided Appearance Refinement (CUAR) module to preserve temporal consistency [559].

Finally, the development of interactive and AI-assisted selection tools is crucial for practical Gaussian-level control, serving as the user-facing interface that bridges the gap between high-level editing intents and the underlying primitive-based representation. ArtisanGS presents an interactive suite of tools that combines a fast AI-driven method for propagating user-guided 2D selection masks to 3DGS with flexible manual selection and segmentation tools, enabling users to achieve virtually any binary segmentation of an unstructured 3DGS scene for downstream editing tasks [560]. The importance of accurate 3D masks is underscored by work on robust part-level editing. RoMaP proposes a robust 3D mask generation module using spherical harmonics to model view-dependent label variations and a regularized Score Distillation Sampling (SDS) loss with an anchor loss to confine modifications to the target region while preserving context [561]. Similarly, AG²aussian uses an anchor-graph structure to organize semantic features, enabling clean and accurate instance-level Gaussian selection for interactive click-based queries, object removal, and physics simulation [562]. Furthermore, TASE projects pretrained 2D semantic features into a truncation-aware embedding space, enabling text-driven edits with explicit control over how strongly edits adhere to the original scene content [563]. Look Before You Edit introduces a method for attention-guided camera placement and multi-view alignment, probing the diffusion model’s attention to find the optimal distance for editing cameras and using multi-view attention alignment to ensure the same edit is applied consistently across views [564]. These tools and techniques collectively position 3DGS as a highly versatile and controllable medium for semantic-aware editing, blurring the lines between professional content creation tools and intuitive, user-friendly interfaces, and providing the executable substrate upon which the scene understanding and reasoning capabilities described in the previous subsection can be deployed for real-world interaction.

## 9 Inverse Rendering, Relighting, and Advanced Appearance Modeling

### 9.1 Physically-Based Shading and Material Decomposition in Gaussian Splatting

Physically-based inverse rendering within the 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) framework represents a significant paradigm shift from view synthesis to the disentanglement of intrinsic scene properties. The core objective is to jointly recover geometry, material attributes (such as albedo, metallic, and roughness), and illumination from multi-view images, enabling downstream applications like relighting and material editing. This subsection reviews the fundamental extensions that have enabled this decomposition, focusing on shading pipelines, approximation strategies, and the integration of physically-based rendering objectives.

A primary challenge in adapting 3DGS for inverse rendering is that the standard rasterization pipeline is designed for forward shading, where the final color is computed per-primitive and composited via alpha-blending. This approach is insufficient for physically-based decomposition because it entangles lighting and material properties within the view-dependent spherical harmonic coefficients. To address this, a significant body of work has adopted deferred shading pipelines. In these frameworks, the rasterization pass renders geometric and material attributes—such as normals, albedo, and roughness—into intermediate G-buffers. A subsequent physically-based shading stage then computes the final pixel color using a rendering equation that explicitly models light transport. This separation is crucial as it allows for the independent optimization of material parameters and lighting while maintaining the real-time rendering capabilities of 3DGS. For instance, DeferredGS decouples texture and lighting by modeling illumination via a learnable environment map and defining attributes like texture parameters and normal direction directly on Gaussians, enabling more realistic relighting through deferred shading [565]. Similarly, Phys3DGS identifies problems incurred by hidden Gaussians in standard volume rendering and proposes adopting deferred rendering alongside a hybrid mesh-Gaussian representation to improve inverse rendering quality [566].

To make the rendering equation tractable for optimization, many methods employ the split-sum approximation for specular reflections. This technique approximates the integral of the Bidirectional Reflectance Distribution Function (BRDF) and the environment lighting into a pre-filtered environment map and a separable BRDF shading term, significantly simplifying the computation. Relightable 3D Gaussian represents the 3D scene as a set of relightable 3D Gaussian points, where each point is associated with normal direction, BRDF parameters, and incident lights, optimizing the scene through 3D Gaussian Splatting while decomposing BRDF and lighting via physically-based differentiable rendering with an innovative point-based ray-tracing approach for visibility baking [567]. Building on this, GS-IR extends 3DGS to estimate scene geometry, surface material, and environment illumination by proposing an efficient optimization scheme that incorporates depth-derivation-based regularization for normal estimation and a baking-based occlusion method to model indirect lighting [568]. Furthermore, the use of 2D Gaussian surfels, as opposed to volumetric 3D Gaussians, has been shown to improve surface alignment and material decomposition [569]. For example, MaterialClusterGS represents scene materials using a compact global palette of shared BRDF prototypes assigned via a continuous spatial material field, jointly optimizing the material field, palette prototypes, and environment lighting under a physically-based rendering objective, which recovers compact and spatially coherent attributes for editing and relighting [570].

However, the simplified shading models described above are inherently limited by their reliance on direct lighting and simplified visibility assumptions. They often fail to capture complex light transport phenomena such as inter-reflection, self-occlusion, and global illumination, which are crucial for achieving photorealistic relighting. Beyond the split-sum approximation, a body of work integrates Monte Carlo path tracing to model the full rendering equation, including inter-reflection and global illumination. This approach provides more physically accurate light transport at the cost of increased computational complexity. GI-GS calculates indirect lighting using efficient path tracing with deferred shading, rendering a G-buffer first to capture geometry and material properties, then performing physically-based rendering for direct lighting and using a lightweight path tracing method for indirect lighting, which achieves better novel view synthesis and relighting under any given lighting conditions [571]. Similarly, IRGS leverages differentiable 2D Gaussian ray tracing to compute incident radiance on the fly, applying the full rendering equation without simplification and demonstrating the capability to accurately model complex inter-reflection effects [572]. However, the variance from Monte Carlo sampling poses a significant challenge for gradient-based optimization. To mitigate this, PTIR-GS proposes a splatting-free path-traced inverse rendering framework where forward light transport and backward gradient propagation are defined within a unified ray-tracing pipeline, ensuring consistency between the forward and backward passes and improving material inversion [573]. Another strategy, employed by GOGS, uses a two-stage framework based on 2D Gaussian surfels, where the first stage establishes robust surface reconstruction using split-sum approximation and geometric priors, and the second stage performs material decomposition by leveraging Monte Carlo importance sampling of the full rendering equation, modeling indirect illumination via differentiable 2D Gaussian ray tracing [574]. A more holistic approach to inter-reflection is presented in IRGS++, which introduces a unified robust and efficient Gaussian inverse rendering framework that employs differentiable 2D Gaussian ray tracing for visibility and indirect radiance queries during transport-aware optimization, making the physical core of Gaussian inverse rendering more grounded [575].

The parameterization of materials within Gaussian primitives is another area of active research, involving the assignment of spatially-varying BRDF properties. These properties are often fine-tuned per-primitive, which can be under-constrained. To address this, OMG introduces a novel approach that augments the opacity term by introducing a neural network that takes material properties as input, providing a physically correct activation function based on radiative transfer principles. This adds a dependency of opacity on material cross-section, facilitating constraint for material optimization and improving material modeling across different baselines [576]. In contrast, spatially-varying representations have been developed to allow per-Gaussian spatially varying parameters, addressing the limitation of constant material parameters in each Gaussian. SVG-IR proposes a Spatially-varying Gaussian (SVG) representation with a SVG splatting scheme akin to vertex/fragment shading, which, when combined with a physically-based indirect lighting model, significantly improves relighting quality by allowing for more detailed material and normal variation across a single primitive [577]. Furthermore, the use of explicit geometry guidance can enhance light transport modeling. GeoSplatting augments 3DGS by differentiably constructing a surface-grounded 3DGS from an optimizable mesh, leveraging well-defined mesh normals and the opaque mesh surface to facilitate mesh-based ray tracing for efficient, occlusion-aware light transport calculations [578]. Finally, the need for robustness in sparse-view settings is addressed by GAINS, which introduces a two-stage inverse rendering framework that leverages foundation models as priors to stabilize geometry and material estimation, unifying these priors with physically-based representations in an optimization scheme to improve material parameter accuracy under sparse captures [579].

In summary, the field of physically-based shading and material decomposition within 3DGS is characterized by a progression from simplified shading models to more physically faithful light transport simulations. The evolution from forward to deferred shading pipelines has been pivotal in enabling the disentanglement of scene properties. Current state-of-the-art methods integrate advanced techniques like split-sum approximations, Monte Carlo ray tracing, and complex material parameterizations, all unified under differentiable objectives that allow for high-quality reconstruction, relighting, and material editing. The challenge remains to balance the computational cost of physically-based light transport with the real-time capabilities of 3DGS, a balance that continues to drive innovation in hybrid and adaptive rendering strategies. These fundamental decomposition techniques provide the essential substrate upon which the more sophisticated illumination modeling approaches—spanning HDR environment estimation, explicit emitter representation, and global illumination inference—are built, as explored in the following subsection.

### 9.2 Advanced Lighting Estimation, Global Illumination, and Indirect Transport Modeling

Advanced lighting estimation and global illumination modeling represent one of the most active and challenging frontiers in 3D Gaussian Splatting research. While the base 3DGS framework excels at novel view synthesis through view-dependent spherical harmonics, it fundamentally bakes illumination into the radiance field, preventing relighting, material editing, and physically consistent scene manipulation. Building upon the physically-based shading and material decomposition techniques discussed in the previous subsection—which established the disentanglement of geometry, materials, and lighting—this subsection surveys the sophisticated illumination modeling techniques that complete this decomposition by explicitly estimating and simulating the lighting itself. These techniques span HDR environment estimation, explicit emitter representations, spatially-varying lighting fields, and the critical challenge of indirect illumination through path tracing, radiance transfer fields, and inter-reflection modeling.

**HDR Environment Map Estimation and Lighting Decomposition.** A foundational step toward relightable 3DGS is the estimation of high-dynamic-range illumination that can be separated from scene materials. Early approaches in this direction leverage parametric lighting models to capture scene energy. [580] bridges the gap between parametric light models and realistic panorama generation by combining spherical Gaussian parameters with GAN-based LDR panorama extrapolation, yielding HDRI-ready lighting that supports editable light direction, intensity, and count. For street scenes, [581] proposes a hybrid representation containing an HDR sky dome for extreme solar intensity alongside a volumetric lighting model for spatially-varying appearance, enabling differentiable virtual object insertion. More recently, [582] introduces the concept of using HDR Gaussian Splats themselves as light source representations, marking the first time regular images serve directly as light sources in a 3D renderer through a two-stage process of diffusion-based dynamic range enhancement followed by 3D lighting modeling. In the domain of outdoor scenes, [583] employs a two-stage pipeline where monocular normal priors guide 2D Gaussian Splatting geometry reconstruction, followed by a hybrid lighting model combining spherical Gaussian functions for high-frequency sunlight with spherical harmonics for low-frequency skylight, achieving state-of-the-art relighting accuracy for unbounded outdoor environments. Similarly, [298] addresses the exposure inconsistency problem in autonomous driving data by jointly decomposing a view-invariant linear HDR radiance field, per-view exposure scales, and tone-mapping functions directly from LDR images, enforcing relative-exposure consistency and HDR-domain regularization based on the physical image-formation process.

**Explicit Area Emitters and Spatially-Varying Lighting.** A key limitation of environment-map-based approaches is their inability to model local light sources with physical spatial extent. [584] directly tackles this by explicitly modeling local area emitters within a relightable Gaussian Splatting representation, integrating multiple importance sampling with targeted regularization in a differentiable deferred rendering pipeline to accurately simulate local light transport, produce correct light attenuation and realistic shadows, and enhance downstream tasks including controlled relighting and virtual object insertion in scenes with complex local lighting. For indoor scenes with spatially varying illumination, [585] proposes a Volumetric Spherical Gaussian representation that parameterizes exitant radiance on a voxel grid, paired with a physics-based differentiable renderer that formulates energy-conserving image formation for joint training of albedo, normals, depth, and lighting without requiring ground-truth HDR data. In the context of large-scale indoor environments, the Texture-based Lighting (TBL) representation introduced in [586] combines 3D mesh with HDR textures to efficiently model direct and infinite-bounce indirect lighting of entire scenes, complemented by a hybrid lighting representation with precomputed irradiance to improve optimization efficiency and reduce rendering noise.

**Path Tracing and Global Illumination Integration.** Modeling indirect illumination—light that reaches a surface after one or more bounces—remains the most challenging aspect of inverse rendering, as it requires evaluating visibility and secondary light transport. This challenge connects directly to the Monte Carlo path tracing and inter-reflection techniques introduced for material decomposition in the previous subsection, where methods like GI-GS and PTIR-GS were first discussed for their role in improving specular and indirect light estimation during decomposition. Here, we focus on their broader implications for illumination modeling in its own right. Several methods have pushed 3DGS toward physically grounded global illumination. [571] renders a G-buffer to capture geometry and material properties, performs PBR only for direct lighting, then computes indirect lighting through lightweight path tracing using the G-buffer and previous rendering results, effectively modeling indirect illumination under arbitrary lighting conditions. [573] goes further by defining a splatting-free path-traced inverse rendering framework where both forward light transport and backward gradient propagation operate within a unified ray-tracing pipeline, introducing a path-space equivalent interaction model for overlapping Gaussian primitives that makes Monte-Carlo path tracing unbiased for the induced light-transport integral while replaying pathwise gradients over the same ray-traced interactions. This pipeline consistency, together with the full rendering equation and ray-traced visibility, yields more plausible shadows, reflections, and relighting under global illumination. For dynamic scenes, [587] formulates a surface light transport model for 3D Gaussians and combines a fast compound stochastic ray-tracing algorithm with an optimized rasterizer, achieving over 40 fps with interactive material editing and dynamic multi-light setups while capturing mutual multi-bounce transport between Gaussians and meshes.

**Inter-Reflection and Radiance Transfer Modeling.** Inter-reflections among multiple objects introduce higher-order lighting effects that simple direct illumination models cannot capture. While the previous subsection reviewed inter-reflection methods such as IRGS and IRGS++ primarily from the perspective of decomposing materials from reflections, this subsection re-examines these same techniques through the lens of illumination estimation itself—where they serve as essential tools for recovering the incident radiance field that characterizes a scene's lighting environment. [572] applies the full rendering equation without simplification, computing incident radiance on the fly through differentiable 2D Gaussian ray tracing, supported by an efficient optimization scheme for Monte Carlo sampling and a novel strategy for querying indirect radiance during relighting. Its successor, [575], extends this with metallic-aware material modeling, robust reflective initialization for glossy and specular surfaces, multiple importance sampling and denoising for finite-sample rendering stability, and mesh-based secondary-attribute queries to reduce relighting costs. The concept of radiance transfer fields is explored in [588], which combines forward rendering for radiance transfer with deferred rendering for reflections to separate high-frequency and low-frequency appearances, mitigating floating artifacts from spherical harmonic overfitting while refining BRDF and lighting decomposition. [589] takes a different approach, locating surface points through refined sphere tracing and explicitly tracing incoming indirect lights based on reflection, estimating each identified light through an efficient neural network and employing Leibniz's integral rule to resolve non-differentiability at boundary lights. [590] introduces Monte Carlo sampling-based path tracing with neural radiance caching for indirect illumination, using spherical Gaussians for smooth environment illumination and a radiance consistency constraint between implicit neural radiance and path tracing results of unobserved rays to improve inter-reflection decomposition. For scenes with participating media, [591] estimates direct illumination via ray tracing and computes indirect illumination with spherical harmonics, avoiding lengthy indirect bounces while preventing energy loss, and [592] formalizes kernel-based volumetric primitives with closed-form transmittance and free-flight distance sampling within any off-the-shelf volumetric path tracer, supporting full path-tracing functionality including scattering, relighting, and complex camera models.
