Multi-bounce path tracing with reservoir sampling is explored in [593], which extracts explicit triangular mesh geometry and employs Monte Carlo integration with reservoir sampling to handle noise in the estimation of self-shadowing and internal reflections. In the co-located light-camera setup, [594] integrates neural implicit surfaces with a neural radiance cache for global illumination approximation, introducing a dynamic radiance cache that adapts to sharp lighting discontinuities from near-field motion and a surface-angle-weighted radiometric loss to suppress specular artifacts.

**Hybrid Rasterization-Ray Tracing Approaches.** Given the complementary strengths of real-time rasterization and physically accurate ray tracing, a number of hybrid approaches have emerged. These approaches parallel the hybrid mesh-Gaussian strategies discussed in the previous subsection for material decomposition (e.g., GeoSplatting), but here the focus shifts toward leveraging this hybridity for efficient and accurate illumination estimation. [595] uses a neural field for primary rays while employing an explicit mesh reconstructed from the underlying field for secondary rays that produce cast shadows and higher-order lighting effects, enabling photorealistic relighting with specular and shadow effects on outdoor datasets. [578] augment 3DGS with explicit mesh guidance by differentiably constructing a surface-grounded 3DGS, leveraging well-defined mesh normals and opaque surfaces to facilitate mesh-based ray tracing for occlusion-aware light transport. [596] combines pre-trained neural fields with a Neural Radiance Cache for indirect illumination, using differentiable marching tetrahedrons initialized from the SDF and single-bounce differentiable MC ray tracing to jointly optimize geometry, material, and light. [575] also adopts a hybrid strategy where mesh-based secondary-attribute queries reduce the cost of relighting under novel illumination while rasterization handles primary visibility. From a different angle, [597] proposes a generalizable 3D light transport embedding that approximates global illumination directly from 3D scene configurations without rasterized or path-traced cues, using a scalable transformer to model global point-to-point interactions and cross-attention for query aggregation at render time, with applicability extending to path guiding for glossy materials. These hybrid approaches demonstrate that the future of high-quality inverse rendering lies not in a single rendering paradigm but in the principled combination of complementary techniques, balancing physical accuracy with computational efficiency for practical deployment across indoor, outdoor, urban, and dynamic scenarios.

In summary, this subsection has traversed the landscape of advanced illumination modeling within 3DGS, from HDR environment estimation and explicit emitter representations to the intricate modeling of global illumination and inter-reflections. Together with the physically-based shading and material decomposition techniques established in the previous subsection, these illumination modeling approaches form the two pillars of inverse rendering in the 3DGS framework: the disentanglement of scene properties and the explicit recovery of the lighting that governs them. The specialized appearance modeling techniques for specular reflections, transparency, and subsurface scattering covered in the following subsection build upon this foundation, addressing the residual challenges in rendering complex real-world materials under the recovered illumination.

### 9.3 Complex Appearance Modeling: Specular Reflections, Transparency, and Subsurface Scattering

The standard 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) framework excels at rendering diffuse and Lambertian surfaces but faces significant challenges when modeling complex optical phenomena such as specular reflections, transparency, and subsurface scattering. These phenomena are inherently view-dependent and require a nuanced understanding of light transport that goes beyond the simple alpha-blending of static Gaussian primitives. This subsection delves into specialized Gaussian-based techniques developed to address these challenging appearances, categorizing them into three main areas: specular and anisotropic reflections, semi-transparent surfaces, and subsurface scattering.

A primary limitation of vanilla 3DGS in handling specular surfaces is its use of spherical harmonics (SH) to encode color. SH are low-frequency basis functions and are ill-suited for capturing the high-frequency, view-dependent variations characteristic of glossy reflections. To overcome this, researchers have explored enhancing the Gaussian primitives themselves. One line of work augments the standard Gaussian kernel with view-dependent opacity, allowing the Gaussian to become "visible" or "invisible" based on the viewing angle. This approach, exemplified by [598], explicitly models specular effects, thereby decoupling them from the diffuse components. Building on this, [599] introduces an additional symmetric matrix to modulate the opacity of each Gaussian based on the viewer's perspective, enabling the model to suppress certain Gaussians for specific views and more accurately represent specular highlights without corrupting the scene's overall integrity. These methods demonstrate that view-dependent opacity is a powerful and efficient mechanism for capturing reflective phenomena.

Another prominent strategy for modeling specular reflections involves extending the representation into higher dimensions. The concept of N-dimensional Gaussians (N-DG) inspired [600], which revisits the 6D spatial-angular representation. This method enhances both color and opacity representation by leveraging the additional directional information in 6D space, allowing for optimized Gaussian control and significantly improved modeling of view-dependent effects while maintaining the real-time rendering capabilities of the original 3DGS framework. Similarly, [601] proposes a hybrid model, Viewing Direction Gaussian Splatting (VDGS), which combines the explicit shape representation of Gaussians with a NeRF-based multi-layer perceptron (MLP) that takes Gaussian parameters and viewing direction as input to produce view-dependent changes in color and opacity. This hybrid approach better captures complex optical behaviors like shadows and reflections. In a different approach, [602] equips each 3D Gaussian with a universal latent feature descriptor, which is then fed into two parallel convolutional neural networks (CNNs) to decode specular and diffuse colors separately, before a view-dependent mask merges them. This allows for a more expressive and accurate decomposition of the scene's appearance.

Further advancing beyond simple view dependence, some works aim for physically-based accuracy. The explicit modeling of reflection and transmission is tackled by frameworks like [603], which integrates a microfacet material model with ray tracing to jointly model specular reflection and transmittance using separate Gaussian primitives. This allows for the simultaneous reconstruction of distant reflections and objects behind semi-transparent surfaces. Similarly, [604] proposes a deferred shading method that leverages per-pixel reflection gradients to propagate nearly correct normal estimations across the surface of reflective objects, leading to high-quality specular effects while maintaining a rendering speed nearly identical to that of vanilla 3DGS. Anisotropic reflections, often seen on brushed metals, have also been a focus. While not strictly 3DGS, [605] demonstrates the power of Anisotropic Spherical Gaussian (ASG) distributions for modeling such appearances and has inspired similar techniques within the 3DGS community. This influence is seen in [606], which explicitly models surface normals and BRDF parameters, using ASG to approximate the normal distribution function under a microfacet model for efficient reconstruction of glossy surfaces.

Modeling transparency and semi-transparent surfaces presents another set of challenges. The order-dependent alpha-blending in vanilla 3DGS is a significant bottleneck, as it creates popping artifacts and fails to correctly represent the transmittance of light through multiple transparent layers. To address the issue of order-dependence, a key innovation is the use of moment-based order-independent transparency. The work [34] characterizes the density distribution along a camera ray using statistical moments, from which a continuous transmittance function can be reconstructed without the need for ray tracing or per-pixel sorting. This allows for physically accurate modeling of light attenuation in complex translucent media, bridging the gap between rasterization and physical accuracy. Another framework, [71], tackles the complex interplay of reflection and transmission on semi-transparent specular surfaces by disentangling geometric occupancy from optical opacity. This factorization allows the representation to act as a surface for reflections and a volume for transmission, effectively handling phenomena that would otherwise cause blurry reflections or occluded backgrounds. The authors introduce a Specular-Aware Gradient Gating mechanism to prevent misleading gradients from specular regions from corrupting the transmission branch. For scenes containing planar transparent surfaces like glass, [607] combines 3D Gaussians with learnable reflection planes, modeling the transmitted and reflected components separately and blending them based on a Fresnel-based, view-dependent weighting scheme. Complex refraction, as seen in transparent objects with intricate shapes, is tackled by [608], which introduces transparent Gaussian primitives that enable specular refraction through a deferred refraction strategy and uses Gaussian light field probes (GaussProbe) to encode ambient light and nearby contents for efficient querying.

Finally, subsurface scattering (SSS) represents the most complex optical phenomena, where light penetrates a surface, scatters internally, and exits at a different point. This effect is crucial for realistic rendering of materials like skin, wax, and marble. Applying standard 3DGS directly to these materials often fails because its primitives are surface-based and cannot capture volumetric light transport. A promising direction is to augment surface Gaussians with an implicit volumetric representation for scattering. [609] proposes a framework that decomposes the scene into an explicit 3D Gaussian surface with a spatially varying BRDF and an implicit volumetric representation of the scattering component, optimized jointly via ray-traced differentiable rendering. A more physically grounded approach is presented in [610], which decomposes reflectance into diffuse, specular, shadow, and subsurface scattering components. It introduces a learnable dipole-based scattering module for subsurface transport, drawing from the classic dipole approximation in radiative transfer, and an occlusion-aware shadow formulation. This enables high-fidelity relighting and physically plausible decomposition. Building on this, the challenge of data acquisition for SSS is addressed by [611], which uses diffusion-based data augmentation and strong geometric priors like multi-view silhouette and depth consistency to reconstruct translucent objects from as few as ten images, highlighting a path toward practical deployment. The integration of physics and neural networks is also explored in [612], which, while not exclusively a 3DGS method, introduces a formulation that reconciles physically complete light transport with general-purpose neural optimization, estimating optical properties and the full light field as neural fields through a residual objective derived from the Radiative Transfer Equation. This philosophy of using neural fields for light transport is echoed in [613], which solves the rendering equation by minimizing the norm of its residual using neural networks to represent the radiance distribution. Together, these methods—encompassing view-dependent opacity, hybrid renderers, order-independent transmittance, and specialized scattering modules—represent a significant stride in expanding 3DGS from simple diffuse scenes to a wide array of complex, real-world appearances, complementing the physically-based illumination modeling discussed previously and laying the groundwork for the spectral extensions explored in the next section.

### 9.4 Spectral Extensions, Multispectral Rendering, and Cross-Modal Appearance Adaptations

The trichromatic RGB color space forms the foundation of most 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) frameworks, yet it represents only a narrow window of the electromagnetic spectrum. Extending 3DGS beyond this conventional paradigm unlocks the ability to capture wavelength-dependent phenomena critical for scientific imaging, material analysis, remote sensing, and robust perception under challenging conditions. This subsection surveys the emerging body of work that adapts 3DGS to multispectral and hyperspectral rendering, spanning new radiance representations, cross-modal scene reconstruction strategies, and data-efficient learning techniques. The progression from appearance modeling in the previous subsection to spectral extensions here is a natural one: while view-dependent effects and scattering phenomena operate within the visible spectrum, spectral methods generalize these concepts across wavelength bands, requiring analogous innovations in radiance representation, physical modeling, and data efficiency.

**Multispectral and Hyperspectral Radiance Representations.** The most direct approach to spectral extension involves augmenting each Gaussian primitive with a wavelength-aware color representation. In the multispectral domain, [614] proposes augmenting each Gaussian with spectral radiance encoded via per-band spherical harmonics, supervised under a dual-loss scheme that jointly optimizes RGB and multispectral signals. A notable finding of this work is that performing spectral-to-RGB conversion at the pixel level—rather than at the per-Gaussian level—retains richer spectral cues during optimization, leading to improved rendering quality on scenes with translucent materials and anisotropic reflections. Building on the observation that naive per-band optimization often produces poor reconstructions due to inconsistent geometry across spectral domains, [615] systematically evaluates three strategies: separate per-band reconstruction, split optimization (where RGB geometry is first optimized and then transferred to spectral bands), and joint optimization with an initial RGB-only warm-up phase. The authors find that the joint strategy significantly improves spectral reconstruction and even enhances RGB rendering quality through spectral cross-talk, suggesting that spectral information should be integrated directly into the spherical harmonics color components of each Gaussian.

For hyperspectral settings, where the number of spectral bands can be extremely large, [616] introduces wavelength-aware spherical harmonics combined with a Kullback-Leibler divergence-based spectral loss. This method addresses the challenge of reconstructing continuous spectral radiance fields from sparse hyperspectral observations and demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on real-world scenes from the Hyper-NeRF dataset. A complementary perspective is offered by [617], which diverges from per-channel optimization entirely. This work encodes multispectral information into a learned, compact per-splat feature embedding, decoded by a shallow multilayer perceptron to produce spectral color values. This neural color representation captures underlying spectral and spatial correlations across modalities without requiring cross-modal camera calibration, making it versatile across thermal, near-infrared, and other spectral domains. The authors demonstrate its practical utility in agricultural applications, where rendering vegetation indices such as the normalized difference vegetation index (NDVI) provides actionable insights into crop health.

**Physically-Based Spectral Modeling and Spectral-Spatial Coupling.** Beyond simple radiance encoding, several methods integrate physical models of spectral phenomena into the 3DGS optimization framework. [618] introduces a cross-spectral rendering framework that estimates reflectance and illumination per spectral band using an improved physically-based rendering approach for Gaussian splats. By jointly optimizing geometry, per-spectra reflectance, and lighting, this method achieves superior performance compared to both spectral NeRF baselines (e.g., XNeRF and SpectralNeRF) and non-spectral state-of-the-art methods, while also enabling spectral-aware scene editing such as style transfer and inpainting. For thermal-infrared modalities, [619] leverages two physics-based principles: Fourier's law of heat conduction is integrated prior to alpha blending to model intensity interpolation caused by thermal conduction between neighboring Gaussians, and the Stefan-Boltzmann law combined with the inverse-square law formulates a depth-aware thermal radiation map that imposes geometric constraints. This approach models the Lambertian nature of thermal emission and reduces the number of Gaussians required for high-fidelity RGB-T reconstruction.

The challenge of spectral-spatial coupling is further addressed in [620], which, while not a 3DGS method itself, highlights the importance of frequency enhancement and spatial-spectral state-space modeling for infrared hyperspectral data. Its core insights—that state-space modeling captures global spectral dependencies while wavelet decomposition and Fourier gating recover structural contours and high-frequency details—are directly relevant to the design of spectral 3DGS kernels that must balance spectral fidelity with spatial sharpness. Similarly, [621] demonstrates that a single vision transformer backbone, conditioned on the active sensor modality, can effectively span NIR, SWIR, and LWIR bands. This suggests that spectral-adaptive conditioning mechanisms could be transferred to Gaussian-based representations to enable seamless cross-band rendering with a shared underlying scene representation.

**Cross-Modal Scene Reconstruction and Modality Adaptation.** A significant body of work addresses the challenge of reconstructing scenes when multiple modalities, such as visible and thermal cameras, observe the same geometry. [622] presents the first thermal 3DGS approach, which calibrates RGB and thermal cameras and learns multimodal 3D Gaussians with multimodal regularization constraints. The authors introduce smoothing constraints tailored to the physical characteristics of thermal data, preventing overfitting to any single modality while reducing model storage costs by 90%. A more recent framework, [623], proposes a Spectrum-Aware Adaptive Modulation mechanism that dynamically conditions shared latent features on thermal structural priors, guiding visible texture synthesis with reliable cross-modal geometric cues. Additionally, a Modality-Adaptive Geometric Decoupling scheme learns independent opacity offsets for the thermal branch, accommodating modality-specific geometric inconsistencies, while a hybrid rendering pipeline integrates explicit spherical harmonics with implicit neural decoding to preserve both semantic consistency and high-frequency details.

The issue of modality-specific geometry inconsistencies is further explored in [624], which proposes using only thermal infrared images combined with depth estimation to derive radiance fields, eliminating the reliance on visible light. This method, Thermal-to-Depth Gaussian Splatting (TDg), outperforms multiple single-modal Gaussian baselines while reducing training time by 55%, demonstrating that thermal-only reconstruction is viable for applications such as surveillance and industrial inspections. For scenarios where one or more modalities are scarce, [625] introduces SPoILeR, which learns mutual correlations between RGB and unconventional modalities (infrared, polarimetric, multispectral) during a multimodal pre-training phase. During fine-tuning, the model can predict accurate renderings of unconventional modalities using only RGB supervision, offering a data-efficient paradigm for cross-modal rendering.

**Spectral Rendering Infrastructure and Datasets.** Underpinning these advances are new representations for spectral rendering and datasets that facilitate evaluation. [626] proposes a compact latent representation enabling spectral rendering using standard RGB rendering operations. By encoding spectral images with a small number of RGB passes followed by a decoding step, and by introducing a learned non-negative linear encoder-decoder architecture that preserves scaling and addition while approximating multiplicativity under the Hadamard product, this method achieves significant color error reduction over RGB baselines at substantially lower computational cost than naive n-sample spectral rendering. At the data level, [627] presents a single-spectrograph system spanning the visible-near-infrared to short-wavelength infrared spectrum (450–1500 nm), reconstructing dense broadband hyperspectral reflectance alongside accurate 3D geometry. The authors model the image formation of broadband dispersed structured light and validate their approach on diverse real-world scenes, demonstrating accurate reconstruction of metameric materials and imaging through opaque layers.

**Data-Efficient and Diffusion-Augmented Approaches.** Given the scarcity of spectral training data, several methods employ generative models to augment reconstruction. [616] integrates a diffusion-based denoiser into the 3DGS pipeline, leveraging generative priors to enhance reconstruction quality across the entire spectral range. For the related task of hyperspectral image generation, [628] introduces a latent diffusion model with multi-condition control, enhanced by a novel two-stage hyperspectral super-resolution framework that first applies RGB-band super-resolution and then uses a Rectangular Guided Attention Network for guided hyperspectral super-resolution. While not directly a 3DGS method, the underlying principles of spectral fidelity preservation and spatial diversity augmentation are directly transferable to spectral 3DGS training. In the mobile photography domain, [629] leverages the complementarity between RGB and low-resolution multispectral images to predict shading, reflectance, and material semantic priors, integrating these priors into an HDRNet for dynamic range enhancement. This work highlights how spectral information can guide appearance modeling even when spectral data is low-resolution.

**Spectral Foundation Models and Cross-Domain Generalization.** The development of spectral foundation models represents a promising direction for improving the generalization of spectral 3DGS. [630] introduces a spectral encoder with a self-attention-cross-attention mechanism that distills salient spectral information into camera-agnostic learned representations, demonstrating robustness to spectral heterogeneity across medical, autonomous driving, and satellite imaging domains. Similarly, [631] makes an initial attempt to build a generalized RGBT multispectral foundation model using a Cross-Modality Structural Sparsity metric and a GMM-based progressive masking strategy for self-supervised pre-training. These foundation models, while developed for 2D imagery, provide a pathway toward spectrally-aware feature embeddings that could be distilled into Gaussian primitives, enabling zero-shot or few-shot generalization across unseen spectral sensors. In summary, the spectral extension of 3DGS is rapidly evolving from per-band spherical harmonic extensions toward physics-informed, feature-based, and generative approaches that jointly model spectral radiance, material properties, and cross-modal correlations, laying the foundation for wavelength-aware real-time rendering across diverse application domains.

## 10 Domain-Specific Applications and System Integration

### 10.1 3DGS for SLAM and Robotic Navigation

Section 10.1 provides a comprehensive examination of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) applied to Simultaneous Localization and Mapping (SLAM), a domain where the explicit, differentiable nature of Gaussian primitives has catalyzed a paradigm shift from traditional sparse feature-based localization and dense implicit neural mapping toward real-time, photorealistic dense reconstruction. This subsection systematically analyzes the architectural diversity, algorithmic innovations, robustness mechanisms, and collaborative extensions that define the current landscape of 3DGS-SLAM, situating these developments within the broader context of robotic perception and establishing the foundational techniques that subsequently enable the large-scale urban and autonomous driving applications discussed in Section 10.2.

**Sensor Modalities and SLAM Pipelines.** The architectural design of a 3DGS-SLAM system is fundamentally dictated by its sensor configuration, with monocular, RGB-D, and multi-modal setups each presenting unique trade-offs between hardware accessibility, reconstruction fidelity, and tracking robustness. RGB-D pipelines have established the baseline for high-quality dense reconstruction: Gaussian-SLAM organizes scenes into independently optimized sub-maps from real-world single-camera RGBD video, achieving interactive-time photo-realistic rendering while scaling to larger environments through sub-map decomposition [632]. The RGBD GS-ICP SLAM system further optimizes efficiency by fusing Generalized Iterative Closest Point (G-ICP) with 3DGS, enabling a single Gaussian map to serve both tracking and mapping while exchanging covariances and aligning scales to minimize redundant computation, ultimately achieving system speeds up to 107 FPS [633]. However, the depth sensor dependency of these approaches constrains their applicability on low-cost or power-limited platforms. This limitation has motivated substantial research into monocular pipelines. MonoGS++ eliminates the depth sensor requirement by leveraging online visual odometry for real-time sparse point cloud generation, and introduces dynamic Gaussian insertion with clarity-enhancing densification to achieve a 5.57x improvement in frames per second over prior monocular state-of-the-art [634]. KiloGS-SLAM tackles the coupled challenges of fragile long-term pose tracking and excessive memory overhead in kilometer-scale outdoor scenes, introducing a motion-adaptive hybrid tracking module with a condition-triggered three-tier solving pipeline and a lifecycle-managed Gaussian mapping strategy to scale to sequences exceeding 10,000 frames on a single GPU [635]. Beyond single-sensor approaches, multi-modal systems leverage complementary sensing modalities to enhance accuracy and robustness. MM3DGS SLAM incorporates inertial measurements into the 3DGS framework, achieving a 3x improvement in tracking accuracy and a 5% improvement in rendering quality by integrating IMU pre-integrated measurements directly into the loss functions [636]. Other works exploit learned priors to bootstrap monocular systems: GeoGS-SLAM and MyGO-Splat employ feed-forward reconstruction priors and foundation model depth estimates, with MyGO-Splat introducing a closed-loop design that analytically rasterizes Gaussians into pixel-wise depth and normals to actively supervise camera pose optimization, achieving performance comparable to RGB-D methods [243; 637]. Extending to multi-camera configurations, MCGS-SLAM presents the first purely RGB-based multi-camera SLAM system built on 3DGS, fusing dense RGB inputs from multiple viewpoints into a unified Gaussian map via multi-camera bundle adjustment, thereby enabling reconstruction of side-view regions that monocular setups inherently miss [247].

This progression from depth-dependent to monocular and multi-modal systems mirrors the sensor evolution observed in autonomous driving platforms, where heterogeneous camera rigs and varying exposure conditions necessitate increasingly flexible reconstruction frameworks—a theme that carries directly into the multi-camera handling strategies discussed in Section 10.2.

**Real-Time Tracking, Mapping, and Memory Management.** The stringent real-time requirements of SLAM necessitate careful co-design between tracking efficiency and mapping quality. A prevailing architectural pattern is decoupling, exemplified by GLAM-SLAM, which pairs a robust feature-based frontend for lightweight tracking with a structured, sparse anchor grid representation for scalable mapping [245]. This decoupling ensures that tracking remains computationally lightweight while the mapping module maintains scene coherence across extended sequences, as demonstrated on challenging benchmarks such as KITTI Odometry and Oxford RobotCar [245]. Memory management emerges as a critical bottleneck, since the number of Gaussian primitives can grow unboundedly during exploration. Pocket-SLAM introduces a rendering-area-aware pruning strategy that removes Gaussians based on their effective contribution to rendered images rather than relying solely on opacity or gradient magnitude, achieving over 60% memory reduction and a 2x FPS improvement in large-scale outdoor scenes [638]. Similarly, CaRtGS addresses computational misalignment by adaptively enhancing optimization iterations and refining densification to achieve high-fidelity rendering while maintaining a compact Gaussian representation [639]. At the system level, AGS proposes an algorithm-hardware co-design framework that exploits frame covisibility, using a coarse-then-fine-grained pose tracking method and a CODEC-assisted frame covisibility detection engine to achieve up to 17.12x speedup against high-end GPUs [640]. MGSO demonstrates that photometric SLAM can generate dense structured point clouds for Gaussian initialization, accelerating optimization and producing more efficient maps with fewer Gaussians while maintaining laptop-level performance [641].

**Robustness to Dynamic Environments and Challenging Conditions.** Early 3DGS-SLAM systems inherited the static-world assumption from classical SLAM, which constitutes a major obstacle for real-world deployment in dynamic settings. A substantial body of research has emerged to address this limitation. DynaGSLAM goes beyond merely removing dynamic objects, achieving high-quality online rendering, tracking, and motion prediction of moving objects while jointly estimating ego-motion [642]. Dy3DGS-SLAM addresses monocular dynamic scenes by fusing optical flow masks and depth masks through a probabilistic model to derive a fused dynamic mask that constrains tracking and eliminates transient interference during mapping [401]. DAGS-SLAM introduces a spatiotemporal motion probability state per Gaussian and triggers semantic computation on demand via an uncertainty-aware scheduler, achieving a practical speed-accuracy tradeoff with reduced semantic invocations [404]. DL-SLAM computes dual-level dynamic probability maps that combine semantic and geometric information, enabling categorical pruning of dynamic Gaussians while allowing transiently static objects to contribute valuable geometric constraints for pose estimation [643]. WildGS-SLAM and UP-SLAM develop uncertainty-aware frameworks [644; 405], with UP-SLAM using a training-free uncertainty estimator that fuses multi-modal residuals to estimate per-pixel motion uncertainty and a probabilistic octree for adaptive Gaussian management [405]. For degraded imaging conditions, RoGER-SLAM introduces a Structure-Preserving Robust Fusion mechanism and an adaptive tracking objective to maintain trajectory accuracy and reconstruction quality under noise and low illumination [645]. These robustness strategies—particularly the handling of dynamic objects, exposure variations, and degraded visibility—lay crucial groundwork for the urban driving scenarios in Section 10.2, where moving traffic, changing illumination, and challenging weather conditions are inherent rather than exceptional.

**Multi-Agent and Collaborative SLAM Architectures.** The extension of 3DGS-SLAM to multi-robot systems introduces challenges in global consistency, communication efficiency, and data fusion. Surveys on collaborative SLAM with 3DGS systematically categorize approaches by centralized and distributed architectures, analyzing core components such as multi-agent consistency, communication-efficient Gaussian representation, and real-time scalability [646]. MAGiC-SLAM proposes a rigidly deformable 3D Gaussian-based scene representation to dramatically speed up the system, integrating loop closure for globally consistent map merging from multiple agents [647]. MAGS-SLAM presents the first RGB-only multi-agent 3DGS SLAM framework, integrating compact submap communication and occupancy-aware Gaussian fusion to enable coherent global reconstruction without active depth sensors [648]. Addressing communication bandwidth constraints, Compact Keyframe-Optimized Multi-Agent Gaussian Splatting SLAM incorporates a compaction step to remove redundant 3D Gaussians, achieving up to 85–95% reduction in transmitted data compared to state-of-the-art approaches [649]. For outdoor scenarios, CoMo3R-SLAM presents a collaborative monocular dense SLAM system that leverages learned feed-forward 3D reconstruction priors for cross-agent verification and global bundle adjustment, achieving globally consistent metric maps from monocular RGB alone [650]. These collaborative frameworks anticipate the multi-agent and fleet-level coordination requirements that emerge in autonomous driving contexts, where multiple vehicles must maintain consistent environmental representations.

**Semantic Mapping and Scene Understanding.** The integration of semantic information into 3DGS-SLAM has catalyzed a new class of spatial AI capabilities. SemGauss-SLAM was the first semantic SLAM system utilizing 3D Gaussian representation, incorporating semantic feature embedding and feature-level loss for precise semantic scene representation, alongside semantic-informed bundle adjustment for joint optimization [651]. GS3LAM models the scene as a Semantic Gaussian Field and jointly optimizes camera poses and the field via multimodal error constraints, introducing a Random Sampling-based Keyframe Mapping strategy to mitigate catastrophic forgetting [652]. Open-set semantics have been explored in OpenMonoGS-SLAM, the first monocular SLAM framework that unifies 3DGS with open-vocabulary understanding by leveraging Visual Foundation Models such as SAM and CLIP [653]. FeatureSLAM integrates dense feature rasterization into novel-view synthesis, enabling entirely new downstream tasks via free-viewpoint, open-set segmentation [654]. LEGO-SLAM introduces a scene-adaptive autoencoder to distill high-dimensional language embeddings into a compact 16-dimensional feature space, reducing memory per Gaussian and enabling language-guided loop detection [655]. STAMICS integrates semantic information with 3D Gaussian representations, combining graph-based clustering for temporal semantic consistency with an open-vocabulary system to classify unseen objects [656]. These semantic capabilities not only improve mapping quality but also enable high-level reasoning and interaction essential for autonomous navigation and manipulation in complex environments, as highlighted by research on human-in-the-loop and LLM-enhanced object SLAM [657; 658]. The semantic segmentation and object-level understanding developed here find direct application in the autonomous driving systems of Section 10.2, where distinguishing and manipulating dynamic agents (vehicles, pedestrians) from static backgrounds is a prerequisite for safe closed-loop simulation and corner-case generation.

In summary, this subsection establishes the core algorithmic toolkit of 3DGS-SLAM—spanning sensor-adaptive architectures, real-time optimization strategies, dynamic robustness, collaborative consistency, and semantic understanding—that collectively provide the scalable, editable, and photorealistic scene representations on which the autonomous driving and urban reconstruction systems of Section 10.2 are built. The techniques surveyed here, from multi-camera fusion and exposure handling to dynamic scene decomposition and memory-efficient large-scale mapping, constitute the foundational capabilities that enable the transition from indoor and small-scale environments to the kilometer-scale, traffic-dense, sensor-heterogeneous driving scenarios that follow.

### 10.2 3DGS for Autonomous Driving and Urban Reconstruction

### 10.2 3DGS for Autonomous Driving and Urban Reconstruction

The application of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) to autonomous driving and large-scale urban reconstruction represents one of the most active and impactful research frontiers, fundamentally transforming how driving scenes are captured, simulated, and validated. The core appeal of 3DGS in this domain stems from its explicit, point-based representation that supports real-time rendering, photorealistic fidelity, and scene editability—properties that are indispensable for creating high-fidelity digital twins, closed-loop simulation environments, and synthetic data generators for safety-critical scenario testing. Building directly upon the sensor-adaptive architectures, dynamic robustness mechanisms, and memory-efficient mapping strategies established in the 3DGS-SLAM systems of Section 10.1, this subsection examines how these foundational techniques are scaled, adapted, and extended to meet the unique challenges of kilometer-scale, traffic-dense, and sensor-heterogeneous driving environments. The transition from SLAM to autonomous driving is characterized not only by a dramatic increase in spatial scale and dynamic complexity but also by a fundamental shift in emphasis: whereas SLAM prioritizes online tracking and incremental mapping for robot localization, autonomous driving applications demand offline photorealistic reconstruction, closed-loop simulation, and controllable scenario generation for perception validation. Nevertheless, the algorithmic toolkit developed in Section 10.1—including multi-camera fusion, handling of dynamic objects, and geometric regularization—reappears here in expanded and specialized forms, while the real-time rendering and interaction techniques explored in this section will subsequently find direct application to the constrained hardware and user-centric demands of Extended Reality in Section 10.3.

**Sensor Simulation for Closed-Loop Testing and Photorealistic Digital Twins**

A central application of 3DGS is the creation of photorealistic digital twins from real-world driving logs, which serve as the foundation for closed-loop simulation and perception model validation. Unlike traditional game-engine-based simulators that suffer from a domain gap with real-world data, neural reconstruction methods like 3DGS offer a scalable solution for creating realistic virtual environments. This approach is exemplified by frameworks such as SceneCrafter, which leverages 3DGS to create an interactive and efficient simulator capable of generating realistic driving logs across diverse traffic scenarios while also enabling robust closed-loop evaluation of end-to-end models, thereby improving model generalization through synthetic data generation [659]. Similarly, the framework known as RealEngine reconstructs background scenes and foreground traffic participants separately from real-world data using 3DGS, allowing for flexible scene composition and photorealistic rendering across multiple sensor modalities, which supports non-reactive simulation, safety testing, and multi-agent interaction [660]. This decomposition of driving scenes into static backgrounds and dynamic agents mirrors the dynamic-object-handling strategies developed for 3DGS-SLAM in Section 10.1, but extends them to support independent control and re-composition of traffic participants for simulation purposes.

However, a significant challenge in this context is the "sim-to-real" gap introduced by the edited 3DGS-rendered videos, which often contain rendering artifacts, degraded foreground assets, and temporal flickering. To address this, RealityBridge proposes a structure-preserving and asset-aware framework that uses multimodal controls, including rendered videos, masks, and semantic maps, to repair 3DGS-specific artifacts and harmonize illumination, thereby enhancing the realism and temporal consistency of edited driving videos [661]. For large-scale digital twin integration, the DrivIng dataset provides a comprehensive multi-modal dataset with a complete geo-referenced digital twin of an 18 km route, leveraging 3DGS to enable a one-to-one transfer of real traffic into simulation while preserving agent interactions for realistic scenario testing [662]. Additionally, frameworks that synthesize novel trajectory views by leveraging world model priors, such as DriveDreamer4D, enhance 4D driving scene representation with improved spatiotemporal coherence for closed-loop simulation [663].

**Handling Multi-Camera Setups and Varying Exposures**

Real-world autonomous driving data is characterized by heterogeneous camera pipelines, varying exposures, and dynamic outdoor illumination. A critical limitation of conventional 3DGS is its implicit assumption of consistent exposure and tone mapping across views, which is violated in real driving data, leading to artifacts and inconsistent illumination. This challenge directly extends the multi-camera fusion and exposure-handling considerations introduced in the SLAM systems of Section 10.1, but in the driving context, the scale and heterogeneity of camera rigs—often spanning 6 to 12 cameras with differing exposure times and field-of-view characteristics—amplify the difficulty substantially. To address this, P2GS introduces a physically consistent Gaussian Splatting framework that jointly decomposes a view-invariant linear HDR radiance field, per-view exposure scales, and tone-mapping functions from LDR images without HDR supervision, ensuring photometric consistency and robust illumination across diverse scenes [298]. Furthermore, multi-camera setups require native support for diverse camera models. Instant NuRec presents a feed-forward neural reconstruction model that turns a short multi-view driving log into a simulatable 3DGS world in a single forward pass, with native support for non-pinhole camera models via 3DGUT, enabling rapid reconstruction and high-fidelity closed-loop simulation [664]. For modeling fine-grained variations between frames and camera viewpoints, ArmGS devises a multi-level appearance modeling scheme that optimizes transformation parameters at local Gaussian, global image, and dynamic actor levels, enabling composite Gaussian refinement that models both global appearance variations and local fine-grained changes of background and objects [665]. These techniques demonstrate how the multi-camera handling strategies of Section 10.1 are systematically extended to accommodate the hardware heterogeneity and photometric complexity inherent to production-grade autonomous driving platforms.

**Geometric Consistency and Scalability for City-Scale Scenes**

Scaling 3DGS to large-scale urban environments presents significant challenges in geometric consistency, memory efficiency, and computational scalability. While the SLAM systems of Section 10.1 typically operate at the scale of indoor rooms or bounded outdoor environments, autonomous driving reconstruction targets city-scale areas spanning kilometers, requiring fundamentally different strategies for spatial partitioning, memory management, and geometric regularization. The UrbanGS framework addresses these issues by introducing a depth-consistent D-normal regularization module that integrates monocular normal constraints with external depth supervision, ensuring comprehensive updates of all geometric parameters and multi-view depth alignment. Additionally, a spatially adaptive Gaussian pruning strategy and a unified partitioning scheme are designed to reduce redundancy and eliminate boundary artifacts, achieving superior performance in rendering quality, geometric accuracy, and memory efficiency for city-scale applications [225]. The memory-efficient pruning strategies developed here echo the rendering-area-aware pruning of Pocket-SLAM from Section 10.1, but are adapted to the much larger scale and more heterogeneous scene content of urban environments. For highway-specific scenarios, LiHi-GS proposes a LiDAR-supervised Gaussian splatting method that improves scene reconstruction through LiDAR supervision and supports LiDAR rendering, addressing the challenging highway scenes characterized by sparse sensor views and monotone backgrounds [234].

**Scene Editing, Data Augmentation, and Safety-Critical Scenario Generation**

A key advantage of 3DGS is its explicit representation, which facilitates scene editing and the generation of diverse synthetic data for safety-critical scenario creation. This capability builds on the semantic mapping and scene understanding foundations established in Section 10.1, but extends them toward controllable manipulation and synthetic data generation—a requirement unique to autonomous driving validation. DecoupleGS presents a framework that decomposes scenes into a static background and manipulable dynamic agents, using an object-centric canonical representation to enable high-fidelity background and controllable dynamic agents for end-to-end autonomous driving testing [666]. For photorealistic object insertion, R3D2 leverages a lightweight diffusion model to generate plausible rendering effects, such as shadows and consistent lighting, enabling realistic insertion of complete 3D assets into existing 3DGS scenes, which supports text-to-3D asset insertion and cross-scene object transfer [667]. Moreover, MultiEditor introduces a dual-branch latent diffusion framework that uses 3DGS as a structural and appearance prior to jointly edit images and LiDAR point clouds, thereby enhancing the perception accuracy of models on underrepresented vehicle classes [668]. DriveWeaver offers a point-conditioned video inpainting framework that generates temporally consistent vehicles for insertion, explicitly extracting 3D Gaussian representations to ensure real-time rendering and lighting consistency between foreground and background [669]. The CARLA-GS framework further unifies visual representation, semantic reasoning, and physics-based execution, using an editable Gaussian scene and a multi-agent LLM to generate physically feasible corner-case trajectories that are re-projected into the scene for photorealistic corner-case generation [670]. This integration of LLM-driven reasoning with editable 3DGS scenes anticipates the LLM-XR synergies that will be explored in Section 10.3, where language models similarly power intelligent content creation and interactive world manipulation.

**Integration with Traffic Simulators and Feed-Forward Reconstruction**

Integrating 3DGS with traffic simulators enables comprehensive safety validation. SynthDrive proposes a scalable real2sim2real system that automates asset mining and generation from real data, addressing the scalability limitations of both CG-based and learning-based approaches for generic objects [671]. To alleviate the per-scene optimization cost of traditional 3DGS, feed-forward reconstruction methods have been developed. ReconDrive leverages a 3D foundation model with hybrid Gaussian prediction heads and a static-dynamic 4D composition strategy to rapidly generate high-fidelity 4D Gaussian representations for driving scenes, achieving performance competitive with per-scene optimization while being orders of magnitude faster [672]. The feed-forward paradigm introduced here—which prioritizes inference speed and generalization over per-scene optimization—represents a conceptual departure from the online optimization focus of the SLAM systems in Section 10.1, and anticipates the efficiency-first approaches that will become dominant in the XR applications of Section 10.3, where real-time interactivity on constrained hardware is paramount. Finally, to enable robust reconstruction without expensive annotations, S3Gaussian presents a self-supervised method that decomposes dynamic and static elements from 4D consistency using a spatial-temporal field network, demonstrating the ability to reconstruct street scenes without requiring tracked 3D vehicle bounding boxes, which broadens the applicability of 3DGS in-the-wild scenarios [673]. For further improving the fidelity of reconstructions under novel viewpoints, generative enhancers like GenRe distill diffusion priors into 3D Gaussian representations to generalize reliably to challenging unseen viewpoints [674]. Additionally, frameworks such as DecoupleGS emphasize asset compression and map-guided geometric registration to achieve balanced fidelity-efficiency trade-offs for large-scale evaluation [666].

In summary, this subsection has traced the evolution of 3DGS from the online, resource-constrained SLAM systems of Section 10.1 to the large-scale, editable, and simulation-oriented frameworks that define the autonomous driving and urban reconstruction landscape. The key technical transitions—from online tracking to offline reconstruction, from single-vehicle perception to multi-camera heterogeneous sensor fusion, and from passive mapping to active scene manipulation for safety-critical testing—collectively establish the representational power and system-level maturity of 3DGS as a core technology for next-generation autonomous driving platforms. These capabilities, particularly the ability to render photorealistic scenes in real-time, edit and recompose scene elements, and generate controllable synthetic data, lay the essential groundwork for the transition to Extended Reality applications in Section 10.3, where similar requirements for real-time rendering, scene editability, and user-driven interaction are reinterpreted within the context of immersive experiences on constrained hardware.

### 10.3 3DGS for Virtual, Augmented, and Mixed Reality (XR)

The transition from the structured, sensor-rich environments of autonomous driving and urban reconstruction to the more fluid and interactive domain of Extended Reality (XR) is marked by a fundamental shift in priorities. While the previous subsection emphasized geometric accuracy and scalability for large-scale city modeling, the integration of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) with Virtual Reality (VR), Augmented Reality (AR), and Mixed Reality (MR) focuses on real-time interactivity, hardware constraints, and user-centric experiences. Moreover, as we will see in the subsequent subsection, the principles developed here—such as efficient rendering on constrained hardware and the integration of scene understanding—find direct parallels in the demands of medical imaging and remote sensing. In XR, the convergence of 3DGS with immersive technologies represents a paradigm shift in how digital content is created, rendered, and interacted with. 3DGS offers an explicit, point-based scene representation that delivers photorealistic novel view synthesis at real-time frame rates, directly addressing the stringent performance requirements of XR headsets and the demand for interactivity that prior implicit neural rendering methods could not satisfy [675]. This subsection explores the multifaceted integration of 3DGS into XR systems, focusing on real-time rendering on constrained hardware, hardware-software co-design, physics-aware asset creation, user-guided editing, telepresence, and the synergistic coupling with large language models (LLMs).

A primary challenge in deploying 3DGS within XR is achieving high-fidelity rendering on resource-constrained, standalone headsets that have limited compute, memory, and power budgets—a constraint that is less prominent in the server-side processing typical of autonomous driving but becomes a dominant factor in consumer XR devices. The computational demands of rendering large-scale 3DGS models often exceed the capabilities of consumer-grade devices, highlighting a critical need for system-level optimization [676]. To address this, an end-to-end framework introduces a cache-centric pipeline that eliminates redundant computations, an efficiency-aware scheduler for elastic multi-GPU rendering, and optimized CUDA kernels. This synergy between representation and system design achieves up to a 5.35x performance improvement and supports 2K binocular rendering at over 120 FPS, demonstrating a scalable path for real-time neural rendering in immersive environments [676]. Beyond software optimizations, the architectural landscape of XR workloads—which are increasingly characterized by heterogeneous operator types and complex dataflow structures—demands a shift from conventional CNN-centric accelerators [677]. This insight underscores the need for phase-aware scheduling and elastic resource allocation in next-generation XR System-on-Chips (SoCs).

Hardware-software co-design is essential to unlock the full potential of 3DGS in XR. A notable example is an architecture-algorithm co-design framework that introduces axis-shared rasterization and order-independent transmittance. By precomputing common terms along X- and Y-axes, the design reduces multiply-and-accumulate operations by up to 38%, while a lightweight multilayer perceptron (MLP) approximates transmittance to remove the need for explicit sorting, enabling efficient alpha blending and achieving a 1.33 to 1.88x speedup over state-of-the-art accelerators [111]. Furthermore, the feasibility of native MR compositing on devices like the Meta Quest 3 has been explored, with benchmarking showing that the Snapdragon XR2 Gen 2 can support lightweight native MR content creation at 720p30, albeit with thermal limits restricting operation to 5–10 minutes. This study underscores the tight thermal and power headroom that must be managed in XR hardware design [678].

The creation of interactive and dynamic XR content is another frontier where 3DGS excels. While the previous subsection focused on static and dynamic scene reconstruction for simulation, XR elevates the requirement to real-time physical interaction. Traditional techniques for content creation are engineering-intensive and require extensive expertise. Systems like VR-GS represent a leap forward by integrating physical dynamics awareness into an interactive 3DGS system within VR. By constructing a two-level embedding strategy alongside deformable body simulations, VR-GS ensures real-time execution with highly realistic dynamic responses, enabling users to interact with and manipulate virtual objects in a physically plausible manner [679]. This capability is further enhanced by LLMs, which can predict reasonable physical parameters from static Gaussian assets in seconds, facilitating the rapid creation of physics-based dynamic assets without manual annotation. This integration of language models with 3DGS enables non-experts to author highly realistic, interactive VR scenes, as demonstrated in the LIVE-GS system [680].

Building upon the physical interactivity of Gaussian assets, user-guided scene editing and manipulation are crucial for spatial computing. The explicit nature of 3DGS primitives allows for intuitive and precise editing, which can be further controlled through semantic understanding. For instance, EditSplat introduces a text-driven 3D scene editing framework that integrates Multi-view Fusion Guidance to ensure multi-view consistency and Attention-Guided Trimming to selectively prune Gaussians, enabling semantically rich and localized edits [538]. Additionally, the rise of generative AI and LLMs is transforming XR authoring. New interfaces are emerging that allow users to create outdoor scenes through natural language interaction, combining offline scene understanding and fast 3D asset generation, as exemplified by the ImaginateAR system [681]. Such systems translate natural language prompts into spatial intent and executable constraints for generative 3D design [682], moving towards continuous co-creation where part-aware representations enable component-level control [683].

Another key application where 3DGS uniquely bridges the gap between the physical and virtual worlds is telepresence and teleoperation, building on the sensor fusion techniques used in autonomous driving but applied here to human-centric communication. Traditional teleoperation systems often rely on screen-based interfaces that lack depth cues, hindering task performance. Multi-view VR telepresence systems that fuse geometry from multiple cameras to produce GPU-accelerated point-cloud rendering on standalone VR hardware have shown improved usability, and integrating a localized RGB stream can further enhance performance [684]. In the domain of human telepresence, frameworks like Mon3tr integrate 3DGS-based parametric human modeling into telepresence, dividing the process into a one-time offline reconstruction and a monocular online inference phase. This amortized computation strategy, where a single RGB camera drives a 3DGS avatar, achieves photorealistic rendering at ~60 FPS on a Meta Quest 3 with an end-to-end latency of ~80 ms, while reducing bandwidth by over 1000x compared to point-cloud streaming [685]. This represents a major step toward accessible and realistic holographic communication.

Finally, the integration of 3DGS with large language models (LLMs) is enabling intelligent interactions and context-aware experiences, a trend that anticipates the multi-modal system integration challenges discussed in the next subsection. LLMs can power the real-time creation and modification of interactive XR experiences, with frameworks like LLMR demonstrating 4x improvements in error rate over standard GPT-4 by using scene understanding and task planning techniques [686]. Moreover, large multimodal models (LMMs) are being used to build voice-first assistants in MR that can understand physical environments and generate content, facilitating a more natural human-AI collaboration [687]. The combination of LLMs' reasoning power with 3DGS's visual fidelity promises to make XR environments not only more immersive but also more intelligent and responsive to user intent, pushing the boundaries of what is achievable in interactive media and virtual worlds [688].

### 10.4 3DGS for Medical Imaging, Remote Sensing, and Cross-Domain System Integration

The versatility of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has catalyzed its adoption across a diverse range of domain-specific applications, moving well beyond the conventional boundaries of computer vision and graphics. This subsection surveys the significant strides made in integrating 3DGS within two particularly demanding fields: medical imaging and remote sensing. We also examine the system-level integration challenges and opportunities that arise when deploying 3DGS in these real-world pipelines, highlighting how the unique properties of Gaussian primitives are leveraged to address domain-specific constraints. While the previous subsection focused on the real-time interactivity and user-centric demands of XR, the applications discussed here shift the emphasis toward domain-specific constraints such as anatomical accuracy, multi-temporal consistency, and scalability to geospatial extents. Nevertheless, the principles of efficient rendering on constrained hardware and the integration of scene understanding developed in the XR context find direct parallels in the clinical and remote sensing pipelines explored below.

**3DGS in Medical Imaging and Surgical Applications**

The medical domain presents a unique set of challenges for 3D reconstruction, characterized by the need for high fidelity, anatomical accuracy, and the ability to handle complex, often dynamic, biological tissues. Traditional methods, while robust, are often computationally expensive and can lead to structural discontinuities, especially when working with sparse slice data [689]. 3DGS has emerged as a powerful solution to these challenges, offering a blend of rendering efficiency and geometric fidelity that is well-suited for clinical workflows. Just as the XR domain demanded real-time performance on resource-constrained headsets, medical applications require rapid reconstruction for intraoperative decision-making, yet they impose the additional burden of strict anatomical correctness.

A primary application area is the reconstruction of surgical scenes for intraoperative navigation and simulation. Endoscopic procedures, for instance, suffer from photometric inconsistencies caused by non-Lambertian surfaces and dynamic motion due to patient breathing [690]. Here, 3DGS-based SLAM systems have been developed to provide robust real-time tracking and mapping, mirroring the real-time demands of XR telepresence systems discussed previously, but with the added complexity of deformable tissue and specular reflections. For example, EndoFlow-SLAM integrates optical flow loss as a geometric constraint and a depth regularization strategy to mitigate photometric inconsistencies, demonstrating superior performance in both static and dynamic surgical scenes [690]. Similarly, to address the limitations of Structure-from-Motion in feature-sparse endoscopic environments, SurGSplat proposes a paradigm that progressively refines 3DGS through geometric constraints, enabling detailed reconstruction of critical structures like vasculature for enhanced intraoperative decision-making [691].

Beyond navigation, 3DGS is being used to create immersive visualization platforms, directly building upon the XR foundations established in the previous subsection. Multi-Layer Gaussian Splatting introduces a layered representation for CT scans, enabling interactive exploration of different anatomical structures in virtual reality at frame rates suitable for immersive headsets—a task previously prohibitive with computationally intensive path tracing [692]. This approach retains a degree of interactivity, such as selective activation and clipping of layers, which is crucial for medical education and pre-operative planning [692]. Furthermore, the interplay between 3D reconstruction and advanced imaging modalities is being explored. For instance, PhysioSynth, a modality-specific simulator, can generate synthetic CT and MR images based on anatomical priors, which can be used to train and validate downstream reconstruction models in a data-efficient manner [693]. This synergy between simulation and reconstruction is vital for overcoming data scarcity and privacy concerns in medical AI, echoing the broader trend of using in-silico experiments to scale up model training [694].

The integration of 3DGS with other medical data types and deep learning models is a growing trend, paralleling the multi-modal fusion strategies seen in both XR and remote sensing. The success of AI models like nnU-Net and its variants in tasks such as brain tissue segmentation and lung registration underscores the importance of robust 3D representations, and 3DGS offers a complementary, rendering-centric approach [695]. The explicit nature of Gaussian primitives also facilitates the incorporation of geometric priors, which can be critical in complex tasks like surgical phase recognition [696]. More advanced frameworks, such as MedGS, enhance 3DGS with physically based relightable models, separating light effects from tissue properties to generate photorealistic reconstructions with better generalization across novel views and enabling realistic tissue modifications—a significant step towards practical clinical simulation tools [697].

**3DGS in Remote Sensing and Satellite Photogrammetry**

Remote sensing applications, particularly those using satellite or aerial imagery, present a distinct set of challenges, including large-scale scenes, multi-temporal data, radiometric inconsistencies, and complex geometric distortions. While the XR and medical domains prioritize real-time interactivity and anatomical fidelity, respectively, remote sensing emphasizes scalability, temporal robustness, and geometric accuracy across vast spatial extents. 3DGS is increasingly recognized as a valuable tool for overcoming these hurdles, especially for large-scale urban and environmental monitoring [698; 113].

One of the primary difficulties in applying 3DGS to satellite imagery is the incompatibility with the Rational Polynomial Coefficient (RPC) camera model, a challenge unique to, yet analogous with, the sensor-specific constraints encountered in endoscopic imaging. SkySplat directly addresses this by integrating the RPC model into a generalizable 3DGS pipeline, enabling rapid and accurate digital surface model (DSM) reconstruction from sparse, multi-temporal satellite images [699]. This work demonstrates not only an 86× speedup over per-scene optimization methods but also superior accuracy, highlighting the potential of 3DGS for large-scale geospatial applications [699]. Similarly, SwiftGS introduces a meta-learned system that predicts geometry-radiation-decoupled Gaussian primitives in a single forward pass, replacing expensive per-scene fitting with an episodic training approach. This allows for immediate, zero-shot surface recovery from multi-date satellite imagery, which is critical for rapid disaster response and environmental monitoring [700]. The framework's design, which couples a differentiable physics graph with spatial gating, ensures both geometric accuracy and view-consistent rendering, overcoming the traditional cost of per-scene optimization [700].

Scalability to large urban and aerial scenes remains a key research focus, echoing the constraints of large-scale reconstruction discussed in the context of autonomous driving. Approaches like HUG and LoBE-GS introduce novel hierarchical and load-balanced frameworks to manage the massive memory consumption and slow training times associated with city-scale data [698; 113]. LoBE-GS, for instance, utilizes a load-balanced KD-tree scene partitioning scheme and visibility cropping to achieve up to 2× faster end-to-end training times than state-of-the-art baselines, enabling reconstructions previously infeasible with vanilla 3DGS [113]. These advances are complemented by distributed computing frameworks like Radiant and LandMarkSystem, which partition regions based on heterogeneous environment information and leverage distributed parallel computing to handle large-scale scenes [701; 702]. Furthermore, research in this area often builds upon the foundation of classic stereo and multi-view satellite photogrammetry, and the integration of these established pipelines with modern neural rendering is an active area of exploration [703; 704].

**Cross-Domain System Integration and Challenges**

The integration of 3DGS into real-world pipelines across these domains reveals several common threads and challenges that also connect back to the XR applications discussed in the previous subsection. A significant challenge is the robustness of the reconstruction pipeline to imperfect or heterogeneous inputs. For surgery, this means handling tool occlusions, bleeding, and specular reflections [705]. For remote sensing, it involves dealing with illumination changes, sensor heterogeneity, and transient objects [699; 700]. These robustness requirements parallel the need for XR systems to handle dynamic user movements and varied environmental lighting conditions.

System-level integration often requires fusing 3DGS with other data modalities, such as LiDAR point clouds, thermal infrared images, or multi-spectral data [93; 623; 706]. For example, ThermoSplat demonstrates a cross-modal framework that integrates RGB and thermal infrared data for robust environmental perception, a capability crucial for both autonomous systems and defense applications [623]. The fundamental trade-off between high-fidelity visuals from neural representations like 3DGS and the robust geometric accuracy of traditional surface meshes is a critical consideration. For functional tasks like line-of-sight analysis and physics simulations, a hybrid approach that combines a traditional mesh scaffold for geometric integrity with a neural representation for visual detail is often the most practical solution [93].

In the medical domain, integrating 3DGS with other analytical tools, such as large language models for interactive querying or vision-language models for automated reporting, remains a significant frontier [707; 708]. This trend toward language-driven interaction mirrors the LLM-integrated XR systems discussed in the previous subsection, suggesting a convergent trajectory toward more intelligent and context-aware 3D applications. The ability to segment and understand the reconstructed scene is also paramount. While datasets like 3DLAND provide 3D lesion annotations for medical CT volumes, the seamless integration of dense 3D scene reconstruction with object-level or semantic understanding is still an open research problem [709]. Overall, the deployment of 3DGS in these critical domains hinges on overcoming challenges related to computational scalability, data efficiency, multimodal fusion, and the development of robust evaluation protocols that reflect real-world operational conditions [37]. These challenges, while manifesting differently across XR, medicine, and remote sensing, collectively define the research agenda for the next generation of 3DGS-based systems, setting the stage for the cross-cutting methodological discussions that follow in the subsequent subsection.

## 11 Security, Quality Assessment, and Benchmarks

### 11.1 Threat Modeling and Security Analysis of 3DGS Systems

The rapid adoption of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) in safety-critical domains such as autonomous driving, augmented reality, and robotic navigation has made the security of its reconstruction pipeline a pressing concern. Unlike traditional deep learning models operating on flat 2D inputs, 3DGS involves a multi-stage pipeline that transforms a collection of posed images into an explicit, editable 3D representation. This multi-stage nature creates a unique and expanded attack surface, where malicious actors can intervene at various points—from the input data to the intermediate geometric representations to the final optimized Gaussian parameters. A formal threat-modeling framework is therefore essential to systematically analyze adversarial capabilities, identify vulnerabilities, and develop robust countermeasures.

The 3DGS reconstruction pipeline can be broadly segmented into three primary stages: data ingestion, geometric and appearance optimization, and model deployment. At the data ingestion stage, the adversary can poison the training images by injecting malicious textures, altering camera poses, or embedding backdoor triggers into the visual content [710]. During the optimization stage, the attacker might interfere with the densification and pruning processes, manipulate the gradient signals, or implant malicious weights into the Gaussian parameters. Finally, at the deployment stage, the rendered output can be attacked through adversarial patches applied to the physical environment or through model-level perturbations that trigger undesired behaviors in downstream perception systems [711]. It is crucial to note that the multi-stage nature of this pipeline produces heterogeneous intermediate representations—including multi-view images, point clouds, training dynamics, and Gaussian statistics—each of which may carry distinct forensic signals that reveal the presence of an attack. Indeed, recent work has emphasized that the detectability of poisoning is inherently stage-dependent, as an attack introduced at one stage may produce observable signatures only at later stages of the pipeline [712].

Within this framework, we can define a taxonomy of adversarial capabilities based on the attacker's knowledge and access. A white-box attacker has full access to the model architecture, training data, and optimization pipeline, enabling them to execute highly sophisticated attacks such as gradient-guided backdoor injection or direct parameter manipulation [713]. Conversely, a black-box attacker operates with limited information, often relying on query access to the rendered outputs or the assumption of a specific model architecture [714]. This distinction is critical, as it shapes the feasibility and stealthiness of different attack vectors. For instance, white-box attacks can optimize for the joint objective of attack efficacy and perceptual realism, as demonstrated by the GaussTrap framework, which implants viewpoint-consistent poisoned renderings that yield high-quality output in non-target views while causing targeted scene confusion under specific conditions [710]. Gray-box settings, where the attacker possesses partial knowledge, are also relevant, particularly in scenarios involving model extraction or transferable attacks [715].

The attack landscape for 3DGS can be categorized into several distinct types. First, backdoor injection attacks aim to embed hidden behaviors into the trained 3DGS model, causing it to render malicious content or misrepresent the scene when a specific trigger is present. These attacks often involve poisoning a small subset of training data with carefully crafted triggers that remain imperceptible to human inspection [710]. The triggers can be spatial (e.g., a specific pattern in the scene), spectral, or even semantic in nature, exploiting the model's ability to learn and activate on distributed representations [716]. Second, computation cost attacks, or denial-of-service (DoS) attacks, seek to degrade the system's performance by injecting samples that cause excessive densification or expensive gradient computations, leading to resource exhaustion and rendering delays. These attacks exploit the adaptive nature of the 3DGS optimization process, where a single malicious image can trigger the creation of an extremely large number of Gaussian primitives [717]. Third, adversarial patch poisoning involves the placement of physical or digital patches that deceive the neural components of the 3DGS pipeline, such as feed-forward reconstruction networks, into producing incorrect geometric or appearance representations [711]. This can compromise the entire scene reconstruction, leading to environmental misperception in downstream applications.

A significant challenge in defending 3DGS systems is the stage-dependent nature of detectability. Forensic signals that are indicative of an attack may be highly visible at one stage but completely absent at another. For instance, an illusory object injection attack might be challenging to detect from the final rendered images if the renderer maintains high fidelity, but the training dynamics—such as a sudden spike in the gradient norm or an unusual distribution of Gaussian opacities—might reveal the malicious intervention [712]. Similarly, computation cost attacks might leave minimal visual trace in the output but are likely to produce anomalous Gaussian parameter statistics, such as an unusually high primitive count in a specific region [717]. This underscores the need for multi-stage detection pipelines that monitor not only the final output but also the intermediate representations and training behaviors. Furthermore, the effectiveness of detection is not uniform across attack types; certain attacks, such as adversarial weight perturbations, may be better detected by analyzing the Gaussian covariance matrices, while others, like data poisoning, may be more apparent in the input image frequencies or the multi-view consistency of the reconstructed geometry [712].

Defense strategies for 3DGS systems must therefore adopt a defense-in-depth approach. At the input stage, detection mechanisms can filter adversarial patches and calibration errors [717]. During optimization, robust training techniques, such as adversarial training or gradient regularization, can reduce the impact of poisoned samples [718]. At the model level, post-hoc purification methods can be applied to remove backdoor triggers or to unlearn malicious associations [719; 720]. Importantly, these defensive measures must be understood in relation to the broader ecosystem of 3DGS asset protection. While the threat model defined here addresses attacks that corrupt the *integrity* and *availability* of a 3DGS model during its lifecycle, the *confidentiality* and *ownership* of the asset itself are defended by the active and passive copyright protection mechanisms discussed in the next subsection. Notably, the principles of threat modeling and the cat-and-mouse dynamic between attackers and defenders are mirrored in the copyright domain, where watermarking schemes are pitted against increasingly sophisticated purification and removal attacks. Notably, the concept of proactive defense—where the defender actively engages with the training process to amplify the forensic signals of an attack—offers a promising direction, as exemplified by prototype frameworks in other domains that promote "proactive" poison detection [721]. In the specific context of 3DGS, this could involve monitoring the distribution of Gaussian scales, the entropy of spherical harmonic coefficients, or the temporal consistency of rendered views to expose subtle anomalies introduced by an adversary [712].

In summary, a comprehensive threat model for 3DGS systems must account for the diverse attack surfaces introduced by its multi-stage pipeline, the variety of adversarial capabilities, and the heterogeneous nature of stage-dependent forensic signals. By establishing this taxonomy, we can better guide the development of robust and reliable 3DGS systems that are resilient to both current and emerging security threats. This foundation of security analysis, which focuses on the integrity of the reconstruction process, naturally leads to the complementary question of protecting the *final 3DGS asset* itself, a topic that encompasses the copyright protection mechanisms explored in the following subsection. Future research must focus on standardized benchmarks that enable the systematic evaluation of detection and defense methods under unified protocols, ensuring that progress in this critical area is both measurable and reproducible [712; 717].

### 11.2 Active and Passive Copyright Protection for 3DGS Assets

### 11.2 Active and Passive Copyright Protection for 3DGS Assets

The explosive growth of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) as a de facto standard for real-time, high-fidelity 3D scene representation has created a pressing need for robust intellectual property (IP) protection. While the preceding subsection established a threat model focused on the *integrity* and *availability* of 3DGS models—addressing adversarial attacks that corrupt the reconstruction process—a complementary security concern arises once a legitimate 3DGS asset has been created and deployed: the protection of its *confidentiality* and *ownership*. Unlike implicit representations like NeRF, the explicit and editable nature of Gaussian primitives—each with its own position, covariance, opacity, and appearance parameters—makes unauthorized copying, redistribution, and malicious manipulation of 3D assets significantly easier.
