Addressing this dual threat requires a two-pronged approach: passive protection mechanisms, primarily in the form of watermarking for ownership tracing, and active protection strategies that deter unauthorized editing. This subsection surveys the landscape of these protection mechanisms, analyzes their underlying principles, and discusses the attacks that threaten their efficacy, thereby establishing a direct bridge to the quality assessment challenges discussed in the following subsection, where the perceptual impact of protective measures must be carefully balanced against their security benefits.

**Passive Protection: Watermarking for Ownership Tracing**

Passive copyright protection for 3DGS is predominantly achieved through digital watermarking, which embeds imperceptible ownership information (e.g., a bitstream or a logo) into the model. Once embedded, this information can be reliably extracted to assert ownership if the asset is misused. Early efforts in this domain adapted existing concepts to the unique structure of 3DGS. For instance, [722] introduced an uncertainty-based method that constrains the perturbation of Gaussian parameters to embed watermarks invisibly, ensuring that message decoding is reliable from both the 3D model and rendered 2D images, even after various distortions. Similarly, [723] proposed a universal watermarking strategy that utilizes a pre-trained watermark decoder and treats the 3DGS generation module as an encoder, incorporating novel 3D distortion layers to bolster robustness against real-world point cloud transformations.

A significant challenge in 3DGS watermarking is the efficiency of embedding messages. The generalizability and message capacity of watermarking schemes have been key areas of innovation. For instance, [724] presents a generalizable framework that enables protection of Splatter Image-based 3DGS models through a single forward pass, overcoming the need for expensive per-message fine-tuning. To further enhance capacity, [725] introduces a bit-compression framework that encodes multiple message bits per token, breaking the traditional limits of bit-to-token methods and achieving high message recovery accuracy for larger payloads like 128-bit messages. To improve the invisibility and robustness trade-off, [726] presents a representation-native framework that separates carrier selection from quality preservation, using a Trio-Experts module and a Safety and Budget Aware Gate to allocate Gaussians effectively and achieve both high watermark persistence and explainability.

A further dimension of passive protection is steganography, which aims to hide the very existence of the secret communication. In the 3DGS context, [727] proposes an end-to-end, key-secured 3D steganography framework that jointly optimizes the Gaussian model and a key-secured decoder. This framework highlights that Gaussian features contribute unequally to secret hiding and allows for multi-secret hiding while preventing unauthorized access, with a novel 3D-Sinkhorn distance introduced to quantify steganographic imperceptibility.

**Active Protection: Edit Deterrence and Unified Frameworks**

Passive watermarking, however, does little to prevent an adversary from maliciously editing a 3DGS asset. This has led to the development of active protection mechanisms. One approach involves "lifting" adversarial perturbations from 2D to 3D. For instance, [728] proposes the first editing safeguard for 3DGS, which prevents instruction-driven editing across arbitrary views by lifting strictly bounded 2D adversarial perturbations into the 3D Gaussian representation, effectively disrupting diffusion-based editing pipelines.

Recognizing that copyright protection requires both tracing and deterrence, recent research has moved toward unified frameworks. [729] presents the first unified protection framework that jointly optimizes a scene-wide watermarking objective with an adversarial objective for edit deterrence. This framework not only embeds a recoverable watermark but also uses adversarial techniques to divert the editing trajectory, preventing unauthorized modifications while preserving rendering fidelity. This approach effectively combines the strengths of passive and active protection, achieving a balance between bit accuracy, edit deterrence, and rendering quality, as demonstrated on Mip-NeRF 360 and Instruct-NeRF2NeRF scenes. This represents a significant step forward, suggesting that practical IP protection for 3DGS assets is best served by integrating ownership tracing and unauthorized editing deterrence into a single, coherent optimization process, rather than treating them as separate problems.

**Threat Model and Attacks: Watermark Purification and Removal**

Despite the advances in watermarking, the security of 3DGS watermarking systems has come under scrutiny. A critical question is whether these watermarks can withstand dedicated removal attacks, a concept analogous to adversarial attacks in image processing. [730] systematically explores the vulnerabilities of current 3DGS watermarking frameworks. It demonstrates that conventional 2D watermark removal techniques do not generalize effectively to the 3DGS scenario due to the specialized rendering pipeline and unique attributes of each Gaussian primitive. This insight led to the proposal of GSPure, the first watermark purification framework specifically for 3DGS. GSPure exploits view-dependent rendering contributions and geometrically accurate feature clustering to isolate and remove watermark-related Gaussian primitives while preserving scene integrity, reducing watermark PSNR by up to 16.34 dB with minimal degradation to original scene fidelity. This is a potent example of a purification attack, highlighting a serious vulnerability in current passive protection schemes.

Furthermore, the broader field of watermark security highlights that attacks can be universal and black-box. For instance, [731] introduces the Group-based Multi-objective Evolutionary Attack (GMEA), a first-of-its-kind universal black-box attack designed to challenge both 1D and 2D watermarks embedded within 3DGS. GMEA formulates the attack as a large-scale multi-objective optimization problem, balancing watermark removal with visual quality, thus demonstrating that effective watermark removal can be achieved without any internal knowledge of the watermarking scheme. The existence of such powerful attacks underscores the need for more robust and threat-aware watermarking solutions. The interconnectedness of these issues is also emphasized by the broader research agenda presented in [732], which argues that effective progress requires articulated security objectives and realistic threat models. This position paper advocates for scenario-driven formulations that clearly define adversarial capabilities and evaluate methods under explicit security assumptions, a principle that is critically important for advancing the robustness and practical utility of 3DGS copyright protection.

The cat-and-mouse dynamic between protection and attack mechanisms described here directly parallels the threat modeling discussion in the preceding subsection, where the detectability of security violations was shown to be stage-dependent. Similarly, the effectiveness of a watermarking scheme must be evaluated not only by its embedding capacity and robustness but also by its impact on perceptual quality—a consideration that connects naturally to the quality assessment methodologies reviewed in the following subsection. Indeed, the watermarking schemes discussed above often introduce subtle perturbations to Gaussian parameters, and the assessment of whether these perturbations remain perceptually invisible is, at its core, a quality assessment problem. The development of 3DGS-specific quality metrics, as surveyed in the next subsection, will consequently play a pivotal role in enabling the fair and robust evaluation of future copyright protection mechanisms, ensuring that security measures do not come at the cost of user-perceived fidelity. In summary, the field is moving from simple embedding techniques toward a sophisticated ecosystem where advanced unified protection frameworks contend with increasingly powerful purification and removal attacks, all while being constrained by the perceptual quality requirements that govern the deployment of 3DGS technology in practical applications.

### 11.3 Quality Assessment, Benchmarks, and Evaluation Protocols

The rapid proliferation of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) as a dominant representation for real-time novel view synthesis has created a critical demand for robust and standardized quality assessment methodologies. Unlike traditional image or video quality assessment, the evaluation of 3DGS-rendered content is complicated by a unique set of factors, including the interactive nature of view synthesis, the explicit but complex primitive-based representation, and the wide variety of distortion types introduced by optimization, compression, and rendering processes. This subsection reviews the current landscape of quality assessment for 3DGS, spanning subjective databases, objective prediction models, and the evaluation protocols that govern their comparability, while also recognizing the growing intersection between quality evaluation and the security mechanisms discussed in the preceding subsections.

A foundational challenge in 3DGS quality assessment is the lack of large-scale, purpose-built subjective datasets. Early efforts in the broader field of 3D graphics quality assessment, such as studies on textured meshes, established methodologies using paired-comparison protocols and crowdsourced subjective experiments to capture perceptual quality under geometry and texture distortions [733; 734]. Building upon these principles, the community has recently introduced several datasets specifically tailored to 3DGS. For instance, 3DGS-QA was introduced as the first subjective quality assessment dataset for 3DGS, comprising 225 degraded reconstructions across 15 object types to systematically study the perceptual impact of factors like viewpoint sparsity, limited training iterations, point downsampling, and noise [735]. Similarly, to address the specific distortions introduced by compression, 3DGS-IEval-15K provides a large-scale dataset with 15,200 images rendered from 10 real-world scenes using 6 representative compressed 3DGS algorithms, evaluated by 60 viewers [736]. Extending this to video sequences, 3DGS-VBench offers a video quality assessment benchmark with 660 compressed 3DGS models and corresponding videos from 11 scenes, while GScomp-QA focuses on isolating compression-induced distortions from synthesis artifacts across 9 state-of-the-art compression solutions [737; 738]. These datasets collectively provide a foundation for training and validating quality metrics specifically for 3DGS content.

Given the explicit nature of 3DGS, a notable trend in objective quality assessment is the development of no-reference (NR) models that operate directly on Gaussian primitives rather than rendered images. This approach is motivated by the inefficiency of rendering multiple views solely for quality evaluation. For example, the quality prediction model accompanying the 3DGS-QA dataset directly extracts spatial and photometric cues from the Gaussian representation to estimate perceived quality in a structure-aware manner, eliminating the need for image rendering [735]. Similarly, Aes3D introduces a lightweight model, Aes3DGSNet, that predicts scene-level aesthetic scores directly from 3DGS representations, capturing high-level aesthetic cues like composition and harmony without rendering multi-view images [739]. SpatialQ goes a step further by proposing a multimodal quality assessment framework that augments a VGGT-based encoder with a quality head and geometric cues to learn structure-aware quality representations, utilizing a Qwen-based MLLM for grounded multimodal reasoning [740]. These primitive-based and multimodal approaches signify a departure from traditional image-based metrics, aiming for more efficient and 3D-aware quality prediction.

Beyond 3DGS-specific methods, the broader field of 3D-generated content (3DGC) quality assessment offers relevant insights and benchmarks. Datasets like 3DGCQA, built using 7 representative Text-to-3D generation methods, highlight common distortion categories in generated assets and expose the limitations of existing assessment algorithms [741]. Hi3DEval proposes a hierarchical evaluation framework that combines object-level and part-level assessments, explicitly evaluating material realism attributes such as albedo and metallicness, using a large-scale dataset and a 3D-aware automated scoring system [742]. Furthermore, platforms like 3D Arena collect large-scale human preferences through pairwise comparisons for generative 3D models, revealing that presentation formats (e.g., Gaussian splat vs. mesh) and texturing significantly influence human preferences [743]. These works underscore the importance of perceptual and utility-based evaluation in addition to pixel-level fidelity. The lessons learned from point cloud quality assessment, where no-reference metrics often rely on projected or extracted features [744; 745], also inform the design of 3DGS quality models, though the continuous and semi-transparent nature of Gaussians requires novel adaptations.

A significant portion of quality assessment research for 3DGS focuses on evaluation protocols, particularly for compression and rendering degradations. The evaluation of compressed 3DGS models typically involves perceptual rate-distortion analysis, where the trade-off between storage size and visual quality is a primary concern [738; 737]. However, studies have shown that many classical objective quality metrics, such as those evaluated on the GScomp-QA dataset, do not fully capture GS-specific distortions [738]. This necessitates the development of specialized metrics or the adaptation of existing ones. For instance, a study on high-fidelity compressed images emphasizes the need for metrics sensitive to just-noticeable differences (JND), a concept that is also highly relevant when assessing high-quality 3DGS renderings [746]. Moreover, the impact of benchmark design itself cannot be overstated; as observed in some studies, the choice of dataset splits can significantly affect the comparability of results across different methods [747]. Therefore, standardized evaluation protocols—such as those provided by benchmark toolkits like Splatwizard, which automate the calculation of image quality metrics, chamfer distances, frame rates, and resource consumption—are essential for fair and reproducible comparisons [748].

Finally, the assessment of 3DGS quality also intersects with the security and robustness concerns detailed in the preceding subsections. While the primary focus of quality assessment is on perceptual fidelity, the integrity of the models themselves is increasingly important. The threat of adversarial attacks and backdoor poisoning can severely degrade the visual quality of a 3DGS model, and watermarking techniques for copyright protection often introduce imperceptible perturbations that should not adversely affect perceived quality. Consequently, future quality assessment methodologies must consider not only native rendering quality but also the resilience of models to malicious modifications and the fidelity preservation under protective measures. This dual concern mirrors the tension between protection and usability discussed in the context of active and passive copyright protection, where the balance between watermark robustness and rendering fidelity is a central design challenge. In summary, while significant strides have been made in establishing subjective datasets and objective prediction models for 3DGS, the field still lacks a unified, standardized framework that addresses the unique properties of this representation. Future work should focus on developing more comprehensive benchmarks that encompass diverse distortion types, evaluation protocols that are agnostic to dataset splits, and metrics that are sensitive to both perceptual quality and the subtle artifacts introduced by compression, security mechanisms, and real-world deployment conditions. Such efforts will lay the groundwork for the empirical security evaluations discussed in the following subsection, ensuring that 3DGS technology can be deployed with confidence in both quality and safety.

### 11.4 Adversarial Robustness, Poisoning Defenses, and Security Benchmarks

The empirical evaluation of security and robustness in 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) systems is a nascent but critical area of study that naturally extends the quality assessment landscape discussed in the preceding subsection. While quality assessment focuses on perceptual fidelity under benign distortions, a parallel and equally pressing concern is the integrity of 3DGS models under adversarial manipulation. The explicit, primitive-based nature of 3DGS introduces a unique attack surface that is distinct from both traditional deep learning classifiers and implicit neural representations like NeRF. This subsection synthesizes findings from the broader adversarial machine learning literature to establish a framework for understanding adversarial robustness, poisoning defenses, and security benchmarks applicable to 3DGS pipelines. The core challenge lies in adapting established concepts—such as data poisoning, backdoor injection, and adversarial training—to a domain where the "model" is a collection of optimizable geometric primitives and the "inference" is a differentiable rasterization process. Just as quality benchmarks like 3DGS-QA and GScomp-QA provide standardized evaluations of rendering quality, the security community must develop analogous protocols to quantify attack success and defense robustness, a theme that will be further elaborated in the following subsection.

A foundational step for securing 3DGS is adapting the taxonomy of attacks and defenses from the wider field. A key concern is the threat of data poisoning and backdoor injection, where an adversary manipulates the training images used to optimize the Gaussians—a vector that directly parallels the distortion types examined in quality assessment, but with malicious intent. The literature on backdoor attacks provides a comprehensive overview of the attack surfaces across the machine learning pipeline, which can be mapped directly to 3DGS. These surfaces include data collection, where poisoned images are introduced, and outsourcing, where a pre-optimized 3DGS model might be distributed [749]. The goal of a backdoor attack on a 3DGS scene could be to cause specific artifacts to appear in the rendered output when a trigger is present in the input view, or to subtly corrupt the geometric reconstruction in a way that causes downstream failures in navigation or manipulation—distortions that would be invisible to conventional quality metrics yet catastrophic in practice. The stealthy nature of these attacks, where the model performs nominally on benign inputs but maliciously on triggered ones, is a primary threat [749].

To defend against such threats, several strategies from the classical domain can be re-contextualized for 3DGS, mirroring the way quality assessment methods have been adapted from traditional 3D graphics. Poisoned sample detection (PSD) is a first line of defense, aiming to filter out malicious training images before they influence Gaussian optimization, much as quality metrics filter out distorted renders. The effectiveness of these detection methods has been shown to be correlated with the strength of the backdoor effect; methods like Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM) can be used to train a separate classifier to amplify the backdoor effect, thereby making poisoned samples easier to detect [750]. This suggests that a pre-processing step to identify and remove poisoned frames from a training set for 3DGS is feasible. Another promising direction is the use of machine unlearning techniques. Instead of retraining from scratch on a cleaned dataset, one could train a 3DGS model and then "unlearn" the influence of suspected poisoned samples. This is analogous to methods for purifying backdoored models using a small clean dataset, which derive an upper bound on backdoor risk and use adversarial training to unlearn the malicious behavior [751]. However, applying unlearning to a continuous, non-parametric representation like 3DGS is non-trivial, as the influence of a single training image is distributed across many Gaussian primitives. This remains an open challenge.

Beyond data-centric defenses, the optimization process itself can be hardened. Backdoor attacks often rely on the model learning a "shortcut" that connects the trigger to the target behavior. Research has shown that model training can be made more robust by decoupling the learning process into distinct stages, such as supervised learning, active unlearning, and semi-supervised fine-tuning, to actively prevent the model from fitting the poisoned samples [752]. Translating these training strategies to the 3DGS optimization loop—particularly the adaptive density control and densification phases—could help mitigate the risk of the model structurally encoding an embedded backdoor. Furthermore, a critical lesson from the broader field is the fragility of defenses under adaptive attacks. A defense that is effective against a naive attack can often be bypassed by an adversary who is aware of the defense mechanism [753]. For instance, defenses that rely on the assumption that poisoned and clean samples are separable in the latent space of a classifier can be broken by adaptive attacks that deliberately make the latent representations of poisoned samples more diverse and similar to clean ones. In 3DGS, this means that any defense mechanism must be evaluated against adversaries who can specifically target its assumptions.

The evaluation of these security properties in 3DGS must also be rigorous and standardized, echoing the call for unified benchmarks in quality assessment. The existing body of work on backdoor learning has highlighted the dangers of inconsistent evaluation setups and flawed threat models, leading to potentially overstated effectiveness of both attacks and defenses [754; 755]. A major concern is the "quantization blindspot," where backdoor defenses that work perfectly on full-precision models completely fail when the model is quantized for deployment [756]. This is highly relevant to 3DGS, where model compression and quantization are essential for real-time performance on mobile and edge devices—the same compression scenarios analyzed in quality databases like 3DGS-IEval-15K and GScomp-QA, but now viewed through a security lens. Security evaluations must therefore be conducted at the same precision and with the same rendering pipeline as the final deployment target. To address this, the community should develop comprehensive benchmarks for 3DGS that include standardized protocols for evaluating both attack success and defense robustness, similar to how quality benchmarks standardize perceptual fidelity assessments. Such benchmarks would need to define clear threat models (e.g., white-box, black-box), specify the attack's goals (e.g., inducing rendering artifacts, degrading geometric accuracy), and provide metrics for both security (e.g., attack success rate, defense detection rate) and utility (e.g., PSNR, SSIM of clean renders) [757]. These benchmarks are crucial for facilitating fair comparisons and reproducible research, avoiding the pitfalls of evaluating defenses in isolation without considering the computational overhead and their behavior under benign conditions [758].

Finally, the broader robustness of 3DGS to adversarial perturbations should be considered, just as quality assessment considers a spectrum of distortion types. Unlike a classifier, where an adversarial example is a single input image, an adversarial example for a 3DGS system could be a crafted input view that causes a catastrophic failure in the rendering process or leads to a significant error in a downstream perception task. The concept of "adversarial hypervolume," which assesses robustness across a range of perturbation intensities rather than a single one, is a useful model for evaluating 3DGS systems [759]. It moves beyond the brittle, single-point evaluation of robustness and acknowledges that attacks can vary in strength. Therefore, a comprehensive security evaluation of a 3DGS system must consider not only whether it can be poisoned or backdoored but also how gracefully it degrades under a spectrum of adversarial perturbations to its inputs, both during training and at inference time. This requires a shift towards threat-aware evaluation protocols that rigorously define attacker capabilities and system vulnerabilities, ensuring that 3DGS technology can be deployed with confidence in security-sensitive applications. Such protocols will complement the quality assessment frameworks discussed earlier and provide the empirical foundation for the theoretical security considerations to be explored in the following subsection.

## 12 Future Directions, Open Challenges, and Conclusion

### 12.1 Open Challenges and Current Limitations of 3DGS

Despite the remarkable advances and transformative potential of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) across numerous domains, the field continues to grapple with several fundamental limitations that impede its widespread adoption and constrain its practical utility. These challenges, which have been recurring throughout this survey, span the core pillars of robustness, scalability, generalization, and efficiency, and represent critical barriers that must be addressed for 3DGS to mature into a foundational technology for real-time photorealistic 3D reconstruction. Understanding these limitations is essential, as they directly motivate the active research directions—hybrid representations, generative pipelines, and optimization refinements—that are explored in the subsequent discussion on future frontiers.

One of the most significant open challenges is the **robustness of 3DGS to extreme and challenging lighting conditions**. The standard 3DGS pipeline inherently assumes consistent exposure and tone mapping across all input views, an assumption that is routinely violated in real-world data. Heterogeneous camera pipelines, dynamic outdoor illumination, and diverse camera exposure settings can bake photometric inconsistencies directly into the radiance field, leading to visible artifacts and a breakdown in the fidelity of the reconstruction [298]. In particularly challenging scenarios, such as low-light environments, the acquired images are plagued by severe noise, low dynamic range, and unreliable Structure-from-Motion (SfM) initialization, which collectively destabilize the optimization process [760]. The difficulty is compounded by the fact that the spherical harmonic functions used for color representation are often ill-suited for these degraded linear color spaces, and the limited SfM points fail to capture sufficient scene detail, especially in distant regions [761]. While methods have been proposed to address these issues—through physical priors, per-view adjustments, or illumination-invariant representations—achieving robust and photorealistic rendering under such diverse and unconstrained illumination remains an active area of research [293; 762]. These efforts underscore the need for more physically grounded appearance models, a theme that resonates with the hybrid and regularization strategies discussed later.

Another critical limitation is the **scalability of 3DGS to unbounded and large-scale scenes**, particularly in terms of memory footprint and computational cost. The explicit, primitive-based nature of 3DGS, while advantageous for rendering speed, leads to significant storage demands and excessive GPU memory consumption, making it impractical for large-scale applications on mobile or head-mounted devices [451; 54]. As scenes grow to city-scale, the sheer number of Gaussian primitives required for high-fidelity reconstruction explodes, leading to prohibitively long training times and intractable memory requirements [113; 698]. Furthermore, attempting to render city-scale 3DGS scenes in real-time on resource-constrained mobile devices poses significant challenges due to computationally intensive stages such as level-of-detail search and rasterization [763]. While divide-and-conquer strategies, such as block-based or hierarchical partitioning, have been proposed to mitigate these issues, they often introduce new bottlenecks like severe load imbalance, inefficient use of the coarse stage, or degradation in rendering quality, particularly in areas with high visual complexity and at high resolutions [113; 73]. The omnipresent visibility of panoramic images further invalidates existing partitioning strategies that rely on local camera frustums, causing block-wise optimization to degenerate into global training [109]. This scalability bottleneck is precisely what motivates the exploration of more compact and adaptive representations, including the hybrid mesh-Gaussian and level-of-detail approaches highlighted in the future directions.

The **generalization of 3DGS across unseen scenes and domains** represents a third major challenge. The standard 3DGS pipeline is inherently per-scene, requiring expensive optimization that does not generalize to novel environments. Feed-forward methods that attempt to infer Gaussian parameters in a single pass face fundamental bottlenecks, such as being memory-prohibitive at high resolutions and struggling to fuse dense multi-view observations consistently [236]. The robustness of these models on unseen scenes is also limited by the "sparsity shield" of current benchmarks, which rewards hallucination over physical fidelity and fails to capture the complexities of real-world data [764]. Moreover, the performance of 3DGS models often degrades sharply when deployed in real-world scenarios due to unpredictable noise, dynamic motion, and sensor imperfections—conditions that are largely absent from sanitized training data [765]. Even in the domain of semantic understanding, generalization remains a hurdle, as many Language Gaussian Splatting methods are evaluated only on rendered 2D views close to training viewpoints, limiting insights into holistic 3D understanding [766]. This limitation directly motivates the emerging shift toward generative and feed-forward pipelines, which aim to replace per-scene optimization with learned priors that can generalize across scenes, as elaborated in the following subsection.

Finally, the **real-time performance constraints on mobile and resource-constrained platforms** continue to be a significant open problem. While 3DGS enables real-time rendering on high-end workstations, its training and rendering demands often exceed the capabilities of mobile devices and embedded systems. Training on-device requires negotiating a fundamental tension between training efficiency, memory compactness, and modeling quality, and often remains impractical under minute-scale time budgets and limited peak memory [115]. Even for rendering, the computational cost of rasterization can be prohibitive on low-power GPUs, necessitating software and hardware co-design, aggressive compression, level-of-detail systems, or remote streaming solutions to maintain interactive frame rates [114; 415; 483]. The need for predictable resource consumption is paramount for production-level deployment, yet heuristic Gaussian growth processes often lead to unpredictable memory and computational needs [764]. Addressing these constraints requires not only algorithmic innovations—such as more efficient densification strategies and compact representations—but also system-level co-design, a theme that carries forward into the optimization and robustness research discussed in the next section.

In summary, while 3DGS has achieved remarkable success, the field is confronted with significant open challenges related to lighting robustness, scalability, generalization, and resource efficiency. These limitations are not isolated; they are deeply interconnected and collectively define the research agenda for the field. They highlight the need for continued innovation in physical modeling, compact and adaptive representations, and efficient system-level design—precisely the directions that the emerging work on hybrid representations, feed-forward generative models, and advanced optimization strategies aims to address, as detailed in the remainder of this survey.

### 12.2 Emerging Research Directions in Representation and Optimization

The field of 3D Gaussian Splatting is rapidly evolving, and several promising research directions are emerging to address its current limitations and unlock its full potential. As highlighted in the preceding discussion, the core challenges of lighting robustness, scalability, generalization, and resource efficiency are deeply interconnected, and the avenues explored below—spanning hybrid models, generative and feed-forward pipelines, and optimization refinements—directly target these bottlenecks. Together, they lay the algorithmic groundwork that will later be integrated into the unified, scalable, and deployable systems discussed in the following subsection.

**Hybrid Representations and Unified Frameworks.** A significant trend that directly addresses the scalability and geometric fidelity limitations is the departure from pure Gaussian-only models towards hybrid representations that combine the strengths of 3DGS with other complementary data structures. The integration of explicit meshes is a key focus, aiming to provide structural fidelity and geometric integrity that pure point-based methods often lack, thereby reducing the number of Gaussians needed for large-scale scenes. For instance, an end-to-end mesh-Gaussian representation binds 3D Gaussians to mesh faces, enabling a direct information pathway for jointly learning explicit geometry and renderable appearance [90]. Similarly, hybrid approaches that combine 3DGS with textured meshes have been proposed to handle texture-rich flat areas more efficiently, reserving Gaussians for intricate geometries [47]. This strategy of leveraging a mesh scaffold for large-scale structure and Gaussians for fine details is also echoed in other works [93; 226]. Furthermore, integrating implicit fields, such as Signed Distance Functions (SDFs), is a powerful direction to enforce global surface continuity and improve mesh extraction, as demonstrated by models that squeeze geometry regularization into a compact MLP within a unified framework [209]. This move towards unified, single-model frameworks that jointly optimize for both high-quality rendering and accurate surface reconstruction is a critical area of exploration [209]. The success of these hybrids suggests that the future of 3D scene representation lies not in a single primitive type, but in the synergistic combination of multiple, where each component handles the tasks it is best suited for—a principle that directly informs the system-level design discussions on compact and adaptive representations later in this survey.

**Generative Modeling and Feed-Forward Reconstruction.** Another major frontier that directly confronts the generalization limitation is the shift from per-scene optimization to generalizable, feed-forward reconstruction, which aims to infer a 3D representation from a single forward pass. This paradigm is crucial for scalability and real-time applications, as it replaces the expensive per-scene fitting with learned priors. Current research is actively rethinking the core prediction framework, moving away from simple pixel-aligned predictions to more robust voxel-aligned or geometry-guided formulations to improve multi-view consistency and handle occlusions [767; 768]. The use of powerful transformer architectures as the backbone for these feed-forward models is becoming increasingly common, enabling the prediction of semantically rich and geometrically coherent primitives [769; 672]. A key challenge being addressed is the development of efficient and stable training paradigms for these feed-forward models. For iterative refinement, methods like those using self-supervised densification policies and geometric regularization are being developed to improve the stability of iterative feed-forward pipelines [236]. Another promising area is the use of episodic meta-training to learn transferable priors, allowing the model to reconstruct scenes in a single forward pass by predicting geometry and radiance together [700]. Furthermore, the integration of diffusion models into the reconstruction process is a powerful tool to enhance the quality of predicted primitives or fill in unobserved regions, with some works using that as a robust refinement mechanism [770; 771]. The development of these feed-forward and generative methods is crucial for moving 3DGS from an offline tool to an on-the-fly, interactive system [772], and it provides the scalable, generalizable foundation upon which the streaming and edge-cloud architectures of the next subsection will depend.

**Optimization, Densification, and Robustness.** Key to the success and efficiency of 3DGS is its optimization strategy, and research here is focused on making training faster, more stable, and less memory-intensive—directly tackling the efficiency and robustness challenges outlined earlier. Improving the efficiency of 3DGS is a critical goal, with innovations in the core prediction and optimization pipeline. For instance, sparse query-based models are being explored to reduce memory usage and inference time by allocating capacity only where needed [773]. The "densification" step, where new Gaussian primitives are added, is a critical point of failure that is being addressed through various new strategies, such as Hessian-assisted sampling to guide where new primitives are generated for super-resolution [774], or explicit identification of missing regions through visibility-aware analysis to recover geometry [775]. Some methods are looking to Gaussian processes for principled densification [776]. Stability remains a concern, especially in challenging settings like the low-light and sparse-view scenarios previously discussed, with techniques like alternating densification phases reducing artifacts in sparse-input scenarios [179]. Finally, the issue of geometric fidelity is being tackled by denoising the optimization process through depth and density-guided strategies [264], or by using energy-based models for soft geometric guidance when priors are incomplete [194]. These refinements are essential for making 3DGS reliable and efficient enough for widespread adoption, providing the predictable resource consumption and robust performance that the hardware-software co-design and federated learning frameworks in the following subsection will require.

### 12.3 Toward Unified, Scalable, and Deployable Systems

**12.3 Toward Unified, Scalable, and Deployable Systems**

Building upon the optimization strategies and generative advancements discussed in the preceding section, the trajectory of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) from a high-fidelity novel view synthesis technique to a foundational technology for interactive applications hinges on its ability to operate within unified, scalable, and deployable system architectures. While core algorithmic advances have propelled rendering quality and speed, the practical integration of 3DGS into real-world products—spanning mobile devices, autonomous vehicles, and immersive XR experiences—demands a holistic rethinking of the entire pipeline, from data acquisition and semantic understanding to model compression, transmission, and hardware co-design.

**Unified Multimodal Representations and Semantic Integration.** A key pillar of deployable systems is the seamless fusion of 3D geometry with high-level semantic, linguistic, and multi-sensor information. As discussed in Section 8, embedding semantic features into Gaussian primitives (e.g., via CLIP or SAM distillation) enables interactive scene understanding, open-vocabulary segmentation, and language-guided editing. However, unifying these capabilities within a single, coherent 3DGS framework requires addressing cross-modal representation gaps. The "semantic gap" between low-level photometric radiance and high-level concepts necessitates architectures that can jointly optimize for geometric fidelity and semantic consistency. Drawing inspiration from multimodal foundation models that process text, images, and audio in a shared token space [777], future 3DGS systems could adopt a unified tokenization strategy where Gaussian primitives, semantic features, and textual queries are embedded in a common representational space. This would enable more direct interaction with large language models for scene reasoning and task-oriented communication [778], rather than relying on post-hoc feature aggregation layers.

Moreover, scaling 3DGS to support diverse sensor modalities, including LiDAR, radar, and depth sensors, is critical for applications like autonomous driving and robotic navigation. Sensor data provides crucial geometric priors that can regularize Gaussian optimization and improve metric accuracy in textureless or reflective regions (Section 5.4). A unified system would need to perform online multi-modal fusion, aligning and integrating these heterogeneous data streams in real-time to construct a coherent scene representation. The principle of semantic communication, where raw sensor data is reduced to goal-oriented messages rather than raw bitstreams, is particularly relevant here. For instance, in collaborative robotics, transmitting only task-relevant semantic features—such as object locations and identities extracted from 3DGS maps—rather than full image streams, can significantly reduce bandwidth requirements and enable more efficient edge-cloud collaboration [779].

**System-Level Compression, Streaming, and Scalability.** A fundamental obstacle to ubiquitous 3DGS deployment is its substantial memory footprint (Section 7). While intra-model compression techniques like vector quantization and codebook-based methods offer high compression ratios, a scalable, deployable system must integrate these with standardized streaming protocols. This requires moving beyond per-scene optimizations toward rate-distortion-optimized storage and transmission that explicitly account for human visual perception and semantic importance. For networked and remote rendering scenarios, such as XR, the system architecture must be designed to deliver a satisfying Quality of Experience (QoE) under varying network conditions. The industry standardization efforts by MPEG to manage media enablers and hardware resources underscore the necessity for standardized frameworks to lower the barrier for complex XR experiences [780] and to reconcile the game-engine and multimedia streaming paradigms [781]. A key challenge is the dynamic allocation of computation between the edge and the cloud. Existing edge-cloud collaborative architectures often use fixed-size representations [782], which are not adaptive to fluctuating bandwidth. Future 3DGS streaming systems should therefore employ progressive or scalable encodings, allowing the server to transmit a coarse base representation followed by refinements, as network conditions permit. This approach can be informed by adaptive streaming strategies that adjust both resolution and frame rate based on scene motion and bandwidth constraints to optimize perceptual quality [488].

Furthermore, scalability to city-scale and unbounded scenes requires out-of-core and distributed training and rendering frameworks that can manage billions of primitives (Section 7.3). Beyond raw computation, this demands a hierarchical memory system capable of paging Gaussian data from storage to GPU memory on demand, similar to virtual texture mapping in traditional graphics. To achieve this, the Gaussian representation must be rebuilt around Level-of-Detail (LOD) hierarchies, where coarse representations at distance are replaced by finer ones as the user approaches. This LOD structure is also crucial for efficient streaming in multi-user environments, as it naturally supports progressive data download and discard.

**Hardware-Software Co-Design for Real-Time Edge Deployment.** The ultimate goal of deployable 3DGS systems is real-time, photorealistic interaction on resource-constrained devices like smartphones and embedded platforms. This cannot be achieved by algorithmic efficiency alone; it requires a tight coupling between algorithm design and the underlying hardware architecture. The challenge is analogous to that faced by on-device large language models, where the computational and energy budgets far exceed the capabilities of mobile CPUs and GPUs, leading to the development of specialized acceleration strategies [783]. For 3DGS, the dense rasterization, sorting, and alpha-blending pipeline must be mapped onto hardware accelerators, such as NPUs and Tensor Cores. Research on binary networks for segmentation highlights the potential of extreme quantization combined with hardware-specific instructions to achieve significant speedups and energy savings [784]. Similarly, for 3DGS, implementing rasterization with low-precision arithmetic and custom data paths could unlock real-time performance on mobile GPUs.

The performance bottleneck is not limited to rendering; the training and optimization stages are also extremely computationally intensive. Therefore, on-device fine-tuning and adaptation are challenging but necessary for personalized experiences. Federated learning frameworks, which evaluate the costs of local fine-tuning on real smartphones and edge boards, provide a valuable blueprint for sustainable, privacy-preserving model updates [785]. A unified 3DGS platform must similarly support the ability to incrementally update and refine a scene model on-device, sending only model "deltas" back to a central server. This parallels the development of efficient federated learning methods for edge LLMs, which balance communication, memory, and energy footprints [785].

Moreover, the system software stack must be designed to maximize the utilization of heterogeneous hardware. As evidenced by VLM deployment studies [786], current frameworks often over-utilize the CPU while leaving NPUs and GPUs idle due to inefficient kernel implementations or memory transfer bottlenecks. Future 3DGS deployments must avoid such pitfalls through rigorous profiling and hardware-aware scheduling. This includes dynamic partitioning of tasks, where the edge handles primary visibility and simple lighting, while the cloud computes complex global illumination or updates dense semantic maps, as demonstrated by distributed rendering systems [787]. A successful architecture will be one that can intelligently schedule workloads across CPU, GPU, and NPU, balancing latency, energy, and bandwidth constraints. This necessitates a co-design process where the mathematical formulation of the Gaussian primitive, the rasterization algorithm, and the instruction set architecture are developed in tandem. Custom on-device training frameworks and federated learning offer a pathway to ensure that device-side personalization and continuous improvement are achievable without compromising the system's privacy and latency guarantees [785], making 3DGS a truly deployable ubiquitous technology. Together, these system-level considerations complement the algorithmic foundations established earlier and set the stage for the broader outlook on the field's future trajectory presented in the concluding section.

### 12.4 Future Outlook and Concluding Remarks

---


As we conclude this comprehensive survey, the trajectory of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) reveals a field that has evolved from a breakthrough representation into a versatile and foundational tool for 3D vision and graphics [5]. Building directly upon the system-level considerations of unified representations, scalable architectures, and hardware-software co-design outlined in the previous section, we now synthesize the broader implications of these advances and chart the future directions that will define the next phase of 3DGS research and deployment. The rapid progress since its introduction underscores a paradigm shift in how we approach photorealistic scene reconstruction, moving from computationally intensive implicit neural representations toward explicit, GPU-friendly primitives that balance visual fidelity, rendering speed, and editability [37]. The convergence of technical advances across representation design, optimization strategies, geometric fidelity, robustness, and domain-specific applications positions 3DGS not merely as an alternative to NeRF but as a transformative technology bridging neural rendering, computer graphics, and embodied intelligence [4].

Looking forward, the unified multimodal representations discussed in the previous section serve as a springboard for one of the most promising research directions: the deep integration of semantics, language, sensor data, and physical properties directly into the Gaussian-based scene model. The extension of 3DGS with semantic feature distillation, open-vocabulary segmentation, and language-embedded queries has already enabled richer scene understanding and interactive editing [8]. As large language models become increasingly integrated into 3D pipelines, systems such as LIVE-GS demonstrate how LLM-powered analysis of physical properties can transform static Gaussian assets into dynamic, interactive VR experiences with minimal manual effort [680]. Similarly, the fusion of 3DGS with scene graphs and hierarchical representations paves the way for high-level reasoning systems that enable embodied navigation and manipulation in complex environments [788]. The emergence of physics-aware generation and simulation frameworks further highlights how 3DGS can go beyond visual realism to incorporate physical plausibility, enabling applications in robotics and autonomous systems that require adherence to real-world laws [789].

The scalability and system-level integration challenges identified in the previous section—memory footprint, streaming protocols, and hardware constraints—remain critical open problems that will shape future adoption. Current methods for large-scale urban and aerial scene reconstruction, such as hierarchical block-based approaches and streamlined pipelines, have made significant strides in reducing computational overhead while maintaining quality [698; 790]. Yet the field still confronts fundamental trade-offs between memory footprint, training time, and rendering quality, particularly in unbounded environments and resource-constrained platforms [37]. Out-of-core training frameworks, distributed optimization, and hardware-software co-design strategies—including custom accelerators and compression-aware pipelines—will be essential to realize billion-scale reconstruction at interactive rates [702]. Compression techniques, including pruning, quantization, entropy coding, and factorized representations, have demonstrated that high compression ratios are achievable while preserving visual quality [791]. The development of standardized compression and streaming protocols, as advocated in our discussion of system-level deployment, will be crucial for enabling real-time collaborative experiences across devices, from mobile edge platforms to cloud-based rendering farms [5].

Another emerging frontier that directly extends the algorithmic foundations laid earlier in this survey is the convergence of generative modeling and feed-forward reconstruction with 3DGS. Traditional per-scene optimization approaches, while high-quality, are inherently limited by their computational cost and lack of generalization—a limitation that the scalable and unified systems discussed previously must overcome. Recent advances in feed-forward frameworks that directly regress Gaussian parameters from multi-view images have demonstrated remarkable speed improvements while maintaining competitive fidelity [792; 672]. The integration of diffusion models with 3DGS for both scene generation and refinement is opening new possibilities—from text-to-3D synthesis using video diffusion self-distillation [793] to multi-modal diffusion frameworks that synthesize RGB, depth, and semantic outputs for urban reconstruction [241]. These generative approaches have the potential to break the dependence on dense captured data, enabling reconstruction from sparse, degraded, or even entirely synthetic inputs, and they align with a broader shift in the field from visual realism toward interaction readiness in which generated content must support physical simulation, articulation, and task execution [794; 795].

Dynamic and 4D Gaussian representations represent another vital research axis with substantial applications in digital humans, autonomous driving simulation, and embodied AI, complementing the static scene understanding enabled by unified multimodal systems. Advances in canonical deformation fields, explicit 4D primitives, and motion decomposition strategies have expanded the expressiveness of 3DGS to temporally varying scenes [796]. Physics-based dynamic simulation through differentiable material point methods and 4DGS enables instance-level editing and realistic object-object interactions, supporting the generation of challenging corner cases for autonomous driving validation [797]. The fusion of 3DGS with SLAM systems continues to push the boundaries of online, real-time dense mapping in dynamic and large-scale environments, with methods such as global Sim(3) optimization and voxelized representations enabling robust camera tracking and efficient loop closure [798; 799]. These systems are foundational for robotics, augmented reality, and autonomous navigation, where the ability to reconstruct and update scenes in real time from monocular or multi-modal streams is paramount [800].

Despite the remarkable progress, several fundamental challenges persist that cut across the algorithmic and system-level themes of this survey. Robustness to extreme lighting, adverse weather, sparse views, and imperfect sensor inputs remains an active area of research, with methods leveraging physical priors, multi-modal fusion, and generative enhancement to close the gap between controlled conditions and in-the-wild settings [801; 802]. The simulation-to-real gap, particularly acute in autonomous driving, is being addressed through hybrid approaches that combine neural reconstruction with physics-based rendering, enabling interactive frame rates and multi-modal sensor simulation [803]. Benchmarks such as the Extrapolated Urban View Synthesis benchmark highlight the need for more robust NVS methods under large viewpoint deviations, emphasizing that current approaches often overfit to training views [804].

Security, privacy, and intellectual property protection for 3DGS assets are also emerging as critical considerations as Gaussian representations become commercially valuable, paralleling the deployment concerns raised in our discussion of scalable systems. The explicit parametric structure of 3DGS introduces unique vulnerabilities to adversarial attacks and backdoor poisoning, as well as novel opportunities for watermarking and ownership tracing [791]. Standardized threat modeling, robust defense mechanisms, and comprehensive quality assessment benchmarks will be necessary to establish trust in 3DGS systems across industrial deployments [5].

In summary, 3DGS has fundamentally transformed the landscape of real-time photorealistic 3D reconstruction and rendering. Its explicit, editable, and GPU-friendly nature positions it as a cornerstone technology for a broad spectrum of applications, ranging from virtual and augmented reality to robotics, autonomous driving, and digital twins. The future of 3DGS lies in continued advances toward unified multimodal representations, scalable and efficient systems, physics-aware interactivity, and robust generalization—building directly upon the system-level foundations established in the preceding section. As the field converges with generative AI, foundation models, and embodied intelligence, we anticipate that 3DGS will evolve from a scene representation technique into a comprehensive infrastructure for spatial intelligence—enabling machines not only to perceive and render the world but also to understand, reason, and interact within it. The journey from concept to capability is well underway, and the full impact of 3D Gaussian Splatting on interactive media, autonomous systems, and digital society is only beginning to unfold.
